Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Project Overview••
	• The Works of William Perkins are being re-published in a newly typeset format
	• The project aims to make Perkins' writings accessible to the public, including laymen, pastors, and scholars
• ••Publication Details••
	• The Works will be published in 10 volumes
	• The volumes will include biblical exposition, doctrinal and polemical treatises, and ethical and practical writings
• ••Typesetting and Editing••
	• The text has been modernized to conform to modern American standards
	• Spelling and capitalization have been updated, but original words and word order have been retained
	• Pronouns referring to God are capitalized
• ••Quotation and Citation Style••
	• Quotations are now marked with quotation marks, rather than italics
	• Scripture references in the margins have been brought into the text and enclosed in square brackets
• ••Notes and Marginalia••
	• Parenthetical Scripture references have been abbreviated and punctuated according to modern custom
	• Notes from the margins have been placed in footnotes and labeled as "In the margin"
• ••Volume Breakdown••
	• The project includes four volumes of biblical exposition, three volumes of doctrinal and polemical treatises, and three volumes of ethical and practical writings
• ••Theology and Center of Perkins' Thought••
	• The center of Perkins' theology is difficult to pinpoint, but he emphasized the importance of preaching one Christ by Christ to the praise of Christ
• ••Editorial Approach••
	• The editors have aimed to preserve the original words and word order of Perkins' writings
	• Some archaic terms and obscure references have been explained in footnotes
• ••Introduction and Orientation••
	• Each volume will include an introduction by its editor to orient the reader to its contents
• ••Historical Significance••
	• The publication of Perkins' Works is a significant event in the history of Reformed theology and scholarship

â€¢ ••The Gospel of Jesus Christ••
	• Is the fulfillment of ancient promises given to Abraham for the blessing of all nations
	• Is good news for both Jews and Gentiles
	• Is a message for sinners who need a Savior and a warning against those who would rest their hope in anything but Christ alone

â€¢ ••The Epistle to the Galatians••
	• Was used by Martin Luther as a powerful instrument to distinguish the righteousness of the law from the righteousness of the gospel
	• Was seen by John Calvin as a "mirror" to see clearly when Satan comes to confuse the gospel

â€¢ ••Reformed Orthodoxy••
	• Was a period of time following the Reformation where Reformed writers labored to teach the doctrines of the gospel with greater clarity
	• Was the context in which the commentary on Galatians was written

â€¢ ••Perkins' View of Holy Scripture••
	• Had a high view of the Bible as the only basis of the Christian faith
	• Believed that our faith must be bound by what God has revealed and not wander into speculation

â€¢ ••The Bible as the Written Word of God••
	• Perkins viewed the Bible as the written Word of God, without error in all it taught
	• Received the Scriptures as words of divine authority

â€¢ ••The Role of the Spirit in Scripture••
	• Some scholars have challenged Perkins' view of Scripture as the written words of God
	• Argue that the Spirit's work is necessary to convince the reader that the Bible is God's Word

â€¢ ••Perkins' Response to Criticism••
	• Argued that the Bible remains God's Word in an objective sense, regardless of how people perceive it
	• Said that the Spirit's work is necessary to convince the reader, but the Bible is still the absolute Word of God

â€¢ ••The Nature of Scripture••
	• Perkins taught that Scripture is the Word of God, not just a human document
	• Said that the writings of the apostles are the immediate and mere Word of God

â€¢ ••The Authority of Scripture••
	• Perkins believed that the Bible has divine authority and is without error
	• Said that the Word preached and written by Paul is as authoritative as the Word of God itself

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Commentary••
	• Was written to defend the gospel against the intrusions of man-made legalism
	• Serves as a wall of evangelical defense for the church in all ages

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Perkins' View of Scripture••
	• Perkins believed the Bible is the Word of God
	• He considered the prophets and apostles to be "pen men of the Holy Ghost"

â€¢ ••Inerrancy of Scripture••
	• Perkins believed the Bible is inerrant in all it teaches
	• He argued that the apostles were guided by the infallible assistance of the Spirit

â€¢ ••Reformers' View of Inerrancy••
	• Jack Rogers and Donald McKim argue that the Reformers did not hold to a view of inerrancy as scientifically inerrant words
	• Instead, they believed the Bible gave an authoritative message of salvation

â€¢ ••Perkins' Foundation for Inerrancy••
	• Rogers argues that Perkins laid a foundation for the approach to Scripture among English thinkers
	• This approach emphasized the Bible's authority in salvation, rather than literal inerrancy

â€¢ ••Perkins' Teaching on Inerrancy••
	• Perkins believed the apostles had authority given to them by God to teach and preach without error
	• He argued that the church must submit to Scripture, as the testimony of God is greater than human testimony

â€¢ ••The Bible as the Voice of God••
	• Perkins identified the Scriptures with the voice of the inerrant God
	• He believed the Bible is the voice of the inerrant Christ

â€¢ ••The Apostles as Mouth and Hand of God••
	• Perkins argued that the apostles were guided by the infallible assistance of the Spirit in preaching and writing
	• He believed they could not err in delivering doctrine to the church

â€¢ ••Perkins' Affirmation of Inerrancy••
	• Perkins was not shy about affirming the inerrancy of the Holy Scriptures
	• He believed the apostles could not err in things they delivered to the church

â€¢ ••Perkins' Recognition of Textual Criticism••
	• Perkins recognized that the manuscripts of the Bible have textual variations
	• He attributed these errors to copyists, rather than the Bible itself

â€¢ ••Perkins' Confidence in the Bible••
	• Perkins believed that despite textual variations, the sense of the Bible remains entire, sound, and incorrupt
	• He attributed this to the providence of God watching over the Bible

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Perkins' View of Scripture••
	• He believed the gospel is an absolute truth without error
	• He extended this to all Scripture, saying the apostles and prophets could not err
	• Their apprehension of revealed truths was framed by the Holy Ghost

â€¢ ••Perkins' Commentary Style••
	• He made a detailed commentary on specific phrases of the Bible
	• He found a model in Paul's handling of the Old Testament Scriptures
	• He believed in searching the Scriptures to consider every sentence and circumstance

â€¢ ••Trustworthiness of Scripture••
	• Perkins believed Scripture is trustworthy in its teachings on history and science
	• He saw even the details of biblical history as worthy of study and true
	• He believed creation was about 4,000 years before Christ came, as recorded by Moses and the prophets

â€¢ ••Spiritual Implications of Biblical History and Cosmology••
	• Biblical history and cosmology have spiritual implications
	• They cannot be separated from the message of God
	• They are meant to teach us to seek eternal things in heaven

â€¢ ••Perkins' View of Scripture as Inerrant••
	• He believed the Bible was inerrant and trustworthy in all that the original manuscripts affirm
	• He saw Scripture as written as if without man by the finger of God
	• It contains no human error

â€¢ ••Perkins' Influence on His Commentary••
	• His view of Scripture likely motivated him to labor in his lectures on Galatians
	• He believed the Bible gives us specific words that speak with God's authority to our beliefs, affections, and behaviors

â€¢ ••The Authors of the Commentary••
	• The volume is part of The Works of William Perkins
	• The exposition of chapters 1-5 comes from William Perkins, and chapter 6 from Ralph Cudworth
	• Cudworth also edited Perkins' contribution

â€¢ ••Perkins' Life and Ministry••
	• Perkins was a patriarch of the English Reformed and Puritan movement
	• He devoted his life to explaining and applying the Word to all of life

â€¢ ••The Commentary's Original Context••
	• To appreciate the commentary, we must know its authors, bibliographic history, and reception by the reading public
	• It has precedents in other commentaries on Galatians

â€¢ ••Perkins' View of Scripture vs. Rogers-McKim Thesis••
	• Perkins' view of Scripture as inerrant and trustworthy is in contrast to the Rogers-McKim thesis
	• Rogers cited secondary sources about Perkins, but did not become acquainted with his actual writings

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••William Perkins' Influence••
	• He was a best-selling author in Britain and Protestant Europe
	• His writings shaped and solidified historic Puritanism
	• He directly influenced a generation of ministerial students

â€¢ ••Perkins' Theological Insights••
	• He was a profound theologian who wrote on the Trinity, predestination, and Christ's mediatorial work
	• He wrote on the conversion of sinners into saints and various cases of conscience
	• He remained a pastor at heart, applying the Scriptures to the lives of men and women

â€¢ ••Perkins' Students and Legacy••
	• William Ames, one of his students, remembered him as a worthy master who instructed and stirred up students to seek after godliness
	• Later Reformed experiential writers in England referred to Perkins as "our judicious and learned Perkins"
	• Perkins' writings were regularly quoted and highly esteemed across the international Reformed orthodox community

â€¢ ••Perkins' Influence on Other Theologians••
	• Gisbertus Voetius commended Perkins as a resource for those with doubts about their conversion
	• John Owen denied the charge that English ministers believed in the perseverance of the saints "merely upon the authority and countenance of Mr. Perkins"
	• Jonathan Edwards lent Perkins' Works to a friend over a century after his death

â€¢ ••Ralph Cudworth's Role••
	• He was the original publisher of Perkins' exposition of Galatians 1-5
	• He also wrote the exposition of chapter 6
	• He was a fellow or instructor at Emmanuel College and served as a chaplain to King James I

â€¢ ••Cudworth's Background••
	• He was born at Werneth Hall, near Oldham
	• He matriculated at Emmanuel College in 1589 and progressed to BA, MA, and BD
	• He was mentored by Laurence Chaderton, a patriarch of the Puritan movement

â€¢ ••Cudworth's Ministry••
	• He served as a fellow or instructor at Emmanuel College until 1609
	• He served briefly at a few parishes, including the post of lecturer at St. Andrew's, Cambridge
	• He settled down as the rector of Aller, where he ministered until his death in 1624

â€¢ ••Cudworth's Publication••
	• His exposition of Galatians 6 was his only publication
	• He also served as chaplain to King James I

â€¢ ••Perkins' Preaching••
	• He preached on Galatians on the Lord's Day over the span of three years
	• His exposition was later published by Ralph Cudworth

â€¢ ••Perkins' Writings••
	• His writings were highly esteemed and broadly influential across the international Reformed orthodox community
	• His writings continued to speak after his death, reaching across the seas to influence theologians and pastors in Europe and New England

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Publication History of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Perkins' commentary on Galatians was first published in 1604, two years after his death.
	• The commentary was part of a three-volume edition of his collected works, which was first published in 1609.
	• The commentary was reprinted in several editions, including a separate volume in 1617.
• ••International Legacy••
	• The commentary was translated into Latin and Dutch and published in Geneva and Amsterdam.
	• The commentary became part of Perkins' international legacy.
• ••Later Publications••
	• After 1635, neither Perkins' Works nor the separate Galatians commentary was published in English.
	• Other individual books by Perkins continued to be published through the 17th and early 18th centuries.
• ••Rediscovery of Perkins••
	• Historians rediscovered Perkins in the 20th century through the work of scholars such as William Haller and Perry Miller.
	• The Pilgrim Classic Commentaries Series reproduced the 1617 edition of Galatians in 1989.
• ••Precedents of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Perkins was not the first person to write a commentary on Galatians.
	• Extant commentaries on Galatians include those by Ambrosiaster, Cassiodorus, and John Chrysostom.
• ••Reformed Expositions••
	• Within Perkins' own century, several Reformed expositions on Galatians had already appeared.
	• Commentaries on Galatians by John Calvin, Rudolf Gwalther, and Caspar Olevianus were published during this time.
• ••Martin Luther's Commentary••
	• Martin Luther published his own commentary on Galatians in 1519 and 1535.
	• Luther's commentary was published in English translation in 1575 and reprinted several times.
• ••Ralph Cudworth's Comparison••
	• Ralph Cudworth compared Perkins' commentary to Luther's, saying that Perkins' work was a "forerunner".
	• Cudworth's comparison suggests that readers in England already possessed a wealth of exposition on Galatians.
• ••Perkins' Reputation••
	• Perkins had a reputation as a good commentator.
	• A bibliography for ministers produced 40 years later listed Perkins as a notable commentator.
• ••New Edition••
	• This present volume is the first newly typeset edition of Perkins' commentary since the 17th century.
	• The new edition is based on the 1617 edition, which contains modest grammatical changes and standardized spellings.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Perkins' Commentary on Galatians••
	• Influenced not only pastors and theologians but also society as a whole
	• Was well used by later Puritans, including Nicholas Bownd, William Ames, John Norton, and Samuel Rutherford
• ••Influence of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Was cited by many prominent Puritans, including Thomas Adams
	• Was used to argue for simplicity of worship, evangelism, and conversion
• ••Thomas Adams' Use of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Made extensive use of a portion of Perkins' commentary without citing it
	• Used similar language and ideas in his sermons
• ••Comparison of Cudworth and Adams' Writings••
	• Cudworth's commentary on Galatians 6:2 was compared to Adams' sermon on 2 Corinthians 13:11
	• Similar ideas and language were used in both writings
• ••Architectural Metaphor••
	• Cudworth and Adams used an architectural metaphor to describe the church
	• Compared the church to a building with Christ as the foundation and cornerstone
• ••Proverbial Wisdom••
	• Cudworth and Adams used proverbial wisdom to illustrate their points
	• Used Italian proverbs to describe the importance of mortar in building a strong wall
• ••Importance of Bearing with One Another••
	• Cudworth and Adams emphasized the importance of bearing with one another
	• Used examples of bearing with infirmities and weaknesses to illustrate this point
• ••Mutual Support••
	• Cudworth and Adams emphasized the importance of mutual support in the church
	• Used examples of strong supporting the weak and weak supporting the strong
• ••Perkins' Commentary as a Resource••
	• Perkins' commentary on Galatians was a valuable resource for later Puritans
	• Was used to inform and shape their theology and practice
• ••Legacy of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Perkins' commentary on Galatians continues to be studied and used today
	• Remains an important resource for understanding the theology and practice of the Puritans.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Burden of Others••
	• One stone in an arch bears the weight of the rest, but not equally.
	• Similarly, individuals can ease each other's burdens.

â€¢ ••Christian Charity••
	• Christian charity causes us to suffer all things for the good of others.
	• This includes enduring labor, cost, and travel for the benefit of others.

â€¢ ••Types of Suffering••
	• Suffering for the sake of others (pro fratribus)
	• Suffering for the sake of brethren (propter fratres)
	• Suffering at the hands of brethren (a fratribus)

â€¢ ••Perkins' Commentary••
	• Written by William Perkins in the early 17th century.
	• First published in 1604.

â€¢ ••Influence of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Quoted by Thomas Boston during the Marrow Controversy in the 1720s.
	• Commended by Edward Williams in the 18th century.
	• Repeatedly referred to by John Brown in his 1853 commentary on Galatians.

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Perkins' Commentary••
	• ••Plain••: approached Scripture with confidence in its fundamental perspicuity.
	• ••Pedagogical••: aimed to communicate complex ideas in a simple and clear manner.
	• ••Polemical••: engaged in theological debates and controversies.
	• ••Practical••: applied theological concepts to everyday life.

â€¢ ••Perkins' View on Preaching••
	• Preachers should explain the text, deduce points of doctrine, and apply them to life.
	• Emphasized the importance of simplicity and plainness in preaching.

â€¢ ••Sources and Influences••
	• Perkins likely drew from a common source with Cudworth.
	• Commentaries directly contributed to the preaching of the day.

â€¢ ••Legacy of Perkins' Commentary••
	• Continued use and appreciation into the 19th century.
	• Not reprinted in English since 1635.

â€¢ ••Academic and Publishing Practices••
	• Writers freely borrowed from each other without attribution or citation in the early modern era.
	• Unacceptable practice in today's academic and publishing world.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Perkins' Approach to Scripture••
	• Examined the text to draw doctrines and teachings
	• Connected these teachings to the system of Christian belief
	• Focused on the literal sense of Scripture

â€¢ ••Rejection of Medieval Hermeneutics••
	• Rejected the fourfold sense of Scripture (literal, allegorical, tropological, anagogical)
	• Believed in only one full and entire sense of every place of Scripture
	• Argued that multiple senses lead to uncertainty and confusion

â€¢ ••Perkins' Hermeneutic••
	• Believed in a single sense for Scripture with many applications
	• Argued that interpretations and doctrines must be derived from the genuine meaning of Scripture

â€¢ ••Simplicity and Clarity••
	• Aimed to communicate with simplicity and clarity in his exposition
	• Believed that the ministry of the Word must be plain, perspicuous, and evident

â€¢ ••Pedagogical Approach••
	• Aiming to teach ordinary people the truths of the Bible
	• Used grammatical, rhetorical, and logical analysis to open up the sense of the text

â€¢ ••Systematic Theology••
	• Advised ministers to study systematic theology through the epistle to the Romans and the Gospel of John
	• Recommended reading the books of orthodox theologians both recent and ancient

â€¢ ••Tools for Deriving Doctrines••
	• Employed Ramist analysis to move from general principles to specific teachings
	• Used syllogistic reasoning to derive doctrines from the Scripture

â€¢ ••Ramist Analysis••
	• A method of moving from general principles to specific teachings
	• Used a series of distinctions to derive teachings

â€¢ ••Syllogistic Reasoning••
	• A tool for deriving doctrines from the Scripture
	• Used to resolve the text into specific doctrines

â€¢ ••Perkins' Goal••
	• To communicate the Lord's words with simplicity and clarity
	• To equip ordinary people with the truths of the Bible through his exposition

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Perkins' Theological Perspective••
	• Believes in a natural and spiritual life
	• Natural life is led by natural causes, while spiritual life is led by the Spirit
	• Two degrees of spiritual life: in this life and after the last judgment

â€¢ ••Perkins' Use of Syllogisms••
	• Uses a three-step approach to reasoning: major premise, minor premise, and conclusion
	• Often follows the form of a statement, "but" another statement, and "therefore" the conclusion

â€¢ ••Exposition of Galatians••
	• Regularly follows the pattern of explaining the words and phrases of each verse of Scripture
	• Deduces doctrines from the text by direct statement or logical inference

â€¢ ••Polemical Nature of Perkins' Exposition••
	• Combats doctrinal errors, especially those of Rome
	• Answers objections to Reformed doctrine and engages in lively debates

â€¢ ••Perkins' View of the Catholic Church••
	• Believes in a "catholic church" as the company of the predestinate
	• Seeks a more biblical, Reformed catholicism

â€¢ ••Criticism of Evangelical Protestants••
	• Criticizes evangelical Protestants who live hypocritical lives that contradict their doctrines

â€¢ ••Practical Nature of Perkins' Exposition••
	• Devotes significant space to drawing out the applications of the text
	• Deduces application from explicit statements and logical implications

â€¢ ••Ethical Principles in Perkins' Commentary••
	• Enunciates a wealth of ethical principles that can be used to build an entire ethical system

â€¢ ••Personal Response in Perkins' Exposition••
	• Believes that the ministry of the Word must be powerful and lively in operation
	• Urges hearers to feel the virtue of Christ's person, work, and will

â€¢ ••Perkins' Influence on Biblical Commentary••
	• Modern scholarship has often neglected early Reformed and Puritan writers
	• Perkins' commentary on Galatians is a model of logical and didactic exposition

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Perkins' commentary on Galatians••
	• Made a "sober attempt to deal with the text in its literal sense"
	• Sought to penetrate specific texts to larger theological issues
	• Had an immanently practical goal of practical application

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Perkins' commentary••
	• Peppered with "vivid imagery", "frequent similes", and "interrogative exchanges"
	• Kept the reader's attention and assisted preachers and teachers

â€¢ ••Perkins' focus on Jesus Christ••
	• Emphasized the trinitarian, Christ-centered faith
	• Saw the doctrine of the Trinity as the shape of the gospel

â€¢ ••Perkins' view of the Trinity••
	• Believed the persons of the Trinity are distinct, yet one in operation
	• Saw the work of redemption as a unified effort of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost

â€¢ ••Christ as the center of true religion••
	• Believed Christ is the substance or subject matter of the whole Bible
	• Saw true happiness as reconciliation with God in Christ

â€¢ ••Perkins' view of joy and rejoicing••
	• Believed true joy stands in reconciliation with God in Christ
	• Saw no limits to appropriate rejoicing in Christ

â€¢ ••Perkins' method of writing••
	• Used a plain, pedagogical, polemical, and practical method
	• Constructed a book that is one part biblical commentary, one part systematic theology, and one part experiential and practical exhortation

â€¢ ••The production of the volume••
	• Involved a team of people, including Linda Rudolph, Ann Dykema, and Joyce Griffith
	• Received editorial oversight from Dr. Joel Beeke and Dr. Derek Thomas

â€¢ ••Gratitude and thanks••
	• Expressed gratitude to various individuals and organizations for their support and assistance
	• Thanked the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ for the privilege of working on the project

â€¢ ••Legacy of Perkins' work••
	• Saw the editing of Perkins' commentary as a blessed exercise in obeying Hebrews 13:7-8
	• Believed the wealth of Perkins' work should be scattered abroad for modern readers

	• Perkins' works include commentaries on Galatians and the Creed
	• His works are still studied by theologians today for their insight into the Christian life and the importance of Scripture.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Importance of the Holy Scriptures••
	• The Holy Scriptures are given by divine inspiration and penned by holy men of God.
	• They are a precious depositum to be carefully kept in their integrity.

â€¢ ••The Role of the Church••
	• The church is called "the ground and pillar of truth" (1 Tim. 3:15).
	• It is not only to be defended by the sword of the magistrate, but also to be the pillar and foundation of faith.

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Scriptures••
	• To be the touchstone of truth.
	• To be the shop of remedies for all spiritual maladies.
	• To be an anchor in the blasts of temptation and waves of affliction.

â€¢ ••The Command to Search the Scriptures••
	• We are commanded to search the Scriptures as for silver (John 5:39).
	• We are to seek in them as for treasures.
	• We are to read in them continually, meditate on them day and night (Ps. 1:2).

â€¢ ••The Perfection of the Scriptures••
	• They are of such perfection that nothing may be added unto them, nor anything taken from them (Deut. 4:2).
	• They are of such infallible certainty that heaven and earth shall sooner pass away than one tittle fall to the ground (Matt. 5:18).

â€¢ ••The Pleasure and Profit of the Scriptures••
	• They are so pleasant and delightful that they exceed the honey and the honeycomb.
	• They are so profitable that no treasures may be compared unto them (Ps. 19:10).

â€¢ ••The Ability of the Scriptures to Save••
	• They are able to make us wiser than our enemies, than the aged, than our teachers (Ps. 119:98, etc.).
	• They are able to make us wise unto salvation (2 Tim. 3:15).
	• They are able to give us an inheritance among them that are sanctified (Acts 20:32).
	• They are able to save our souls (James 1:21).

â€¢ ••The Wonder of Christians Who Neglect the Scriptures••
	• It is a wonder that any calling themselves Christians make less account of the Book of God than the Romans in old time did of their twelve tables.

â€¢ ••The Critique of the Schoolmen••
	• The schoolmen (sententiaries, summists, and quodlibetaries) are chiefly to be censured for setting aside the Scriptures.
	• They have vanished away in vain speculations in their questions upon Lombard and Thomas.

â€¢ ••The Corruption of the Text••
	• Some glosses (like the gloss of Orleans) often corrupt the text.

â€¢ ••Examples of Short Commentaries••

â€¢ ••The Importance of the Literal Sense••
	• The literal sense is the only sense intended by the Spirit of God
	• The allegorical, tropological, and anagogical senses are secondary uses and applications

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Allegorical Sense••
	• The allegorical sense is not a reliable source of argument or doctrine
	• It is not a necessary part of understanding the Scripture

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Purpose of the Commentary••
	• To provide a concise and clear explanation of the Scripture
	• To help readers understand the doctrine and application of the text
	• To be a useful tool for both beginners and experts in understanding the Word of God

â€¢ ••Importance of Brevity and Perspicuity••
	• The golden mean of brevity and perspicuity is the best approach
	• A commentator should aim to be brief while still conveying the main points
	• This approach is especially important for readers who are not strong in the faith

â€¢ ••Types of Readers••
	• Some readers are babes in Christ and need everything explained in detail
	• Others are experts and can handle a more concise explanation
	• The commentator should aim to cater to both types of readers

â€¢ ••Types of Teachers••
	• Some teachers are expert and can divide the Word of God aright
	• Others are deceitful workmen who pervert the Scripture to their own destruction
	• The commentator should aim to help the former and hinder the latter

â€¢ ••Goals of the Commentary••
	• To help the ignorance of the one (those who need a more detailed explanation)
	• To hinder the malice of the other (those who pervert the Scripture)
	• To profit the most readers possible

â€¢ ••Unique Approach••
	• The commentator has combined brevity and perspicuity in a way that has not been done before
	• This approach is especially useful for the weighty points of doctrine in the epistle

â€¢ ••Luther's Praise••
	• Luther praised the commentary and took it upon himself to interpret it a second time
	• He began where he ended, according to the saying of Syraci des

â€¢ ••Request for Patronage••
	• The commentator requests the patronage of the reader's honor
	• This is done in the hope that the commentary will find favor and acceptance

â€¢ ••Testimony of Duty and Thankfulness••
	• The commentator testifies to their humble duty to the reader's honor
	• They express thankfulness to God for the riches of His grace bestowed upon the reader

â€¢ ••Closing••
	• The commentator takes their leave, desiring the Lord to honor the reader in their future endeavors
	• They pray that the reader will continue to be a notable instrument of God's glory.

â€¢ ••The Epistle of St. Paul to the Galatians••
	• The epistle was written in response to false apostles who slandered Paul.
	• The false apostles taught that justification and salvation were partly by Christ and partly by the law.

â€¢ ••Occasion and Scope of the Epistle••
	• The occasion of the epistle was the false apostles' slander of Paul.
	• The scope of the epistle is threefold:
		- Defending Paul's calling
		- Defending the truth of his doctrine
		- Prescribing rules of good life

â€¢ ••Chapter 1 of the Epistle••
	• The epistle has three parts: a preface, an instruction, and a conclusion.
	• The preface is in the first five verses and has two parts: an inscription and a salutation.

â€¢ ••Inscription and Salutation••
	• The inscription sets down the persons who wrote the epistle and the persons to whom it is sent.
	• Paul commends himself to the Galatians by his office and function as an apostle.

â€¢ ••Paul's Apostleship••
	• Paul's apostleship was not called by men, but by Jesus Christ and God the Father.
	• He was called immediately by God, rather than being ordained by the church of Antioch.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Calling••
	• Paul was called by Christ and God the Father for three reasons
	• To signify the consent of will in the Father and Christ
	• To teach us about the Trinity and the Godhead

â€¢ ••The Trinity••
	• The Godhead consists of the Father, Jesus Christ, and the Holy Ghost
	• The Trinity cannot be conceived of as separate entities

â€¢ ••The Father's Role••
	• The Father is the fountain of all good things that come to us through Christ
	• The Father raised Christ from the dead to prove He was His natural Son

â€¢ ••The Significance of the Resurrection••
	• The resurrection of Christ declared Him to be the Son of God
	• Paul applies the words of Psalm 2:7 to the time of Christ's resurrection

â€¢ ••Paul's Calling as an Apostle••
	• Paul was called after Christ's resurrection, when He sat at the right hand of God
	• This was a unique calling, unlike the other apostles who were called during Christ's humiliation

â€¢ ••The Importance of a Calling for Ministers••
	• Every minister of the gospel should have a good and lawful calling
	• A man cannot preach unless he is sent by God

â€¢ ••The Anabaptist View••
	• The Anabaptists believe that anyone can preach without a special calling
	• This view is foolish and fantastical, according to the author

â€¢ ••The Meaning of Self-Ordination••
	• When the Corinthians ordained themselves to the ministry, it meant they set themselves apart in their hearts
	• This is not the same as a formal calling or ordination

â€¢ ••The Role of the Church••
	• The church has authority to call and ordain ministers, but only as a ministry or service
	• The church's authority is to testify, declare, and approve whom God has called

â€¢ ••The Manifestation of a Calling••
	• The callings of ministers must be manifest to their own consciences and the consciences of their hearers
	• This is because they are ambassadors, instruments, and the mouth of God

â€¢ ••The Importance of God's Spirit in Ministry••

	• The hearers need the Spirit's operation in their hearts
	• Without the Spirit, the teacher cannot pray in faith or apply God's promises

â€¢ ••Knowledge of Calling and Its Consequences••

	• It also breeds reverence and obedience in the hearts of hearers

â€¢ ••How to Know One's Calling••

	• If one lacks a good conscience, their calling is still good and lawful, but it needs to be repented of

	• Three types: called by man, called by God through men, and called immediately by Christ
	• Paul and the apostles were called immediately by Christ

â€¢ ••The Apostles' Unique Calling••
	• They were called immediately by Christ, taught by immediate inspiration, and aided by the Spirit
	• They had promises and infallible assistance from God

â€¢ ••The Certainty of Our Religion••
	• Faith is the essential note of the church
	• The word of God is certain because it was written by the apostles, who could not err

â€¢ ••The Doctrine of the Apostles••
	• It is the immediate word of God, given by inspiration for both matter and words
	• It is distinct from the doctrine of the church, which is both the word of God and the word of man

â€¢ ••Paul's Authority••
	• He spoke some things of himself, but not without the Spirit's assistance
	• His writings are the immediate and mere word of God, and must be obeyed as such

â€¢ ••The Authority of Apostles••
	• Their authority, office, and function ceased with them
	• There is no succession of apostolic authority to the pope of Rome

â€¢ ••The Infallibility of the Apostles••
	• They were aided by the infallible assistance of God's Spirit
	• Their writings are the immediate word of God, and must be obeyed as such

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Calling••
	• Paul was called by Jesus Christ, not by man
	• This implies that Christ is both God and man
	• Paul's calling is considered superior to other apostles because it occurred after Christ's resurrection

â€¢ ••Address to the Galatians••
	• Paul writes to the churches of Galatia, including both pastors and people
	• The term "brethren" refers to those who have separated themselves from pagans and received the faith of Christ

â€¢ ••Paul's Purpose••
	• Paul writes in the names of the brethren to avoid being seen as delivering private doctrine
	• He wants to ensure that his teachings are grounded in the writings of Moses and the prophets

â€¢ ••The Importance of Consent••
	• Paul writes with the consent of the brethren to better persuade the Galatians to receive his doctrine
	• The consent of pastors and people is of great excellence in conveying the truth of the gospel

â€¢ ••The Force of Consent••
	• Consent can prepare the heart and move it to believe, but it is not the cause of faith
	• The word of God is the object and cause of faith, and consent is only a means to stir up a liking for the doctrine

â€¢ ••Avoiding Errors••
	• Error 1: Consent is not a certain mark of the true church, as it can be found among the wicked
	• Error 2: The consent of believers is not the true and living Scripture, but rather a rude and imperfect extract of it

â€¢ ••The Foundation of Consent••
	• Consent must have its foundation in Christ and flow to the members
	• This consent stands in three things: faith and doctrine, affection, and unity

â€¢ ••The Nature of Consent••
	• Consent in faith and doctrine is essential for unity among believers
	• Consent in affection is necessary for a genuine and heartfelt agreement

â€¢ ••The Role of the Written Word••
	• The written Word is the first and perfect pattern of the mind and will of God
	• The inward consent of the hearts of men is only a rude and imperfect extract of it

â€¢ ••The Importance of True Doctrine••
	• Ministers of the gospel must deliver nothing but what is founded in the writings of the prophets and apostles
	• They must also have the consent of the true church, especially of those who have restored the gospel in this last age

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Consent among Christians••
	• The Bible emphasizes the importance of being of one heart and consenting in speech (Acts 2:47, 1 Cor 1:10)
	• The papists claim to have perfect consent among themselves, but this is not the case

â€¢ ••Papists' lack of consent••
	• The proper points of popery were not known to the apostles or the apostolic churches
	• False doctrines were introduced in the ages following, and are not held by the fathers and councils as they are by the papists

â€¢ ••Differences among Christians••
	• There are differences in doctrine, such as the descent of Christ into hell and the pains of Christ in His agony and passion
	• However, all agree on the foundation of religion and the infinite merit and efficacy of Christ's death

â€¢ ••Government of the visible church••
	• There are differences in the manner of execution and administration, but all agree on the substance of government

â€¢ ••Eucharist••
	• All churches agree that Christ is present in the Eucharist and that His body and blood are there to be eaten and drunk
	• The difference is only in the manner of His presence, whether it be spiritual or local

â€¢ ••Judging churches••
	• We must follow Paul's example in giving judgment of churches of our time with meekness and moderation

â€¢ ••Rules for judging churches••
	• Rule 1: Consider the faults of the church, whether they be in manners or doctrine
	• Rule 2: Determine whether the error is in the whole church or just some individuals
	• Rule 3: Consider whether the error is in the foundation of religion or not

â€¢ ••Error in doctrine••
	• If the error is in some individuals and not in the whole church, it remains a church
	• If the error is in the foundation of religion, the church separates from Christ and ceases to be a church

â€¢ ••Error of human frailty vs. obstinacy••
	• If the error is due to human frailty, the church is still a church, even if it is in error
	• If the error is due to obstinacy, the church separates from Christ and ceases to be a church

â€¢ ••Separating from a church••
	• We may separate from a church if it is in error in the foundation of religion and obstinately refuses to correct its error
	• However, we should still pray for the continuance and increase of consent among Christians.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Paul's Separation from the Church of Galatia••
	• Paul separated the church of Ephesus and Rome from the Galatians (Acts 19:9; 28:28)
	• He did this because they had erred in their foundation and were following a different gospel

••2. Paul's Admonition to the Church of Galatia••
	• Paul writes to the Galatians as brethren and calls them churches, despite their error
	• He had only begun to admonish the church in this epistle and had not yet given judgment

••3. Condemning the Church without Sufficient Conviction••
	• Many people have condemned the Church of England without sufficient conviction
	• This is rash and unmoderated, and does not follow the proper process of examination and conviction

••4. The Church of Rome is Not a Church of God••
	• The Church of Rome may have the name, but it openly and obstinately opposes the principles of Christian religion
	• This does not mean that the Church of Galatia is a church of God, but rather that it is a people who have erred

••5. The Identity of the Church of Galatia••
	• The Church of Galatia was a people of Asia the Less
	• Although they were famous churches in the days of the apostle, the country is now under the dominion of the Turk

••6. The Importance of Repentance••
	• The desolation of the country of Asia the Less shows what God might have done to England for the contempt of the gospel
	• Unless England repents and brings forth better fruits of the gospel, it will suffer a similar fate

••7. The Salutation in the Epistle••
	• The salutation in the epistle is a prayer for grace and peace from God the Father and our Lord Jesus Christ

••8. The Meaning of Grace••
	• Grace is God's gracious favor and good will, whereby He is well pleased with His elect in and for Christ
	• It is not a gift in man, but rather a cause of the gift of holiness and charity

••9. The Error of the Papists••
	• The papists teach that the grace which makes us grateful to God is the infused gift of holiness and charity
	• However, this is not the case, as we are first pleased with God by grace in Christ, and then sanctified and endued with charity

••10. The Meaning of Peace••
	• Peace is a gift in us, and has three parts: peace of conscience, peace with the creatures, and peace in respect of our enemies and the beasts of the field
	• Peace of conscience is a quietness and tranquility of mind arising from a sense and apprehension of reconciliation with God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Third Part of Peace: Prosperity and Good Success••
	• The righteous man's actions prosper (Gen. 39:1-2)
	• Joseph's success as Potiphar's steward was due to his fear of God

â€¢ ••Causes of Grace and Peace••
	• God the Father and Jesus Christ are the two causes of grace and peace
	• They are one God, but distinct in their manner of working
	• The Father gives grace through the Son, and Christ procures and gives grace

â€¢ ••The Role of Grace in Our Lives••
	• Election, vocation, faith, justification, love, and good works are all of grace
	• Every good inclination and work is a result of God's grace
	• Human will is not the cause of good things, but rather a subject to receive God's grace

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Will and Grace••
	• Will is an instrument of God's grace, not the cause of good things
	• Human cooperation with God's grace is not a meritorious work, but rather a response to God's initiative

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• Recognizing that all good things come from God's grace, not human effort
	• Humility is the foundation of a right relationship with God

â€¢ ••The Chief Good Things to Seek••
	• The favor of God in Christ
	• The peace of a good conscience

â€¢ ••The Example of David and Paul••
	• David and Paul valued the favor of God and peace above all else
	• They considered earthly riches and honors as nothing compared to the blessings of God

â€¢ ••The Fault of Most Men••
	• They prioritize earthly pursuits over seeking God's favor and peace
	• They use God's blessings without acknowledging the source of those blessings

â€¢ ••Our Duty••
	• Above all things, seek God's favor and peace
	• True happiness and blessedness come from having the favor of God and a good conscience

â€¢ ••The Importance of Peace with Grace••
	• Peace without grace is no peace (Isa. 57:21)
	• Laughter and prosperity without God's favor lead to destruction (Eccl. 2:2, 1 Thess. 5)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Fault of Worshiping an Absolute God••
	• Christians often worship God without acknowledging the Father and Christ.
	• This fault turns God into an idol.
	• The order of God's communication is through the Father and then Christ.

â€¢ ••The Order of God's Communication••
	• The Father is the fountain of grace.
	• Christ is the conduit or pipe to convey grace from the Father to us.
	• We receive grace for grace through Christ (John 1:16).

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Christ is the one who procures life, teaches the way of life, and commands us to walk in the way of life.
	• He is our Lord, not just our Savior.

â€¢ ••The Fault of Modern Times••
	• Many people profess Christ but do not acknowledge Him as Lord.
	• They want a Savior to deliver them from hell, but not a Lord to command them.

â€¢ ••The Meaning of "Our Lord"••
	• Christ is called "our Lord" to signify that those who receive grace and peace acknowledge Him as their Lord.
	• They yield subjection to Him in heart and life.

â€¢ ••The Effect of Christ's Death••
	• Christ gave Himself for our sins to deliver us from the present evil world.
	• He presented Himself as a price and sacrifice for sin to God the Father.

â€¢ ••The Five Things to Consider in Christ's Giving••
	• The giver: Christ
	• The thing given: Christ Himself
	• The end of His giving: to make satisfaction for our sins
	• The end of His giving: to deliver us from the present evil world
	• The cause that moved Christ to give Himself: the will of God

â€¢ ••The Deliverance from the Present Evil World••
	• Deliverance is not in this life in respect of place, but in respect of quality.
	• It means being severed from the condition of sinful men by sanctification and newness of life.

â€¢ ••The Voluntary Nature of Christ's Death••
	• Christ's death and sacrifice were voluntary.
	• He showed this by not fleeing when He was to be attached.

â€¢ ••The Praise of God••
	• Christ's voluntary death and sacrifice deserve praise and glory.
	• To God be glory for ever and ever, Amen.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Christ's Death as a Sacrifice••
	• Christ's death was a deliberate act to satisfy sin
	• His loud and strong voice showed He was Lord of death
	• His death was a sacrifice, not just a human act

â€¢ ••The Work of Redemption Exceeds Creation••
	• In creation, Christ gave creatures to man
	• In redemption, Christ gave Himself as a sacrifice
	• This shows the magnitude of Christ's love and sacrifice

â€¢ ••Christ's Sacrifice is Unique••
	• Christ gave Himself, not just an angel or a mere man
	• All merits and satisfactions for sin come from Christ's person
	• Human works and satisfactions are not enough to save us

â€¢ ••Receiving Christ's Sacrifice••
	• We must receive Christ with hungry hearts
	• We must take Him by force, as the violent take what they want
	• We must give our bodies and souls to Him in thankfulness

â€¢ ••The First End of Christ's Sacrifice••
	• To be a sacrifice and ransom for sin
	• This knowledge works love in us and moves us to love Christ and the Father
	• It breeds godly sorrow for our sins and motivates us to amend our lives

â€¢ ••The True Beginning of Amendment of Life••
	• If Christ gave Himself to redeem us, we must take up a purpose of not sinning
	• We must never wittingly sin again
	• This knowledge is the foundation of comfort in those who truly turn to Christ

â€¢ ••The Foundation of Comfort••
	• The price is paid for our sins
	• We can boldly oppose the satisfaction of Christ against hell, death, and judgment
	• We have an Advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the just

â€¢ ••Applying Christ's Sacrifice••
	• We must apply Christ's gift and sacrifice to ourselves by faith
	• Faith goes before conversion, but they are both together in practice
	• Misapplying Christ's benefits without turning to Him is a mistake

â€¢ ••The Second End of Christ's Sacrifice••
	• To take us out of this evil world
	• We must be grieved and displeased at the wickedness of the world
	• We must not fashion ourselves after the wicked lives of the world

â€¢ ••Proving the Good Will of God••
	• We must prove what is the good will of God in all things
	• We must not conform to the wicked lives of the world
	• We must seek to do God's will in our lives

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The World and Heaven••
	• We must not dwell in the world, but in heaven (Rev. 12:2)

	• We must prepare ourselves for death and enter into our heavenly home

â€¢ ••The Nature of the World••
	• Paul calls the world an "evil world" to signify that it is full of sin
	• Civil virtues and life are no better than sins before God
	• The papists' error is thinking that good works can be done without grace

â€¢ ••The Will of God••
	• The will of God is the cause of Christ's sacrifice

â€¢ ••The Gospel and God as Father••

	• Our praise and thanks must come from a serious affection of the heart

â€¢ ••The Second Part of the Epistle••
	• Begins with a reproof of the Galatians for revolting from the gospel
	• Has two parts: doctrine and manners
	• The first part concerns doctrine and begins in the sixth verse of the epistle

â€¢ ••The Syllogism of the First Part••
	• If Paul was called by God to teach and his doctrine is true, the Galatians should not have revolted
	• Paul was called by God to teach and his doctrine is true
	• Therefore, the Galatians should not have revolted from his doctrine

â€¢ ••The False Teachers••
	• They are persuading the Galatians to follow another gospel
	• This other gospel is not a true gospel, but a false one that overthrows the gospel of Christ
	• The false teachers are causing the Galatians to be carried away from the true gospel

â€¢ ••The Galatians' Revolt••
	• They are revolting from the true gospel to a false one
	• They are being persuaded by false teachers to follow a different doctrine of salvation
	• This revolt is happening soon after Paul's departure

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Epistle••
	• To reprove the Galatians for revolting from the true gospel
	• To instruct them in the doctrine of the gospel
	• To encourage them to live in obedience to God as their Father

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Paul's Manner of Reproof••
	• Paul reproves the Galatians with meekness and tenderness of heart
	• He frames his reproof with great wariness and circumspection
	• He blames them but in part, and lays the principal blame on others
• ••The Fault Reproved••
	• The Galatians' revolt was a departure from the calling whereby they were called to the grace of Christ
	• There are two kinds of revolt: particular and general
		- Particular revolt: departing from the faith in some principal points
		- General revolt: wholly forsaking the faith and name of Christ
• ••Characteristics of the Galatians' Revolt••
	• The revolt was particular in the point of justification
	• It was a result of weakness and human frailty, not obstinacy
	• Paul signifies this when he says they were carried by others
• ••The Time of the Revolt••
	• The revolt was short, happening "so soon"
	• This shows the lightness and inconstancy of human nature, especially in matters of religion
• ••The Cause of the Revolt••
	• The Galatians were carried away by others
	• The doctrine of Paul and the grace of Christ were the things from which they departed
• ••The Result of the Revolt••
	• The Galatians turned to another gospel
	• This was a result of being troubled by some people
• ••The Importance of Perseverance••
	• To constantly persevere in the profession of the true faith, we must receive the gospel simply for itself
	• We must not receive the gospel for any external reasons, such as the law of the land
• ••The Nature of Human Religiosity••
	• Humans are like wax, and are easily influenced by external factors
	• The law of the land often makes people embrace the gospel, rather than conscience
• ••The Danger of External Influences••
	• The cross and persecution can make people question or forsake the gospel
	• We must be careful not to be swayed by external influences, but to stand firm in our faith
• ••The Need for Love and Tenderness in Reproof••
	• When reproving others, we should show love and tenderness, as Paul did
	• This helps to keep love and avoid causing unnecessary harm.

â€¢ ••Mortification and Renewal••
	• We must be mortified and renewed in the spirit of our minds
	• Suffer no secret unbelief, hypocrisy, or spiritual pride in our hearts
	• This is necessary for spiritual growth and obedience to God

â€¢ ••Christian Modesty••

	• We should give praise to God and his instruments, but not to ourselves
	• This is an example of Christian civility to be joined with our faith

â€¢ ••The Gospel and Grace••
	• The scope of the gospel is to bring men to the grace of Christ
	• We should live our lives seasoned with the savor of God's grace
	• We owe God great thankfulness for the benefit of being called to His grace

â€¢ ••Separation from the World••
	• The Galatians were removed from the doctrine of Paul and the grace of God
	• They joined the works of the law with Christ and His grace in justification

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome••
	• The Church of Rome has departed from the grace of God by making a concurrence of grace and works in justification
	• We cannot make reconciliation with this church in religion because it is an enemy of God's grace

â€¢ ••The Galatians' Revolt••
	• The Galatians revolted to a "better" gospel that was compounded of Christ and the works of the law
	• This was a forged gospel taught by false apostles and quickly received by the Galatians

â€¢ ••Human Nature••
	• Human nature is curious and discontent, always seeking new things
	• We are not content with the good things of God unless they are framed to our minds
	• This is a fault that we must be aware of and avoid

â€¢ ••False Teachers••
	• False teachers often have profound learning of their own, but it is not based on the doctrine of the apostles
	• They often set up their own traditions and doctrines, which are not equal to Scripture

â€¢ ••The Lord's Condemnation••
	• The Lord condemns our curiousness and discontentment
	• He forbids plowing with the ox and the ass, which represents our desire for new and different things

â€¢ ••Our Fault••
	• We, who profess the gospel, are not altogether free from this fault
	• We often seek to garnish our sermons with skill of arts, tongues, and variety of reading, rather than simply preaching the gospel.

â€¢ ••The Sin of Discontentment••

	• This lesson must be learned by the heart, not just the brain and tongue

â€¢ ••The Gospel of Christ••
	• There is only one gospel, one in number and no more
	• This gospel is the way of salvation by Christ, which has been the same from the beginning of the world to the end

â€¢ ••The Body and Blood of Christ••
	• The body and blood of Christ were present to believers in the Old Testament
	• This was through divine acceptance and faith, which makes things that are not seen present to the believing heart

â€¢ ••The Falsehood of Every Man's Own Religion••

	• This would lead to multiple gospels and opinions, which is not the case

â€¢ ••The World's Wisdom and God's Wisdom••

â€¢ ••The Devil's Belief in God••

	• However, this does not mean that the devil comes to God or is saved

â€¢ ••The Authors of the Revolt••

â€¢ ••Discerning False Doctrines••
	• False and erroneous doctrines can be discerned by their tendency to trouble and disquiet the good conscience
	• This is a key characteristic of false doctrines

â€¢ ••The Corruption of the Roman Religion••
	• The Roman religion is corrupt and unsound because it tends to trouble and disquiet the good conscience
	• This is evident in its teachings on justification by works, the vow of single life, and other doctrines.

â€¢ ••The Gospel of Christ••
	• Brings peace and perfect joy to the good conscience
	• Ministers a perfect remedy for every sin and comfort for every distress

â€¢ ••The False Apostles' Teaching••

â€¢ ••The Objection Against Paul's Teaching••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Sticking to the Gospel••

â€¢ ••Interpretation of "accursed"••
	• The word "accursed" means to be severed from Christ and adjudged to eternal perdition.
	• It is a censure or judgment of the church, not a personal curse.

â€¢ ••Who is accursed?••
	• Heinous offenders and desperate persons of whose amendment there is no hope.
	• This judgment is seldom pronounced upon anyone.

â€¢ ••Examples of accursed individuals••
	• Paul accursed Alexander the coppersmith (2 Tim. 4:14).
	• The church accursed Julian the Emperor.

â€¢ ••The church's role in accursing individuals••
	• The church pronounces individuals accursed whom God accurses.
	• The church's judgment is based on the Word and experience.

â€¢ ••God's role in accursing individuals••
	• God's judgment is given in heaven, whereby He accurses obstinate and notorious offenders.
	• God ratifies and approves the church's judgment.

â€¢ ••The process of accursing individuals••
	• The church pronounces individuals accursed based on God's judgment.
	• God ratifies the church's judgment in heaven.
	• Private individuals hold those accursed in execration.

â€¢ ••The importance of preserving gospel purity••
	• The church must be careful to preserve the purity of the gospel.
	• Corrupters of the gospel must be identified and accursed.

â€¢ ••The meaning of "beside"••
	• The preposition "beside" (Ï€Î±Ï á¾½  á½ƒ ) means "diverse" or "distinct", not "contrary".
	• Paul's mind is that those who teach diverse or distinct doctrine should be observed.

â€¢ ••The relationship between the Gospel of John and the Apocalypse••
	• The Gospel of John and the Apocalypse were written after the Epistle to the Galatians.
	• They do not introduce diverse doctrine, but rather one and the same substance.

â€¢ ••The relationship between the Canons of Councils and the gospel••
	• The Canons of Councils are traditions touching order and comeliness.
	• They do not prescribe anything as necessary to justification and salvation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Ministry of Accusation••
	• The church's role is to publish and testify who are accursed of God.
	• Private individuals should not curse others without the church's declaration.
• ••Authority of the Apostles••
	• If an apostle or angel preaches contrary to the apostles' teachings, they must be accursed.
	• The apostles' teachings are authoritative because they represent God's authority.
• ••Certainty of the Word••
	• The word preached and written by Paul is as certain as if it were written by God Himself.
	• This is because Paul spoke by the Spirit of God.
• ••Authority of the Gospel••
	• The articles of faith and doctrine of the gospel are superior to all men and angels.
	• The church and councils cannot authorize the Word of God in the mind and conscience of any man.
• ••Scripture as the Principal Authority••
	• The opinion of the papists that Scripture cannot be known to be the Word of God without the church's testimony is false.
	• The principal authority of Scripture is sufficient to authorize itself without external testimony.
• ••Judging Doctrines and Revelations••
	• Doctrines and revelations that contradict the written Word are accursed.
	• Examples include intercession of saints, prayer to the dead, and purgatory.
• ••Papists and Schoolmen••
	• The writings of papists and schoolmen that contradict the apostles' teachings are accursed.
	• Students of divinity should read them with caution and only in the last place.
• ••Warning Against False Teachers••
	• If anyone preaches contrary to the apostles' teachings, they must be accursed.
	• This warning is repeated to emphasize the importance of adhering to the apostles' teachings.
• ••Love of the Gospel••
	• Those who prefer the writings of papists and schoolmen over the apostles' teachings show little love for the gospel.
	• This is a sign of a lack of love for the gospel in their hearts.
• ••Conclusion••
	• The apostles' teachings are authoritative and must be followed.
	• Those who contradict the apostles' teachings are accursed.

â€¢ ••Paul's Purpose in Speaking••
	• To signify that he spoke advisedly
	• To emphasize the infallibility of the doctrine he preached
	• To remind the Galatians and others to observe and remember his teachings as the foundation of their religion

â€¢ ••The Importance of Receiving the Gospel••
	• The Galatians received the gospel with care and faith

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Not Receiving the Gospel••

â€¢ ••Paul's Ministry••
	• He does not preach to please men, but to please God
	• He does not seek to frame his doctrine to suit the affections of men, but to proclaim the word of God

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Ministry••
	• The ministry is not about the word or doctrine of man, but of God
	• Ministers must handle their doctrine with modesty, humility, and reverence

â€¢ ••The Proper Reception of the Gospel••
	• Hearers must receive the doctrine as the very word of God, not as the word of man
	• This is essential for the gospel to be effective and for God to be glorified

â€¢ ••The Right Manner of Dispensing the Word••
	• The word must not be dispensed to please men, but to please God
	• Ministers must keep a good conscience and not apply their doctrine to the affections, humors, and dispositions of men

â€¢ ••Paul's Relationship with God••
	• He is the servant of God, not of men
	• He seeks to please God, even if it means denouncing curses against those who teach otherwise

â€¢ ••The Importance of Considering God's Majesty••
	• Ministers must consider the majesty of God when handling their doctrine
	• This is essential for God to be glorified and for the gospel to be effective

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Not Considering God's Majesty••
	• The want of consideration for God's majesty is the cause of some people contemning the ministry of the word
	• Others are not touched and moved in hearing because they do not consider the majesty of God.

â€¢ ••The Ministry of the Word••
	• Must be a sacrificing knife to kill and mortify the old Adam in us
	• Must be a means of spiritual growth and fruitfulness
	• Must not always please men, but rather challenge and restrain them

â€¢ ••The Nature of Man••
	• Is prone to rebellion and enmity against God

	• Is filled with vanity and worldly desires when driven by ambition

â€¢ ••The Character of a Faithful Minister••
	• Must deny pride and ambition
	• Must seek the glory of God in their calling
	• Must consider God as a judge and seek to approve their thoughts and actions to Him

â€¢ ••The Example of Paul••
	• Paul's profession of faith is an example to follow
	• He did not preach to please men, but to please God
	• He had sinned in the past, but now sought to serve God

â€¢ ••The Gospel is not of Man••
	• Paul's gospel was not devised by man, but was from God
	• It was preached by the authority of God, not by human authority
	• This is the foundation of Paul's argument in the epistle

â€¢ ••The Importance of Assurance••
	• It is necessary to be assured that the doctrine of the gospel and Scripture is from God
	• This assurance can be obtained through the evidence of God's Spirit in the Scriptures

â€¢ ••The Testimony of Scripture••
	• There are two testimonies that settle our consciences that Scripture is the Word of God
	• The evidence of God's Spirit, imprinted and expressed in the Scriptures
	• The internal witness of the Holy Spirit in our hearts

â€¢ ••The Authority of Scripture••
	• Scripture is the Word of God, not of man
	• It is authoritative and trustworthy
	• It must be obeyed and followed

â€¢ ••The Role of the Minister••
	• The minister's role is to preach the Word of God, not to please men
	• They must be faithful to their calling and seek to please God
	• They must be willing to challenge and restrain their congregation

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Ambition••
	• Ambition can lead to a focus on pleasing men, rather than pleasing God
	• It can lead to a lack of faithfulness and a failure to serve God
	• It can result in a ministry that is not pleasing to God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Supremacy of Scripture••
	• The Word of God is above all other words and writings
	• Contains 13 points that establish its authority and truth
• ••The Purity of Scripture••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Faith and Certainty••
	• The author emphasizes the importance of faith and certainty in the promises of God.
	• The lack of this certainty can lead to heresy, apostasy, atheism, and iniquity.

â€¢ ••Church of Rome's Error••
	• The Church of Rome teaches that one cannot know the Scripture to be the Word of God without the testimony of the church in latter times.
	• The author argues that the testimony of the Spirit and the apostles is sufficient for certainty, even without the church's testimony.

â€¢ ••Assurance of Teachers' Calling••
	• It is necessary for men to be assured that their teachers' calling and authority are from God.
	• The author discusses how to determine if a teacher's calling is from God.

â€¢ ••Types of Callings••
	• There are two types of callings: ordinary and extraordinary.
	• Ordinary callings occur through the voices and consent of men, following the laws of God's word.

â€¢ ••Extraordinary Callings••
	• Extraordinary callings occur when God calls otherwise, through three means:
		- Immediate voice (e.g., Abraham, Moses, apostles)
		- Message of a creature (e.g., Aaron, Elijah, Philip)
		- Instinct (e.g., Philip the deacon, men of Cyprus and Cyrene)

â€¢ ••Objections to Extraordinary Callings••
	• It may be objected that extraordinary callings should be confirmed by miracles.
	• The author responds that the first preachers of the gospel did not need new miracles, as they preached the old doctrine of the prophets and apostles.

â€¢ ••Rules for Determining Extraordinary Callings••
	• Three rules can be observed to determine if a calling is extraordinary:
		- Extraordinary callings only occur when there is no room for ordinary callings.
		- The called must be tried by the word, both for doctrine and life.
		- Extraordinary teachers should be ordained as other ordinary ministers after the laws of God's Word.

â€¢ ••Succession of Ministers••
	• There are three types of succession: of persons and doctrine, of persons alone, and of doctrine alone.
	• The author argues that the succession of doctrine alone is sufficient, as our ministers succeed the apostles in doctrine.

â€¢ ••Ordination of Ministers••
	• Ministers should be ordained according to the laws of God's Word.
	• This includes being tried by the word, both for doctrine and life.

â€¢ ••Verification of Extraordinary Callings••
	• The three rules for determining extraordinary callings are verified in the first preachers of the gospel.
	• These rules can be used to distinguish between true and false teachers.

â€¢ ••The Power of the Keys••
	• The power of the keys (order and jurisdiction) is tied to doctrine in the New Testament.
	• This power is given by God and allows for the choice of ministers from within the community.

â€¢ ••The Calling of Ministers••
	• The calling of ministers is an ordinary process, ordained by predecessors.

â€¢ ••The Divine Nature of Callings••
	• The church's callings are considered divine, despite any defects.
	• This assurance is of great use, causing ministers to make a conscience of their duty and inspiring reverence and obedience in hearers.

â€¢ ••Paul's Calling••
	• Paul received the gospel and his office from Christ, not from man.
	• He learned the gospel through revelation, not human teaching.

â€¢ ••Types of Revelation••
	• Revelation can be ordinary (through the word preached and the Spirit) or extraordinary (without the word preached).
	• Extraordinary revelation can occur through voice, dreams, vision, or instinct.

â€¢ ••Paul's Extraordinary Revelation••
	• Paul's revelation was extraordinary, partly through vision, voice, and instinct.
	• This revelation was a miracle, allowing him to see and hear Christ visibly.

â€¢ ••The Location of Christ••
	• Christ was not on earth, but in heaven, when Paul heard His voice and saw Him.
	• The opening of the heavens was a necessary miracle for Paul's revelation.

â€¢ ••The Authority of Paul's Teaching••
	• Paul's authority to teach comes from Christ, not from man.
	• The gospel he preaches is divine, not human, and preached with divine authority.

â€¢ ••The Scope of the Text••
	• The text contains many points of doctrine, including the power of the keys and the divine nature of callings.

â€¢ ••The Significance of Paul's Revelation••
	• Paul's revelation is a confirmation of the former verse, emphasizing the divine origin of his teaching and authority.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Conversion••
	• Paul's conversion was a result of God's mercy and grace, not his own will or natural disposition.
	• Paul was an "elect vessel" before his conversion, but was wicked and sinful.

â€¢ ••The Role of God's Mercy••
	• The difference between men before God is not in their will or actions, but in God's mercy.
	• Paul quotes Romans 9:11 to emphasize this point.

â€¢ ••Pelagian Error••
	• The idea that men can earn God's supernatural grace through their own efforts is a Pelagian error.
	• This error is refuted by Paul's testimony of his own conversion.

â€¢ ••Paul's Confession••
	• Paul openly confesses his wicked past life, demonstrating that he wrote the Scriptures by the Spirit of God.
	• This confession serves to justify Paul's calling as an apostle and to check the boasting of others.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Scripture••
	• The Scriptures are not books of policy, but the very Word of God.
	• They are not written to keep men in awe, but to reveal God's truth.

â€¢ ••Paul's Profession••
	• Before his calling, Paul's profession was Judaism, specifically Pharisaism.
	• Pharisaism was a sect of the Jews that emphasized external obedience to the law.

â€¢ ••Pharisaic Doctrines••
	• The Pharisees believed in one God, without distinction of persons.
	• They believed in a Messiah with one nature, and an earthly kingdom.
	• They emphasized external obedience to the law, and a natural freedom of the will.
	• They believed in justification by works of the law, without obedience to the Messiah.
	• They observed many unwritten traditions, which were considered essential to worship.

â€¢ ••The Jewish People••
	• The Jewish people had forsaken God, but God had not forsaken them.
	• They were still considered a people of God due to the temple and outward worship.
	• Only a small remnant of them truly feared God and believed in the Messiah.

â€¢ ••The Remnant of Israel••
	• The remnant of Israel consisted of those who truly feared God and believed in the Messiah.
	• Examples of this remnant include Joseph, Mary, Zacharias, Elisabeth, Simeon, and Anna.

â€¢ ••The Significance of Paul's Example••
	• Paul's example serves to demonstrate the power of God's mercy and the importance of humility.
	• It also highlights the distinction between the true people of God and those who only outwardly observe the law.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Preventing Grace in Paul's Conversion••
	• The preventing grace in Paul's conversion was the grace of regeneration.
	• This is the soul of our souls, which must be put into us before any inspiration of heavenly motions can take place.

â€¢ ••Regeneration and Inspiration••
	• Regeneration is the putting off of the old man and the putting on of the new.
	• Inspiration of good desires and motions comes after regeneration.

â€¢ ••Man's Will in Conversion••
	• The will of Paul was not an agent or cause in the effecting of his first conversion.
	• Scripture makes two sorts of conversion: passive (man is converted by God) and active (man converts himself to God).

â€¢ ••Cooperation of Man's Will with God's Grace••
	• Man's will is an instrument of God's grace in conversion.
	• God's grace is the principal agent, and man's will is the instrument.

â€¢ ••Violence or Coaction in Conversion••
	• There is a double violence or coaction in conversion.
	• One type of coaction abolishes all consent of will, and God did not use this type with Paul.
	• The other type of coaction draws out a consent from the will, and God used this type with Paul.

â€¢ ••Efficacy of Preventing Grace••
	• The efficacy of the preventing grace in Paul's conversion does not stand in the event (man's will applying itself to God's grace).
	• The efficacy of grace does not stand in the congruity or aptness of motions or heavenly persuasions.
	• The true answer is that outward means are effectual because they are joined with the inward operation of the Spirit.

â€¢ ••God's Operation in Conversion••
	• God adds to the first grace, the second grace, which gives the power to believe and repent.
	• God gives the will and the deed, and then faith and repentance must necessarily follow.

â€¢ ••Augustine's Statement••
	• Augustine said, "He that made you without you, does not save you without you."
	• This statement highlights the cooperation of man's will with God's grace in conversion.

â€¢ ••Papists' Opinions••
	• The Council of Trent and some papists incline to the opinion that the efficacy of grace stands in the event (man's will applying itself to God's grace).
	• Other papists place the efficacy of grace in the congruity or aptness of motions or heavenly persuasions.

â€¢ ••Key Principles••
	• Regeneration is the soul of our souls, which must be put into us before any inspiration of heavenly motions can take place.
	• The will of man is not an agent or cause in the effecting of his first conversion.
	• God's grace is the principal agent in conversion, and man's will is the instrument.

â€¢ ••The Efficacy of God's Grace••
	• God's grace is effective in working the will and deed of man (Phil. 2:13)
	• Man's will is apt to resist God's grace, but God's will determines and limits man's will

â€¢ ••The Power of God's Will••
	• God's will is perfect and cannot will evil
	• Man's will is an instrument to effect God's will, and the more it aligns with God's will, the greater its liberty

â€¢ ••True Liberty••
	• True liberty is the ability to will only what is good, freely and without compulsion
	• The ability to will evil or resist God's calling is not liberty, but impotence

â€¢ ••The Example of Paul••

â€¢ ••The Call to Learn Christ••
	• All men are called to learn Christ, as Paul learned Him
	• This involves a real and lively learning of Christ, not just intellectual understanding.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Preaching the Gospel••
	• The gospel is the primary doctrine to be preached
	• The law is not the main focus of preaching
	• The gospel is necessary for salvation

â€¢ ••What it Means to Preach Christ••
	• Preaching Christ involves four ministerial actions:
		- Teaching the doctrine of Christ's incarnation and offices
		- Teaching that faith is an instrument to apprehend and apply Christ's benefits
		- Certifying that God's will is to save each individual through Christ
		- Certifying that each individual should apply Christ to themselves through faith

â€¢ ••The Role of the Apostle Paul••

	• The division between Jews and Gentiles was abolished at the death of Christ

â€¢ ••The Obedience of Paul••
	• Paul's obedience to God's calling was a result of both his initial grace and special grace
	• God works the will and deed in every good work in the regenerate

â€¢ ••The Importance of Immediate Obedience••
	• Paul obeyed God immediately without deliberation or consultation
	• We should also obey God's call to amendment and newness of life without delay
	• Reasons for not delaying conversion include:
		- The uncertainty of our life and the last judgment
		- Adding sin to sin and treasuring up wrath against the day of wrath
		- Growing in perfection in sin and bringing forth death

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Delayed Repentance••
	• Delayed repentance is seldom or never true repentance
	• When men are dying, their sins forsake them, and they do not commonly forsake their sins

â€¢ ••The Call of God in England••
	• God has called the people of England for over 40 years
	• Many have not listened to the call of God and have deferred to obey

â€¢ ••Conclusion••

â€¢ ••Pilgrimage••
	• Pilgrimage to Jerusalem was a common practice in the Old Testament

		- It is made a part of God's worship, which is not allowed in the New Testament

â€¢ ••Primacy of Peter••

â€¢ ••Paul's Authority••
