	• He did not receive his authority from any other apostle or human source

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Church in Jerusalem••
	• The apostles at Jerusalem were guided by James and Peter.
	• Jerusalem is considered the mother church of the world, as the gospel was first preached there.

â€¢ ••James, the Lord's Brother••
	• There are three questions about James being the Lord's brother:
		- Which James was it?
		- How could James be the Lord's brother?
		- What benefit did James gain from being the Lord's brother?
	• James was the son of Alphaeus, not Zebedee.
	• James was the Lord's cousin-german, not his brother by birth.
	• Being the Lord's brother does not make James a better apostle or man.

â€¢ ••The Perpetual Virginity of Mary••
	• Helvidius argued that Mary had more sons besides Christ.
	• This argument is refuted by the fact that James was the cousin-german of Christ.

â€¢ ••Paul's Defense••
	• Paul defends himself against accusations of lying.
	• He says that he speaks the truth before God.

â€¢ ••What is a Lie?••
	• A lie is when we speak the contrary to what we think with an intention to deceive.
	• A lie has four components:
		- Speaking a falsehood
		- Speaking against knowledge and conscience
		- A mind or intention to deceive or hurt
		- Speaking without reasonable cause

â€¢ ••Types of Untruths••
	• A man may speak a falsehood without lying, if he thinks it to be true.
	• A man may speak the truth and yet lie, if he thinks it to be false.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Lying••
	• A lie is a sin, as it involves speaking against knowledge and conscience.
	• Boasters and flatterers are considered liars, as they speak without reasonable cause.

â€¢ ••Paul's Testimony••
	• Paul says that he speaks the truth before God.
	• He is not lying, and his words are confirmed by a divine testimony.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Truth••
	• Speaking the truth is essential, as it involves speaking against knowledge and conscience.
	• Lying is a sin, and it can lead to harm and deception.

â€¢ ••Paul's Integrity••
	• Paul is committed to speaking the truth, even if it means defending himself against accusations.
	• He is willing to stand before God and confirm the truth of his words.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Distinguishing between a lie and other forms of speech••
	• A lie is distinct from a parable or figurative speech
	• A parable is a feigned or supposed truth that signifies an unfeigned truth
• ••Distinguishing between a lie and concealment••
	• Concealment is not the same as lying
	• Concealment can be lawful if there is a reasonable cause and it is not necessary to reveal the truth
• ••Distinguishing between lying and feigning••
	• Feigning is not the same as lying
	• Feigning involves speaking something that is beside or diverse from the truth, but not contrary to it
• ••Types of feigning••
	• Feigning can be done for a convenient and reasonable cause
	• Examples of feigning include:
		- Moses' prayer for the Israelites (Ex. 32:10)
		- Joshua's feigned flight to draw enemies out of the city (Josh. 8:5)
		- Physicians deceiving patients for their good
		- Parents deceiving children to keep them safe
• ••The sinfulness of lying••
	• Lying is a sin, as stated in Ephesians 4:25
	• The devil is the author of all lies (John 8)
• ••Objections to the sinfulness of lying••
	• Some argue that lying is not against charity if it is done for the good of men
	• However, lying is still to the hurt and prejudice of truth
	• Those who are deceived by lies are also hurt
	• The person who tells a lie also hurts themselves
• ••Examples of lying in the Bible••
	• The Egyptian midwives and Rahab are commended for their lies (Ex. 1:19; Josh. 2:5)
	• However, this does not justify lying in general
• ••Distinguishing between the work and the execution••
	• The work done by the midwives and Rahab was good, but the execution was through lying
• ••The importance of truthfulness••
	• Truthfulness is essential in speech and action
	• Lying can lead to hurt and prejudice, even if it is done for good intentions

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Importance of Truth••
	• Lying is not approved, even if done for good intentions

â€¢ ••Reasons to Practice Truth••
	• It is God's commandment (James 3:14)

â€¢ ••Consequences of Lying••
	• Destruction is the liar's reward (Ps. 5:6)
	• Liars will have their portion in the lake that burns with fire and brimstone (Rev. 21:8)

â€¢ ••The Form of an Oath••
	• An oath consists of four parts: asseveration, confession, invocation, and imprecation
	• The form of an oath is a certain form of words that expresses some of these parts, while concealing the rest

â€¢ ••Examples of Oaths••
	• "The Lord liveth" (Jer. 4:2) is a form of oath that expresses confession
	• "I call God to witness to my soul" (2 Cor. 1:23) is a form of oath that expresses invocation
	• "The Lord do thus and thus unto me" (Ruth 2:17) is a form of oath that expresses imprecation

â€¢ ••The Commandment Against Swearing••
	• The form of an oath should be plain and direct in the name of God, not indirect or oblique
	• It is the flat commandment of God (Matt. 5:34)

â€¢ ••Paul's Use of Oaths••
	• Paul's words "by my rejoicing" (1 Cor. 15:31) are not an oath, but an obtestation
	• It is not unlawful to name creatures in the form of an oath, if they are considered as pledges presented unto God

â€¢ ••Common Swearing••
	• It is wrong to swear by creatures, such as faith, troth, the Mass, or Mary
	• We should use an oath only in cases of extremity, when a necessary truth is to be confirmed

â€¢ ••The Purpose of an Oath••
	• An oath is used to confirm a necessary truth, and to invoke God's presence and judgment

â€¢ ••The Importance of Using Oaths Wisely••
	• We should use oaths only in cases of extremity, and not as a common practice
	• We should be careful not to swear by creatures, but rather by God's name.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Use of Oaths••
	• Paul uses oaths sparingly, only when necessary

â€¢ ••Preparation Before Swearing••

â€¢ ••The Significance of Swearing••

â€¢ ••The Nature of Faith••
	• Faith consists of three degrees: knowledge, assent, and apprehension.

â€¢ ••The Loss of Faith••

	• The third degree of faith, which is the faith of the elect, is more secure but can still be wasted.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Boldness••

â€¢ ••God's Faith is Unshakeable••

â€¢ ••Pardon of Offense••
	• There are four degrees of pardon: decree, promise, procurement, and donation
	• The donation of pardon requires a hand to give (God's word and sacraments) and a hand to receive (faith)

â€¢ ••Necessity of Pardon••
	• Pardon is necessary for believers, even if sins are already pardoned in decree, promise, and procurement
	• Pardon is given to believers through the word and sacraments

â€¢ ••Conditional and Absolute Pardon••
	• Conditional pardon is given upon condition of future repentance (e.g. baptism)
	• Absolute pardon is given when a believer repents or renews their repentance

â€¢ ••Pardon in the Child of God••
	• The child of God has pardon of their fall in respect of decree, promise, procurement, and conditional donation
	• They may lack absolute pardon until they renew their repentance

â€¢ ••Prayer for Pardon••
	• The child of God prays for two things: continued favor and absolute pardon
	• They pray for continued favor every day, and for absolute pardon when they renew their repentance

â€¢ ••Degrees of Pardon••
	• Pardon is given in degrees: decree, promise, procurement, and donation
	• Each degree builds on the previous one, with the donation being the final and most important stage

â€¢ ••Importance of Faith and Repentance••
	• Faith is essential for receiving pardon and maintaining a relationship with God
	• Repentance is necessary for receiving absolute pardon and renewing one's relationship with God

	• We must be content with some uncertainty in order to understand the substance and doctrine of history

	• Paul's journey to Jerusalem was to establish his doctrine and receive the apostles' consent
	• This was a crucial moment in the development of the early Christian church.

â€¢ ••The Unity of Humanity••
	• All people are children of one Father and part of one family

â€¢ ••The Cause of the Journey••

		- Travelers who go for vain curiosity and bring back bad habits
		- Beggars and rogues who live off the sweat of others

â€¢ ••The Importance of Conferences••
	• Conferences are laudable and to be maintained, especially when they promote unity and consent in doctrine
	• The papists blame Protestants for condemning conferences, but this is a misunderstanding
	• Protestant churches allow Christian councils, but with certain caveats:
		- The council should be gathered by Christian princes
		- The pope should not be a judge, but a party
		- Christ's Word should be the judge, and delegates should determine things by the written Word

â€¢ ••The Manner of Conferencing••
	• Paul communicated with the apostles in a private, plain, and familiar manner
	• There was no assembly or disputation, only Paul declaring his doctrine and the apostles giving assent
	• Paul did not submit the truth of his doctrine to trial, but rather sought the approval of the apostles

â€¢ ••The Matter of the Conference••
	• The conference was about the gospel which Paul preached
	• The papists gather that the church is the judge in all questions of religion, but this is false
	• The sovereign Judge of all questions and controversies in religion is Christ alone

â€¢ ••The Power of the Church••
	• The power to determine questions and controversies in religion is not with the church, but with Christ
	• The church has no authority to judge or determine the truth of doctrine

â€¢ ••The Role of the Pope••
	• The pope should not be a judge, but a party in a council
	• The pope's faith should not be pinned on the sleeve of another

â€¢ ••The Importance of the Written Word••
	• The written Word of Christ should be the judge in all questions and controversies in religion
	• Delegates in a council should determine things by the written Word, not by their own opinions or traditions

â€¢ ••The Purpose of a General Council••
	• A General Council should be for the trial of truth and the staying of unsettled minds
	• The council should be gathered by Christian princes, and the pope should not be a judge

â€¢ ••The Rejection of the Council of Trent••
	• The Council of Trent was rejected and condemned by Protestants
	• The council was unfair, with the pope as both party and judge, and no liberty to make trial of truth.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Role of Scripture in Faith and Good Life••
	• The written Word is the principal voice of the Judge and the definitive sentence.
	• The church's office is to gather, declare, testify, and pronounce this sentence.

â€¢ ••The Church's Relationship with Scripture••
	• The church was before the writing of the Word, but not before the Word itself.
	• Faith presupposes a word of God, which is the Scripture.

â€¢ ••The Church's Authority••
	• The church is not the ultimate judge, but rather a gathering of believers who declare and testify to the Scripture.
	• The church's judgment must be based on the written Word.

â€¢ ••The Papists' Objections••
	• The papists argue that the church is the ultimate authority and that the Scripture must be approved by the church.
	• The author responds that the Scripture has its own evidence and authority.

â€¢ ••The Honor Due to Excellent Men••
	• The author agrees that excellent men, such as Peter and James, deserve honor and reverence.
	• This does not imply that the church is the ultimate authority.

â€¢ ••The Papists' Misinterpretation of Paul's Example••
	• The papists argue that Paul's example of going to Rome to have his doctrine tried and examined implies that the church is the ultimate authority.
	• The author responds that Paul's example is not about going to Rome, but about being tried by the writings of the apostles.

â€¢ ••The Commandment to Go to the Law and the Testimony••
	• In doubtful cases, the commandment is to go to the law and the testimony (Isaiah 8).
	• This implies that the Scripture is the ultimate authority.

â€¢ ••The Warning Against Going to Rome••
	• The author quotes Revelation 18, "Come out of Babylon, my people."
	• This implies that going to Rome is not a good idea.

â€¢ ••The Succession of Peter••
	• The author argues that the bishop of Rome is not Peter's successor in teaching, but rather in denying Christ.
	• The papists' claim of succession is based on human history and faith.

â€¢ ••The End of the Conference••
	• The end of the conference was to stay a false report that Paul's doctrine was contrary to the other apostles.
	• The author wants to ensure that his preaching is not in vain and that the ministry of Paul has passage and profit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Circumcision as a free ceremony••
	• Circumcision was not a sacrament or a necessary part of God's worship
	• It was used as a free ceremony to edify men
	• Paul condescended to circumcise Timothy to avoid offending believing Jews

â€¢ ••Things indifferent••
	• Things are called indifferent because they are neither good nor evil in themselves
	• They can be used well or ill, and their use is not necessarily sinful

â€¢ ••Restraining the use of things indifferent••
	• The law of charity restrains the use of things indifferent to avoid scandal
	• The laws of men, if they tend to the common good, can also restrain the use of things indifferent

â€¢ ••The law of charity••
	• Things indifferent can cease to be indifferent if they cause scandal
	• Paul refused to circumcise Titus to avoid offending the godly and hurting Christian liberty

â€¢ ••Human laws and things indifferent••
	• Human laws do not change the nature of things indifferent
	• They can only temper and moderate the use of things indifferent

â€¢ ••Caution 1: Human laws do not change the nature of things indifferent••
	• God's will can make a thing good or evil, regardless of human laws
	• Human laws cannot reverse the liberty granted by a sovereign power

â€¢ ••Caution 2: Human laws are only temporary••
	• When the end of a law ceases, and there is no contempt of authority, a thing indifferent can be used freely

â€¢ ••Things indifferent and salvation••
	• A thing indifferent cannot be made necessary for salvation
	• The popish religion makes the observance of things indifferent necessary for salvation

â€¢ ••Making a difference of persons••

â€¢ ••God's mercy and condescension••
	• God is merciful and condescending to those who are weak
	• God will not quench the spirit of those who are seeking to please Him

â€¢ ••Paul's defense of his ministry••

â€¢ ••God's acceptance of people••
	• God accepts people based on their present condition, not their past or outward appearance
	• This is illustrated by the example of Job, who was a righteous man despite his outward circumstances

â€¢ ••God's sovereignty••
	• God is a sovereign and absolute Lord over all His creations
	• He is not bound by the same rules as humans, and therefore can deal with people unequally if He chooses to do so

â€¢ ••The concept of "person"••
	• In this context, "person" refers to the outward quality or condition of a person, such as their country, sex, birth, etc.
	• God does not accept people based on their outward appearance or condition, but rather on His own wise and just pleasure

â€¢ ••God's judgment••
	• God judges people based on their hearts and actions, not their outward appearance or condition
	• This is illustrated by the example of Job, who was a righteous man despite his outward circumstances

â€¢ ••The importance of repentance••
	• When we repent, God accepts us based on our present condition, not our past
	• We should not be judged based on our past, but rather on our present and future actions.

â€¢ ••The Efficacy of the Ministry••
	• The ministry is not the source of regeneration, but rather an external instrument.
	• The proper effect of the ministry is to declare or signify the will of God.

â€¢ ••Cautions about the Ministry••
	• The ministry does not include the power to regenerate.
	• Grace is not inseparably annexed to the word preached.
	• The preaching of the word is an external instrument of faith and regeneration.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sacraments••
	• Sacraments do not confer grace ex opera operato, by the work done.
	• The word and sacraments are of one nature, with sacraments being a visible word.

â€¢ ••The Profession of Christ••
	• Requires us to be willing to part with the dearest things in the world for the name of Christ

	• This is a way to life, as appointed by God

â€¢ ••Who Must Satisfy and Restore?••
	• He who is the cause of any wrong or loss to others
	• All those who are accessory to the wrong, including those who command, consent, or receive the wrong

â€¢ ••To Whom Must Restitution Be Made?••
	• To the person wronged, if they are alive and known
	• To their heirs, if they are dead
	• To the poor, if the person wronged is unknown or dead

â€¢ ••Restitution in Unknown Cases••
	• If the person wronged is not known, restitution is to be made to the church or commonwealth
	• Restitution is to be turned into alms for the poor

â€¢ ••Restitution in Cases of Unlawful Transactions••
	• If a transaction is unlawful, such as bribery or simony, restitution is not to be made to the giver
	• Restitution is to be applied to common use, especially for the poor

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••What must be restored?••
	• Things unjustly received or detained, known or unknown
	• If known, restore in kind or value (Ex. 21:19)
	• If unknown, restore according to wise men's judgment
• ••How to restore?••
	• Show a ready and willing mind through confession and pardon
	• If unable to restore, show willingness to do so
• ••When to restore?••
	• Presently satisfy in terms of preparation of mind
	• Execution may be deferred due to ignorance, need, or danger
• ••Order and manner of restoration••
	• Restore certain things first (e.g. bought goods, deposits)
	• Restore uncertain things in part, and seek pardon for the rest
• ••Importance of restitution••
	• Use wariness to avoid damaging one's good name
	• Follow Paul's example of diligence in providing for the poor
• ••Reasons to provide for the poor••
	• The poor represent the person of Christ (Matt. 25:31-46)
	• The poor have a title to part of our goods, as stewards of God
	• Mercy in us is a pledge of God's mercy towards us
• ••Our duty••
	• Provide for the poor in our places and callings
	• Seal relief to the poor, as Paul did (Rom. 15:25-28)
• ••Our fault••
	• We are backward and slack in providing for the poor
	• The cause is not giving ourselves fully to Christ
	• We live without a law, neglecting God's statutes
• ••Consequences of neglecting the poor••
	• We become thieves in respect of the goods we possess
	• We miss out on the enriching benefits of observing God's commandments
• ••Call to action••
	• Make haste to keep God's commandments, especially the commandment of relief
	• Follow Paul's example of diligence in providing for the poor

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Warning••
	• The importance of not just hearing, but also doing and practicing what we hear
	• This is the key to building on the rock (Matthew 7:24-27)
	• A common fault is to hear much and do little (Ezekiel 33:32)

â€¢ ••Paul's Confrontation with Peter••
	• Paul confronted Peter to his face at Antioch
	• Peter was to be blamed for his actions
	• Paul's intention was to do his office and uphold the kingdom of God

â€¢ ••Who Was Resisted?••
	• Peter, the apostle, was resisted by Paul
	• This was not a private matter, but a public confrontation

â€¢ ••Who Resisted?••
	• Paul resisted Peter in truth and good earnest
	• He had a weighty and urgent cause for his reproof

â€¢ ••Paul's Mind and Meaning••
	• Paul's intention was to do his office and uphold the kingdom of God
	• He wanted to ensure that the word of God had free passage and was glorified

â€¢ ••Resisting Evil••
	• We must resist evil to the uttermost of our power (Romans 12:9)
	• We must have no fellowship with the unfruitful works of darkness (Ephesians 5:11)

â€¢ ••What Must We Resist?••
	• Principalities, powers, and spiritual wickedness (Ephesians 6:12)
	• The devil and his angels, who seek to hinder the kingdom of God

â€¢ ••How Must We Resist?••
	• In heavenly things, such as the salvation of our soul and the worship of God
	• We must resist sin, even to the shedding of our blood (Hebrews 12:4)

â€¢ ••The Importance of Boldness and Liberty••
	• We must be bold and free in reproving sin
	• This was a common duty of prophets and apostles (Isaiah 58:1)

â€¢ ••The Example of Paul••
	• Paul's confrontation with Peter is an example of true virtue
	• We must follow his example and resist evil in ourselves and others

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Ministers should use liberty in reproving••
	• This liberty is not a result of boldness, but of God's Spirit (Mic. 3:8)
	• It should be used with a sound mind, to give a good account of reproofs (2 Tim. 1:7)
	• Reproof should be seasoned with love, for the good and salvation of those reproved (Isa. 50:7)

â€¢ ••Paul's example of reproving Peter••
	• Paul reproved Peter to his face, unlike the common practice of backbiting and whispering
	• This shows Paul's ingenuous and honest mind, and his willingness to correct others

â€¢ ••Patience and humility in receiving reproof••
	• Peter's response to Paul's reproof shows patience and humility
	• David also shows humility in saying "Let the righteous smite me" (Ps. 141:5)

â€¢ ••Even excellent men can err••
	• The chief apostles, including Peter, are subject to error and deception
	• This is because they are human, and can be influenced by their own minds, wills, and affections

â€¢ ••Guidance of the Spirit in ministry••
	• When delivering messages to the church, the apostles were guided by the infallible assistance of the Spirit
	• This means they could not err in their writings or sermons, but may have erred in other areas of life

â€¢ ••Examples of apostolic error••
	• Jonah was impotent in his anger after seeing Nineveh was not destroyed
	• Nathan was deceived in giving advice to David about the temple
	• The apostles at the ascension of Christ still dreamed of an earthly kingdom

â€¢ ••Peter's error and its implications••
	• Peter's error was not just in his actions, but also in his mind and will
	• His bad example could have led to the ruin of doctrine, if it had not been prevented

â€¢ ••The error of the bishops of Rome••
	• They cannot be free from error, just like Peter
	• Their error may be in doctrine, as well as in action

â€¢ ••The importance of watching and praying••
	• We should watch and pray, lest we be led into temptation
	• Even excellent men are subject to falling, and we should not abuse their example to justify our own sin

â€¢ ••The need for humility and correction••
	• We should be willing to receive correction and reproof, like Peter
	• We should not use Peter's example to bolster ourselves in our naughty ways, but rather to learn from his mistakes.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• The Bible teaches that all men are sinners (Proverbs 24:16)
	• The just man falls seven times a day, but this refers to affliction, not sin
	• The Bible also teaches that the just man can repent and convert

â€¢ ••Peter's Sin••
	• Peter's sin was simulation (dissembling or pretending to be something he was not)
	• This was a form of unlawful simulation, which is feigning something against the truth or to the prejudice of others

â€¢ ••The Cause of Peter's Sin••
	• Peter's sin was caused by his fear of the Jews from Jerusalem
	• He was afraid of what they would think of him, so he separated himself from the Gentiles

â€¢ ••The Matter of Peter's Sin••
	• Peter used Christian liberty to eat with the Gentiles, but then separated himself from them
	• This was a form of hypocrisy, where he acted one way with the Gentiles and another way with the Jews

â€¢ ••The Effects of Peter's Sin••
	• Peter's sin caused offense to the Gentiles and undermined the gospel
	• It also showed his weakness and tendency to fall away from his profession

â€¢ ••The Nature of Human Weakness••
	• Peter's weakness is a reminder that we are all prone to fall away from our profession
	• We may be tempted to return to our old ways if we are not careful

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith and Obedience••
	• Those who withdraw themselves from their faith, profession, and obedience will perish (Hebrews 10:38; Psalm 73:27)
	• We must always remember to stay true to our faith and profession

â€¢ ••The Struggle between Flesh and Spirit••
	• The regenerate man is partly flesh and partly spirit
	• This means that we are prone to sin and must struggle to do what is good

â€¢ ••The Example of Abraham and Sarah••
	• Abraham and Sarah's desire for a child was a desire from the Spirit
	• This shows that even the most spiritual people can struggle with sin and weakness

â€¢ ••The Importance of Remembering Our Profession••
	• We must always remember our profession and stay true to it
	• This will help us to avoid falling away from our faith and profession.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Imperfection of Good Works••
	• Even the best works are imperfect and mixed with corruption.
	• We must humble ourselves and seek pardon for our defects.

â€¢ ••Peter's Sin••
	• Peter's act of separating himself from the Gentiles was not inherently sinful.
	• However, the circumstances made it a sin, including:
		- Withdrawing from the company of Gentiles and keeping company with Jews.
		- Abstaining from certain meats in a way that offended the Gentiles.
		- Using this abstinence to avoid offending some Jews, while offending all the Gentiles.
		- Tending to the overthrowing of Paul's ministry and the suppressing of the truth of the gospel.

â€¢ ••What Peter Should Have Done••
	• He should have openly withstood the Jews who came from Jerusalem.
	• Alternatively, he should have advertised the Gentiles that he was yielding to the infirmity of some Jews.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Not Offending God••
	• We must not offend God, even if all the world is offended.
	• Less offenses must give place when the great offense is at hand, i.e., when God is dishonored.

â€¢ ••The Cause of Peter's Sin••
	• The cause of Peter's sin was the fear of the offense of the Jews.
	• This fear was a sin because:
		- It was without cause, as Peter was not justified in fearing the offense of the Jews.
		- It was excessive, as Peter neglected his duty to God.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Fear••
	• There are three kinds of fear:
		- Fear without all sin (e.g., Adam and Christ).
		- Fear that is altogether sinful (e.g., the wicked and ungodly).
		- Mixed fear (e.g., regenerate individuals who fear God and man).

â€¢ ••Peter's Fear••
	• Peter's fear was a mixed fear, in which the corrupt fear of man prevailed over the true fear of God.
	• This fear was a sin, as it was excessive and without cause.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Fearing God••
	• We must inure ourselves to fear God above all things.
	• We must fear God simply because He is Lord of body and soul, and can destroy both.

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Fear and Faith••
	• Fear that is severed from faith and obedience is sinful.
	• Fear that is joined with faith and obedience is a mixed fear, which can be a sin if it is excessive or without cause.

â€¢ ••The Need for Humility and Pardon••
	• We must humble ourselves and seek pardon for our defects, even in our good works.
	• We must recognize that our best works are imperfect and mixed with corruption.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Peter's Sin••
	• Was a sin of infirmity, not malice
	• Committed due to being overpowered by temptation or strong emotions (fear, anger, lust)
	• Incident to regenerate individuals, like Peter

â€¢ ••Sin of Infirmity••
	• Characterized by a purpose not to sin, but the will is overpowered
	• Examples: fornication, drunkenness, etc.
	• Not a genuine desire to sin, but a lack of self-control

â€¢ ••Effect of Peter's Sin••
	• Drew others to similar dissimulation (hypocrisy)
	• Demonstrates the contagion of evil example
	• Ministers must join good doctrine with good example

â€¢ ••Ministers' Responsibility••
	• Must be patterns of good life (1 Peter 5:3, 1 Tim. 4:12, Phil. 4:9, Matt. 5:16)
	• Practice is part of teaching
	• Lives must be virtuous and godly to receive God's preference and protection

â€¢ ••Consequences of Wicked Lives••
	• Ministers of wicked lives face fearful judgments of God
	• Destruction befell the sons of Eli and Aaron due to their lewd example

â€¢ ••Superiors' Example••
	• Must go before their inferiors by good example
	• Moses' bad example in his family was nearly punished by God
	• David's evil example led to punishment and judgment against sin

â€¢ ••Consent of Many is Not Truth••
	• Peter, Barnabas, and the Jews were deceived, while Paul had the truth
	• A single layman with Scripture is preferred over a whole council

â€¢ ••Paul's Reproof of Peter••
	• Occurred as soon as Paul saw Peter's offense
	• Demonstrates the importance of resisting and cutting off the first beginnings of temptation and sin

â€¢ ••Foundation of Reproof••
	• Based on certain knowledge of Peter's offense
	• Warns against censuring and condemning others on suspicions or hearsay

â€¢ ••Importance of Certainty in Reproof••
	• Public persons, like magistrates and ministers, should not be condemned without certain knowledge of their fault

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Accusations against others••
	• Must be proven by two or three witnesses (1 Tim. 5:19)
	• Not to be received without sufficient evidence

â€¢ ••Walking with a right foot••
	• Refers to living a life that is consistent with the truth of the gospel
	• Involves ascribing all good to God's grace and mercy through Christ

â€¢ ••Condemning those who walk contrary to the gospel••
	• Papists who join faith and works in justification and salvation
	• Carnal Protestants who separate justification and sanctification, faith and good life

â€¢ ••The sin of walking unevenly••
	• A common sin of our days
	• Can lead to the judgment of God and the banishment of the gospel

â€¢ ••Performing the duty of walking with a right foot••
	• Requires a right heart (humble and honest)
	• Must be accompanied by making straight steps (obeying God's commandments)

â€¢ ••Characteristics of a right heart••
	• Humble: abasing oneself under all creatures and exalting Christ
	• Honest: carrying a settled purpose of not sinning

â€¢ ••Making straight steps••
	• Involves obeying God's commandments in all aspects of life
	• Requires effort and intentionality

â€¢ ••The importance of reproof••
	• Those who sin openly must be reproved before all men (1 Tim. 5:20)

â€¢ ••Reasons for restraining sin••
	• Paul's example of restraining Peter's sin (Gal. 2:11-14)
	• Involves setting a good example and not causing others to stumble

â€¢ ••The consequences of not walking with a right foot••
	• Can lead to being bound hand and foot and cast into darkness (Matt. 22:13)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Importance of Example••
	• The observation of ceremonial law is necessary
	• A Jew's example should not compel Gentiles to Judaize
	• Superiors, magistrates, and ministers should be mindful of their examples

â€¢ ••The Power of the Ministry••
	• The apostles' actions compelled men to do certain things
	• Their doctrines and exhortations had a greater impact
	• The fault of our times is lacking the power of godliness

â€¢ ••Peter's Sin••
	• Peter constrained men to observe the ceremonial law
	• This was a mistake, as the gospel has made us free from such law
	• The Roman religion places unnecessary burdens on its followers

â€¢ ••The Vows of Perfection••
	• Vows of perpetual continence, poverty, and obedience are not states of perfection
	• They are, in fact, estates of abomination

â€¢ ••Justification by Faith••
	• A man is not justified by the works of the law, but by the faith of Jesus Christ
	• Paul defends justification by faith in both judgment and practice

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Jews and Gentiles••
	• The distinction is due to the good will and pleasure of God
	• God chose the Israelites above all nations

â€¢ ••The Cause of the Distinction••
	• God's election of the Israelites was based on His love for their fathers
	• This election is a free choice, not based on human merit

â€¢ ••The Refusal of Christ••
	• There cannot be a refusal of Christ where the Messiah is concerned
	• The distinction between Jews and Gentiles is not based on human choice

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith in Jesus Christ is the means of justification
	• The works of the law are not sufficient for justification

â€¢ ••The Testimony of Scripture••
	• The psalm "By the works of the law no flesh shall be justified" confirms Paul's argument
	• This testimony is amplified by the context of the psalm.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Difference between Jews and Gentiles••
	• The Jews were a holy and peculiar people, opposed to sinners of the Gentiles.
	• The distinction lies in the fact that the Jews were in the covenant, while the Gentiles were out of the covenant.

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Difference••
	• The difference was not based on earthly things, such as the law, which was given to the Israelites.
	• The law was a schoolmaster to Christ and an introduction to a better hope.

â€¢ ••The Duration of the Difference••
	• The difference between Jews and Gentiles endured until the death of Christ.
	• The disciples were forbidden to go into the way of the Gentiles, and Christ said He was not sent to the Gentiles.

â€¢ ••The Abolishment of the Difference••
	• Christ's death fully merited the abolishment of the difference between Jews and Gentiles.
	• The execution of this abolishment was by degrees, begun by the ministry of the apostles.

â€¢ ••The Holiness of the Jews••
	• The Jews are a holy people by nature, not because holiness is conveyed to them by generation.
	• They are holy from their beginning and birth, by virtue of the covenant.

â€¢ ••The Covenant and Posterity••
	• If the root (the parent) is holy, the branches (the children) are holy.
	• The parent's faith makes their posterity partaker of the covenant.

â€¢ ••The Double Person of the Parent••
	• The parent sustains a double person: as a child of Adam and as a believer.
	• As a believer, the parent's child is in the covenant and holy in the judgment of charity.

â€¢ ••The Double Person of the Child••
	• The child sustains two persons: as a child of the first Adam and as a child of believing parents.
	• As a child of believing parents, the child is in the covenant and holy, with original sin covered.

â€¢ ••The Use of the Covenant••
	• There was no absolute necessity of circumcision, as children born before the eighth day were born holy.
	• Baptism is not of absolute necessity, as children of believers are born holy and Christian.

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Baptism••
	• Baptism is not to confer the first grace, but to confirm and seal it.
	• Adoption and life begin before baptism, as children of believers are born holy and Christian.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Justification••
	• The word "justify" comes from courts of judgment and means to absolve or pronounce innocent
	• It can also mean to give testimony or declare and approve

â€¢ ••Distinguishing Justification from Regeneration and Renovation••
	• Regeneration is the change of the inward man, making us born anew
	• Renovation is the change of both the inward and outward man, heart and life
	• Justification is a certain action in God that acquits us of sins and accepts us to life everlasting

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome's Mistake••
	• The Church of Rome misunderstands justification as a physical transmutation of the heart from evil to good
	• This mistake mixes the law and gospel

â€¢ ••The Disposition of the Party Justified••
	• A person must come before God's judgment seat and plead guilty, condemning themselves
	• They must then flee to the throne of grace for pardon in Christ

â€¢ ••The Subject of Justification••
	• The person to be justified is man generally, signifying that "a man is justified"
	• This is because all men need justification, even those who are regenerate

â€¢ ••The Need for Justification••
	• All men, including the regenerate, need justification (Rom. 3:23)
	• The profane and secure Epicures never consider justification

â€¢ ••God's Communication of Justification••
	• God communicates the benefit of justification to all sorts of men through the ministry of the word
	• He beseeches men to be reconciled to God (2 Cor. 5:20)

â€¢ ••Inducement to Come to Christ••
	• We should come to Christ, humbling and judging ourselves, to be justified
	• God uses reasons to move us to practice this duty

â€¢ ••God's Gracious Preservations••
	• God preserves us graciously and continually, using reasons to move us to practice justification
	• This is an inducement for us to come to Christ

â€¢ ••The Publican's Example••
	• The publican came before God, acknowledging his sins and pleading for mercy (Luke 18)
	• He departed justified, showing the process of justification

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Day of Visitation••
	• The author encourages readers to take advantage of the time to seek God's kingdom and righteousness.
	• Neglecting this opportunity will result in the woe pronounced upon Chorazin and Bethsaida.

â€¢ ••Excluded from Justification••
	• The author argues that the works of the law are excluded from justification.
	• This includes both ceremonial and moral law.

â€¢ ••Works of the Law••
	• Ceremonial actions are of no use without moral duties of love and mercy.
	• Paul's discourse against justification by works is not limited to ceremonial law.

â€¢ ••Works Before and After Faith••
	• Works done before faith are excluded from justification.
	• Works done after faith, even in the estate of grace, are also excluded.

â€¢ ••Scriptural Examples••
	• David, Abraham, and Paul are cited as examples of individuals who were not justified by works.
	• Even the best works, such as those of faith, are not the cause of justification.

â€¢ ••Cooperation of Faith and Works••
	• Faith and works cooperate in the manifestation of truth and sincerity.
	• This cooperation is not in the act of justification, but in the declaration and approbation of faith.

â€¢ ••Papist Doctrine••
	• The author warns against the papist doctrine of justification by the works of the law.
	• This doctrine is considered pestilent and damnable.

â€¢ ••The Meritorious Cause of Justification••
	• Christ is the meritorious cause of our justification.
	• This is based on His obedience, including His passion and fulfilling of the law.

â€¢ ••Christ's Obedience••
	• Christ's obedience is the thing in Him by and for which we are justified.
	• This obedience includes His passion, fulfilling of the law, and duties of love to God and man.

â€¢ ••Christ as Our Surety••
	• Christ has become our surety for the double debt we owe to God.
	• This debt includes fulfilling the law and satisfying for the breach of the law.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••When does Christ's obedience begin and end?••
	• Begins in His incarnation
	• Ends in His death
	• Christ says "It is finished" in John 19:30

â€¢ ••What is the significance of Christ's death?••
	• Christ made a perfect satisfaction for us to the justice of God
	• By His death, we receive the promise of everlasting inheritance (Heb. 9:15)
	• He perfected those who are sanctified (Heb. 10:14)

â€¢ ••What is the purpose of Christ's resurrection and ascension?••
	• To apply and communicate the benefits Christ procured for us
	• To put us in possession of the benefits purchased by His death
	• To give gifts to men and to fill all things (Eph. 4:8, 10)

â€¢ ••How can Christ obey as both God and man?••
	• Christ must be considered as God-man or man-God
	• The Godhead and manhood work together in His obedience
	• The operations of both natures make the compound work of a mediator

â€¢ ••What is the nature of Christ's obedience?••
	• Obedience is a subjection of the will in reasonable creatures to the will of God
	• Christ yields subjection only in respect of the will of the manhood

â€¢ ••How does the Godhead contribute to Christ's obedience?••
	• The operation of the Godhead makes the obedience meritorious and satisfactory
	• God was in Christ reconciling the world unto Himself (2 Cor. 5:19)

â€¢ ••What is the value of Christ's obedience?••
	• The obedience of Christ has value, price, and dignity due to the operation of the Godhead

â€¢ ••How is Christ's obedience made ours?••
	• By the free donation of God
	• Christ is really given unto us in the word and sacraments

â€¢ ••What is the significance of Christ's gift to us?••
	• The obedience of Christ is made ours through His gift
	• We receive the benefits of Christ's obedience through faith

â€¢ ••What is the relationship between Christ's obedience and our justification?••
	• Christ's obedience serves to justify us, but in a different way than His death
	• His resurrection and ascension apply and communicate the benefits of His obedience to us

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The obedience of Christ as our justice••
	• It is not our justice in a natural manner, but rather by God's acceptance
	• God accepts Christ's obedience as our own, making it ours indeed

â€¢ ••Two errors to avoid••
	• ••Error 1: Justification by Christ's passion alone••
		- This would mean we are justified without fulfilling the law
		- We owe a double debt to God: fulfilling the law and satisfying the breach of the law
	• ••Error 2: Papist view of justification••
		- They teach that remission of sins and inherent justice are the same thing
		- This is incorrect, as inherent justice and justification are distinct gifts of God

â€¢ ••Objections to the obedience of Christ••
	• ••Objection 1: Christ fulfilled the law for Himself••
		- Christ fulfilled the law as a holy high priest, but as mediator, He became subject to the law
		- He did not fulfill the law for Himself, but for us
	• ••Objection 2: We are not bound to fulfill the law••
		- Christ fulfilled the law in a way of redemption and satisfaction, not in the same manner as us
		- We fulfill the law in a way of thankfulness for our redemption

â€¢ ••The law and its demands••
	• ••The law in the estate of innocence••
		- The law threatened punishment, but only exacted obedience
	• ••The law after the fall••
		- The law exacts both obedience and punishment

â€¢ ••The role of the passion of Christ••
	• ••The passion as a payment••
		- The passion of Christ is a payment for the second debt (satisfaction for the breach of the law)
	• ••The passion as a means of deliverance••
		- The passion of Christ procures deliverance from hell, but alone does not purchase a right to eternal life

â€¢ ••The relationship between the passion and obedience••
	• ••The passion as a kind of action••
		- Christ's passion is a kind of action, and He obeyed and suffered in His passion
	• ••The passion and prayers••
		- Christ's passion is not separate from His active and voluntary obedience

â€¢ ••The nature of justification••
	• ••Justification as revealed without the law••
		- The justice whereby a sinner is justified is revealed without the law
	• ••Inherent justice and justification••
		- Inherent justice and justification are distinct gifts of God

â€¢ ••God's role in justification••
	• ••God as justifier••
		- God justifies none but such as bring unto Him the obedience of Christ
	• ••God as just in justifying••
		- God is just in justifying, because He justifies none but such as bring unto Him the obedience of Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Imperfection of Inward Justice••
	• Inward justice is imperfect because it is increased by a second justification
	• It is mixed with the corruption of the flesh in this life

â€¢ ••The Righteousness of a Good Conscience••
	• It is a gift of God, but not the means of justification
	• It does not justify us (1 Cor. 4:4)

â€¢ ••The Error of Popish Doctrine••
	• Remission of sins is not only the abolishing of guilt and punishment, but also of corruption
	• This is contrary to Paul's statement that sin dwells in him (Rom. 7)

â€¢ ••The Use of the Doctrine of Justification••
	• We are taught to deny ourselves and go out of ourselves, relying on Christ
	• The obedience of Christ is the foundation of our obedience, comfort, and happiness

â€¢ ••The Obedience of Christ••
	• It is the foundation of our obedience, comfort, and happiness
	• We are to fashion our lives after Christ's example of love, mercy, meekness, and patience

â€¢ ••The Means of Justification••
	• The faith of Christ is the means of justification
	• This faith is not just a persuasion of God's power, but also His will and promise

â€¢ ••The Object of Abraham's Faith••
	• Abraham's faith was not just a persuasion of God's power, but also His will and promise
	• He believed in the promise of God that the Messiah would descend from his loins

â€¢ ••The Nature of Justifying Faith••
	• It is not just a gift of God, but also a response to God's promise and will
	• It involves trusting in God's power and will, not just His ability to perform miracles

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is essential for justification and salvation
	• It is not just a intellectual assent, but a deep trust and reliance on God

â€¢ ••The Foundation of Our Thankfulness••
	• If we believe that Christ suffered and fulfilled the law for us, we should show ourselves thankful
	• This thankfulness is a response to God's mercy and love in sending Christ to save us

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Faith••
	• Faith is a gift that enables us to apprehend Christ and His benefits.
	• To believe in Christ and to receive Christ are equivalent expressions (John 1:12).
	• Faith is the means by which we receive the word of Christ and acknowledge it (John 17:8).

â€¢ ••Nature of Faith••
	• Faith is not just a general knowledge or acknowledgment of Christ's divinity.
	• True faith involves a personal relationship with Christ and a reliance on Him (John 20:28).
	• Faith is not just a mental assent, but a heart-level trust and affection (Rom. 10:9).

â€¢ ••Justifying Faith••
	• Justifying faith is a supernatural act of the understanding that applies Christ and His benefits to ourselves.
	• This application is made by believing that Christ with His benefits is really ours.
	• Justifying faith is not just a certain confidence, but a gift of God that enables us to approach God with confidence (Eph. 3:12).

â€¢ ••Confidence and Faith••
	• Confidence is a fruit of faith, not the same thing as faith.
	• Faith and confidence are often used interchangeably in Scripture, but they have distinct meanings.
	• Confidence is the effect or result of faith, not the same as faith itself.

â€¢ ••Grounds of Apprehension••
	• Special faith must have a special and infallible ground.
	• The grounds of apprehension are three:
		- General promises of remission of sins and life everlasting in the gospel.
		- A commandment to apply these promises to ourselves (1 John 3:23).
		- The testimony of the Spirit that we are the sons of God (Rom. 8:16).

â€¢ ••Testimony of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit testifies together with our spirit that we are the sons of God (Rom. 8:16).
	• This testimony involves four things:
		- A recognition of our sonship.
		- A sense of adoption.
		- A sense of peace and joy.
		- A sense of being in Christ.

â€¢ ••Faith and Salvation••
	• Salvation is promised to general faith (Rom. 10:9).
	• General faith is sufficient for the obtaining of a miraculous cure.
	• However, true faith involves a personal relationship with Christ and a reliance on Him.

â€¢ ••Devils and Faith••
	• The devils believe that Christ is God, but they do not have a personal relationship with Him.
	• True faith involves a heart-level trust and affection for Christ, not just a mental assent.

â€¢ ••Thomas and Faith••
	• Thomas's confession of Christ as his Lord and God (John 20:28) is an example of true faith.
	• Thomas's faith is not just a general acknowledgment of Christ's divinity, but a personal relationship with Him.

â€¢ ••Faith and the Word••
	• To receive the word of Christ and to believe it are equivalent expressions (John 17:8).
	• Faith is the means by which we apprehend Christ and His benefits, and apply them to ourselves.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Grounds for Assurance of Salvation••
	• The testimony of God is sufficient to certify and assure us of our salvation
	• This testimony must be certainly known to be effective
	• It is found and perceived in the use of the word, prayer, sacraments
	• It is especially given and felt in times of great danger and affliction

â€¢ ••Special Faith••
	• A special faith may be gathered from things generally revealed in the Word of God
	• It may also be gathered from sense, observation, and experience
	• This faith is a certain testimony that leads to the conclusion of forgiveness of sins and salvation

â€¢ ••The Role of Conscience••
	• The testimony of the conscience is a certain testimony that confirms our faith
	• Paul says that this is his rejoicing (2 Cor. 1:12)

â€¢ ••Degrees of Apprehension••
	• There are two degrees of apprehension: weak and strong
	• Weak apprehension is when we endeavor to apprehend, but may still struggle with doubt

â€¢ ••Weak Apprehension••
	• Weak apprehension is characterized by an honest heart and a will to believe
	• God accepts the will to believe for faith itself
	• Doubts may still abound, but they are not of the nature of faith

â€¢ ••Strong Apprehension••
	• Strong apprehension is a full persuasion of God's mercy
	• This is not incident to all believers, but to the prophets, apostles, martyrs, and those who have been long exercised in the school of Christ

â€¢ ••Attaining Strong Apprehension••
	• There must be a knowledge of Christ
	• A general persuasion of the possibility of God's promises follows

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is not just a feeling, but a supernatural act that presupposes a supernatural power or gift
	• The will to believe and repent presupposes the power and gift of faith and repentance in the heart

â€¢ ••The Common Faith of True Believers••
	• True believers live by hungering and thirsting for God, rather than by full apprehension of Christ
	• Our comfort stands rather in being known by God, than in knowing God

â€¢ ••The Measure of Faith••
	• The measure of faith is not just a matter of degree, but a matter of quality
	• A full persuasion of God's mercy is the highest degree of faith.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Faith••
	• Faith is a gift from God that allows us to believe in the pardon and mercy of God
	• It is not just a feeling or a strong imagination, but a genuine trust in God's love and forgiveness

â€¢ ••The Process of Faith••
	• Faith begins with a desire to believe and a humble invocation for pardon
	• It is followed by a quieting of the conscience and an experience of God's mercies and love
	• This experience leads to a full persuasion of God's love and forgiveness

â€¢ ••The Degrees of Faith••
	• Not everyone has a full persuasion of God's love and forgiveness at the same time
	• Faith is a process that develops over time, and it is not always easy to distinguish between true faith and a strong imagination

â€¢ ••The Papist View of Faith••
	• The papists believe that faith is a work that merits justification
	• They believe that faith justifies because it stirs up good motions and affections in the heart

â€¢ ••The Protestant View of Faith••
	• Faith justifies because it is an instrument to apprehend and apply Christ and His obedience
	• Faith is not a work that merits justification, but rather a means to receive God's justification

â€¢ ••The Role of Good Works••
	• Good works are a necessary consequence of true faith
	• Faith justifies us in life and death, and good works are a means to justify our faith

â€¢ ••The Importance of Prayer and Sacraments••
	• Prayer and sacraments are essential for the development of true faith
	• Faith conceived without the word, prayer, and sacraments is not a true faith

â€¢ ••The Possibility of Faith••
	• Faith and repentance are possible to all who by grace desire to believe and repent
	• God accepts the will for the deed, and those who will to believe and repent have begun to believe and repent

â€¢ ••The Manner of Justification••
	• Faith justifies because it is an instrument to apprehend and apply Christ and His obedience
	• Faith does not justify as a work or as an excellent virtue, but rather as a means to receive God's justification

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Faith and Works••
	• Faith and works are not mutually exclusive, but rather complementary
	• Good works are a necessary consequence of true faith, and faith justifies us in life and death.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Faith is the key to justification••
	• We are to fix our eyes on Christ for justification and salvation
	• Faith is the means by which we receive God's mercy and grace

â€¢ ••True religion exalts grace over nature••
	• We are to make God the sole worker of our salvation
	• We are to receive God's mercy and grace by faith, not by nature

â€¢ ••Faith alone justifies••
	• Paul says we are justified by faith, without the works of the law
	• This means faith is the sole cause of our justification, not works

â€¢ ••The papist's error••
	• Papists try to translate Paul's words to mean faith is necessary, but not sufficient
	• This corrupts the text and contradicts Paul's clear meaning

â€¢ ••Faith is the sole means of justification••
	• We cannot do more in curing spiritual diseases than in curing physical diseases
	• Christ says "only believe" (Mark 5:36) to emphasize the sufficiency of faith

â€¢ ••Abraham's example••
	• Abraham was justified by faith, not by his works
	• This shows that faith is the sole means of justification, not works

â€¢ ••The doctrine of justification by faith alone is of great use••
	• It teaches that justification is by God's mere mercy, not by human merit
	• It excludes all merit of congruity, works of preparation, and human cooperation

â€¢ ••Justification is by Christ's merit alone••
	• We are justified freely by the redemption that is in Christ
	• Christ's obedience is the sole cause of our justification, not anything in us

â€¢ ••Faith is the sole property of justification••
	• Faith is like a hand that opens itself to receive a gift
	• Love and hope are not the sole means of justification, but faith is

â€¢ ••Objections to justification by faith alone••
	• Faith is never alone in the person justified or in godly conversation
	• However, in the act of justification, faith is alone, just as the eye is alone in seeing.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Faith as an instrument or way••
	• Faith can be considered as a means to apprehend Christ for salvation
	• However, if faith is seen as a way, other virtues and works are also necessary for salvation
• ••The role of sacraments••
	• Sacraments serve to confirm faith, which is the instrument that applies Christ
	• The papists' teaching that sacraments apply Christ's sacrifice is incorrect
• ••Kinds of justification••
	• The papists teach two kinds of justification: making a bad person good and making a good person better
	• However, the Bible teaches that there is only one justification by faith without works
• ••Ground of justification by faith••
	• The ground of justification by faith is laid down in Romans 16:16, "No flesh shall be justified by the works of the law"
	• This is supported by Psalm 143:2, which says "No flesh shall be justified in thy sight"
• ••Interpretation of Scripture••
	• The apostles and Christ often add words to Old Testament quotes without changing the sense
	• This is seen in examples such as Matthew 4:10 and Hebrews 10:5
• ••Practice of the justified••
	• The practice of those who are justified is to believe or put their trust in Christ
	• This is seen in examples such as 2 Chronicles 20:20 and Proverbs 16:3
• ••Rules for faith and practice••
	• Faith and practice must reign in the heart and have all at command
	• Faith must overrule nature and command the strongest affections
• ••Faith and sin••
	• If we seek to be made righteous by Christ and are found sinners, Christ is not the minister of sin
	• This is a misunderstanding of the relationship between faith and sin
• ••Comfort in Christ••
	• When we are distressed, we must fix our hearts on Christ without separation
	• This is seen in examples such as 2 Chronicles 20:12 and Job 23:12
• ••True comfort••
	• True comfort comes from fixing our hearts on Christ without separation
	• This is seen in examples such as Psalm 27:13

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's speech to Peter and the Jews••
	• Paul's speech is directed not only to Peter, but also to the Jews who were maintaining justification by the law.
	• Some interpret Paul's words as a response to the false apostles, but this is not the most likely interpretation.

â€¢ ••The objection to justification by faith alone••
	• If we are justified by Christ alone without observing the law, then there is no difference between Jews and Gentiles.
	• This would imply that Christ is the minister of sin, which is a blasphemous idea.

â€¢ ••Paul's response: "God forbid"••
	• Paul's response is not a direct confutation of the objection, but rather a continuation of his previous speech.
	• Paul is using a third reason to dissuade Peter from halting between the Jews and Gentiles.

â€¢ ••The argument against a second justification by works••
	• If being justified by Christ means remaining sinners and needing further justification by the law, then Christ is the minister of sin.
	• But this is a blasphemous idea, and therefore those justified by Christ need not further be justified by the law.

â€¢ ••The nature of Christ's ministry••
	• Christ is the minister of righteousness, not sin.
	• He brings everlasting righteousness and takes away the sins of the world.

â€¢ ••The error of making Christ the minister of sin••
	• This is a blasphemy that undermines the true nature of Christ's ministry.
	• Many who profess Christ are guilty of this error, presuming on mercy and living as they please.

â€¢ ••The perfection of justification by Christ••
	• Those justified by Christ are perfectly justified and need not further be justified by anything outside of Christ.
	• This includes the works of the law.

â€¢ ••The corruption of original sin in justified believers••
	• The corruption of original sin remains in justified believers, but it is not imputed to them by God.
	• This corruption has received its deadly wound by the death of Christ.

â€¢ ••The role of confession in obtaining pardon••
	• Confession of sin is not a cause of pardon, but rather a way to obtain it.
	• The uncovering of one's sins is a way to cover them before God.

â€¢ ••The rejection of a second justification by works••
	• The papists teach that there is a second justification by works, but this is not supported by Scripture.
	• Those justified by Christ are fully justified and need not further be justified by anything outside of Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Christ is sufficient for justification••
	• Paul teaches that Christ alone is sufficient for our justification (Col. 2:10)
	• Christ is a well of grace and life that is never dried up (John 4:14)

â€¢ ••We must content ourselves with Christ alone••
	• We must despise all merits and satisfactions done by man in relation to justification
	• We should seek to be justified by the faith of Christ (Phil. 3:9)

â€¢ ••The care of men in this world••
	• Our care should be to seek to be justified by the faith of Christ
	• We should desire to be found in Christ, having not our own righteousness but the righteousness that comes through faith in Christ

â€¢ ••Building again what has been destroyed••
	• Paul means that building again the justice of the law is like destroying what has been accomplished in Christ
	• This is a form of making oneself a transgressor (1 Cor. 3:17)

â€¢ ••The modesty and meekness of Paul••
	• Paul is careful not to offend others, even when speaking about Peter and the Jews
	• He bids us to please all men in that which is good (Rom. 15:2)

â€¢ ••The danger of building again what has been destroyed••
	• Teachers, rulers, and believers can all be great offenders when good doctrine is joined with bad conversation or example
	• This can lead to the kingdom of darkness being built up again

â€¢ ••Being constant in what is good••
	• We should be constant in holding fast the gospel and reformed religion
	• We should not build again what we have destroyed, either in word or deed

â€¢ ••The law and justification••
	• Paul is dead to the law, not to live as he lists, but to live to the honor of God
	• This is a second reason to prove that Christ is not a minister of sin

â€¢ ••What it means to be dead to the law••
	• To be dead to the law is to be free from the dominion of the law
	• We are free in four respects: from the accusing and damning power of the law, from the condemning power of the law, from the commanding power of the law, and from the corrupting power of the law

â€¢ ••The purpose of being dead to the law••
	• We are dead to the law so that we may live unto God
	• This is a key aspect of being justified by faith in Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Law's Relationship to the Believer••
	• The law is a ministry of death and condemnation (2 Cor. 3:7, 9)
	• The law cannot reveal faith in Christ, repentance, or other spiritual truths
	• The law is not a cause for spiritual growth or good works

â€¢ ••The Law's Effect on the Unregenerate••
	• The law provokes and stirs up corruption in the unregenerate (Rom. 7:8)
	• The law exacts perfect obedience, which is impossible for humans to achieve
	• The law is a hard and cruel master that accuses, terrifies, and condemns

â€¢ ••The Law's Relationship to Justification••
	• Those justified by Christ are dead to the law (Rom. 7:4)
	• If we are justified by works, we are made alive to the law
	• The law has no power to justify or save

â€¢ ••The Papist Error••
	• Papists teach that the law and gospel are one in substance
	• This is incorrect, as those justified by Christ are dead to the law, not the gospel
	• The gospel is a separate and distinct message from the law

â€¢ ••The Means of Death to the Law••
	• The law itself is the means of death to the law (Rom. 7:4)
	• The law cannot reveal faith in Christ or other spiritual truths
	• The law is not a cause for spiritual growth or good works

â€¢ ••The Two Types of Repentance••
	• Legal repentance is a response to the law's condemnation
	• Evangelical repentance is a gift of grace, turning to God through faith in Christ

â€¢ ••The Law's Dominion••
	• The law reigns over all men without exception until they are justified
	• When we begin to believe in Christ and amend our lives, the law's dominion ceases
	• We are then under grace, not the law

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Living Under the Law••
	• Those who live under the law will be accused, terrified, and condemned
	• They will face death and destruction without mercy
	• The law is merciless and will not spare those who are under its dominion

â€¢ ••The Comfort of Being Under Grace••
	• Those who have begun to repent and believe are under grace, not the law
	• They are dead to the law and have a merciful Savior who will protect them
	• They will be spared as a father spares his child, not broken or crushed

â€¢ ••The Role of the Gospel••
	• The gospel is not the law, but a separate and distinct message
	• The gospel is the means of faith in Christ, which leads to spiritual growth and good works
	• The gospel is a gift of God's grace, not a human achievement.

â€¢ ••Understanding Legal Sorrow vs. Evangelical Sorrow••
	• Legal sorrow is sorrow for sin in respect of punishment, wrought by the law
	• Evangelical sorrow is sorrow for sin because it is sin, wrought by the preaching of mercy and reconciliation

â€¢ ••The Role of the Law••

	• The law is an occasion for us to flee to Christ, who causes us to die to the law

â€¢ ••The End of Our Death to the Law••
	• To live to God
	• This life is described as the action, motion, or operation of the Spirit in us

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Spiritual Life••
	• Serves to make us live to God, to the honor and glory of God
	• Described by living wisely, godly, and justly

â€¢ ••Rules for Living Wisely••
	• Labor with all diligence and speed to be worthy to stand before the Son of Man at His coming
	• Labor to be in Christ, having true faith and good conscience

â€¢ ••Rules for Living Godly••
	• Bring ourselves into the presence of the invisible God
	• Set our thoughts, wills, affections, and actions in His sight and presence

â€¢ ••Rules for Living Godly (continued)••
	• Take knowledge of the will of God in all things
	• Acknowledge God's will when time and place serve

â€¢ ••Rules for Living Godly (continued)••
	• Bring ourselves in subjection to the known will of God
	• Captivate all our senses unto it and suffer God to set up His kingdom in us

â€¢ ••Rules for Living Godly (continued)••
	• Remember that we have to deal with God Himself in all our actions
	• Consider the examples of Enoch, Abraham, Isaac, David, Cornelius, and Paul

â€¢ ••Rules for Living Godly (continued)••
	• Join ourselves to the assemblies where the word is preached, prayer is made, and sacraments are administered
	• Consider the practice of Moses and David

â€¢ ••Humble yourself before God••
	• Confess your offenses and make instant deprecation for mercy

â€¢ ••Be silent in your heart••

â€¢ ••Live unto God through faith••

â€¢ ••Respond to God's call to live unto Him••

â€¢ ••Paul's statement "I am crucified with Christ"••
	• Refers to the fact that believers are united with Christ in His death and resurrection

	• Mortification of sin by the virtue of Christ's death

	• Freed from hell, death, and condemnation

	• Kills sin in us

â€¢ ••The love of God in Christ••
	• Revealed through the fact that our sins crucified Christ
	• Demonstrated through the benefits of His passion

â€¢ ••Christ's partnership in our miseries and afflictions••
