	• This was the moment when the new world began

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Ceremonial laws••
	• Were either of figure and signification or of order
	• Were abrogated at the coming of Christ (Col. 2:17)
	• Examples include the Sabbath and the law of tenths

â€¢ ••Moral laws••
	• Are perpetual and not abrogated
	• Serve to maintain and uphold the Ten Commandments
	• Examples include the law against idolatry (Deut. 13:6-9)

â€¢ ••Judicial laws••
	• Concern inheritances, lands, bargains, controversies, and crimes
	• Were given to the Jews and pertain to the regiment of the commonwealth
	• Some are abrogated, while others are not

â€¢ ••Abrogated judicial laws••
	• Include laws that particularly concern the nation of the Jews, the land of Canaan, and the times before Christ
	• Examples include the law of tenths and the law of restoring fourfold or being a bondman

â€¢ ••Non-abrogated judicial laws••
	• Include laws that are moral in equity and perpetual
	• Serve to maintain and uphold the Ten Commandments
	• Examples include the law against idolatry and the law against theft

â€¢ ••Judicial laws in different contexts••
	• Laws that were given to the Jews in the Old Testament may not be applicable in modern times
	• Examples include the law of tenths, which is not applicable in modern countries

â€¢ ••Positive laws vs. judicial laws••
	• Positive laws are laws that are established by human governments
	• Judicial laws are laws that are established by God
	• Examples include the law of tenths, which is established by positive laws in modern countries

â€¢ ••The role of the magistrate••
	• The magistrate has the power to increase punishment for sins
	• Examples include the punishment of theft with death in some cases

â€¢ ••The relationship between judicial laws and the commonwealth••
	• Judicial laws pertain to the regiment of the commonwealth
	• Examples include laws that concern inheritances, lands, and bargains

â€¢ ••The importance of discerning judicial laws••
	• Judicial laws that are moral in equity and perpetual are not abrogated
	• Examples include laws that serve to maintain and uphold the Ten Commandments

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Judicial laws are necessary for God's glory and human salvation••
	• They are essential for maintaining the first commandment (worshiping God alone)
	• They are necessary for the salvation of men

â€¢ ••Examples of judicial laws••
	• "Thou shalt not suffer a witch to live" (Ex. 22:18) - a fence to the first commandment

â€¢ ••Reasons for judicial laws••
	• To uphold the honor due to parents (fifth commandment)

	• To prevent the land from being defiled with blood (sixth commandment)

	• Present ourselves to Him as instruments of His glory

â€¢ ••Imitating Christ••
	• Strive to resemble Christ in all good virtue and holy conversation
	• Be like Him, our eldest brother and firstborn of many brethren (1 John 3:2-3)

â€¢ ••Love for the Word of God••
	• Desire and love the Word of God to grow in knowledge, grace, and good life
	• The Word is the milk and food that feeds God's children (1 Peter 2:2)

â€¢ ••Afflictions and Correction••
	• We must expect many afflictions as God's children
	• We must resign ourselves to God's will and good pleasure in times of correction

â€¢ ••The Internal Means of Adoption••
	• Faith in Christ is the internal means of adoption
	• This faith is a particular or special faith with three acts or effects:
		- Believing Christ to be a savior
		- Believing Christ to be our savior
		- Placing confidence in Him

â€¢ ••Objections to Special Faith••
	• Papists object that special faith requires a special word of God
	• Answer: We have a general promise with a commandment to apply it to ourselves

â€¢ ••The Word and Promise of God••
	• The word and promise of God in Scripture is made particular in the public ministry
	• When we apply the promise to ourselves, we have a special faith

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is essential for adoption as God's children
	• We must have a special faith that believes Christ to be our savior and places confidence in Him

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••God's Will is Revealed through the Word••
	• God reveals two things to people: His will to save them through Christ and His will that they believe in Christ.
	• The word preached in the public ministry is like an angel speaking directly from heaven.

â€¢ ••Special Faith is Not Absurd••
	• The argument that special faith is absurd because a sinner must believe in pardon before receiving it is false.
	• Faith and pardon are given at the same moment, and faith is not a prerequisite for pardon.

â€¢ ••Two Degrees of Faith••
	• There are two degrees of faith: weak faith and strong faith.
	• Weak faith is characterized by doubt and a desire to believe, while strong faith is a full persuasion of God's mercy in Christ.

â€¢ ••Weak Faith Precedes Justification••
	• Weak faith precedes justification and is a necessary step towards strong faith.
	• Weak faith is a will and desire to believe in Christ, accompanied by a care to use good means and increase in faith.

â€¢ ••Strong Faith Follows Justification••
	• Strong faith follows justification and is a result of observing and experiencing God's providence and goodness.
	• Strong faith is a full persuasion or resolution of God's love and mercy in Christ.

â€¢ ••When Faith Begins to Breed in the Heart••
	• Faith begins to breed in the heart when a person is touched by their conscience for their sins.
	• This is accompanied by a sense of spiritual poverty and a hunger and thirst for Christ and His righteousness.

â€¢ ••The Promise of Faith••
	• The promise of faith is declared in Revelation 21:6, where Christ says, "I will give to him that thirsteth of the well of the water of life freely."
	• This promise indicates that thirsting is a measure of faith, and that faith is a necessary step towards receiving Christ.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Thirsting••
	• Professors of the gospel who lack a sense of their unworthiness and thirsting are far from true faith, regardless of their gifts.
	• Thirsting is a necessary step towards true faith and a deep understanding of God's mercy in Christ.

â€¢ ••How Faith is Conceived in the Heart••
	• Faith is conceived in the spiritual exercises of invocation and repentance.
	• This involves seeing one's sins and God's anger against them, making confession and prayer for pardon, and striving against unbelief.

â€¢ ••The Role of Holy Means in Conceiving Faith••
	• Faith is also conceived in the use of holy means, such as the preaching of the word and sacraments.
	• Meditation on the promise of mercy and applying it to oneself is a key way to conceive true faith.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism as a sign of adoption••
	• Baptism is not a mark of God's child on its own
	• It must be joined with faith to be a true sign of adoption
	• The Bible speaks of both outward and inward baptism (Matt. 3:11, 1 Peter 3:21)

â€¢ ••Marks of the inward baptism••
	• The Spirit of grace and supplications (Zech. 12:10)
	• Hearing and obeying the voice of God in all things (John 8:47, 10:27)
	• Not sinning, living a life according to God's commandments (1 John 3)

â€¢ ••Distinguishing between outward and inward baptism••
	• Outward baptism is not enough on its own
	• Inward baptism is marked by a new birth and the Spirit of God

â€¢ ••Addressing concerns about baptism••
	• If you have sorrow for your corruptions and sins, you are born of God
	• If you have a purpose to sin no more, you are born of God
	• If you avoid the occasions of sin and fear to offend, you are born of God

â€¢ ••Addressing concerns about faith••
	• If you have sorrow for your unbelief, you have a measure of true faith
	• If you have a will and desire to believe, you have a measure of true faith
	• If you care to increase in faith by good means, you have a measure of true faith

â€¢ ••Addressing concerns about prayer••
	• If you can pray, though your prayer is not heard, you are the child of God
	• The prayer of the heart is the mark of the Spirit of adoption (Rom. 8:15, 26)

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome and baptism••
	• The Church of Rome claims to have true baptism, but it is severed from true faith
	• Baptism in the Church of Rome is not joined with the inward baptism of the Spirit

â€¢ ••The importance of true faith••
	• True faith is the principal part of the inward baptism
	• Without true faith, baptism is not a true sign of adoption

â€¢ ••The light in the lantern••
	• The confession of faith and baptism in the papacy pertain to another hidden church
	• They are not a true sign of adoption in the papacy

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Baptism joined with true faith is the infallible mark of the child of God
	• The outward baptism must be joined with the inward baptism of the Spirit to be a true sign of adoption

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism is a sign of adoption, not of the Romish church••
	• Baptism is a sign of our adoption by God, not a sign of membership in the Roman Catholic Church.
	• We must be careful not to deceive ourselves into thinking that baptism alone is sufficient for salvation.

â€¢ ••Baptism is not just an outward act••
	• Baptism is not just a physical act, but also an inward spiritual transformation.
	• We must be washed inwardly by Christ's Spirit in order to have a part in Him (John 13:8).

â€¢ ••Circumcision is not enough••
	• Circumcision, like baptism, is not enough on its own to save us (Rom. 2:25).
	• We must be doers of the law, not just outwardly circumcised or baptized.

â€¢ ••Baptism saves, but not the water••
	• Baptism indeed saves, but it is not the water itself that saves us (1 Peter 3:21).
	• It is the stipulation of a good conscience, by the resurrection of Christ, that saves us.

â€¢ ••The outward baptism without the inward is not enough••
	• The outward baptism without the inward spiritual transformation is not a mark of God's child.
	• It is like making a vow and then breaking it (Eccl. 5:4).

â€¢ ••Baptism is a seal of adoption••
	• Baptism is not only a sign of adoption, but also a seal of it.
	• It conveys to us the adoption that we have in Christ.

â€¢ ••The nature of baptism••
	• Baptism has eight aspects: name, matter, form, end, efficacy, necessity, circumstances, and use.
	• We will explore each of these aspects in more detail.

â€¢ ••The name of baptism••
	• Baptism is taken six ways in Scripture:
		- Superstitious washings of the Pharisees
		- Washings appointed by God in the ceremonial law
		- Washing by water that seals the covenant of the New Testament
		- A metaphor for a grievous cross or calamity
		- The bestowing of extraordinary gifts of the Holy Ghost
		- The whole ecclesiastical ministry

â€¢ ••The phrases used in Scripture of baptism••
	• Phrases like "baptized into Christ" and "put on Christ" are used in Scripture.
	• These phrases are to be explained and understood in their context.

â€¢ ••The matter of baptism••
	• The matter of baptism includes the sign, the thing signified, and the analogy of both.
	• The sign is partly the element of water and partly the rite of washing the body.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism Method••
	• Dipping is used in hot countries and for adults, as it represents spiritual washing more fully.
	• Sprinkling is used in cold countries and for infants, due to health and life concerns.

â€¢ ••Baptism Significance••
	• Baptism signifies Christ's mediation and cleansing power.
	• The substance of baptism is Christ Himself, who washes and cleanses us.

â€¢ ••Analogy of Baptism••
	• Water represents Christ's merits and efficacy in cleansing us from sin.
	• External washing of the body resembles inward washing by the Spirit.

â€¢ ••Baptism Elements••
	• Dipping signifies mortification and fellowship with Christ in His death.
	• Staying under water signifies the burial of sin.
	• Coming out of the water signifies resurrection from sin to new life.

â€¢ ••Form of Baptism••
	• The form of baptism is to "teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit" (Matt. 28:19).

â€¢ ••Divine Order in Baptism••
	• God's order in making a covenant with man is:
		- Call men to believe and repent.
		- Make promise of mercy and forgiveness.
		- Seal promise with baptism.

â€¢ ••Biblical Examples••
	• Abraham was called to walk before God and be upright before circumcision.
	• The Israelites were told to turn and obey before God made His promises and covenant good.

â€¢ ••Repentance and Baptism••
	• Repentance belongs to baptism and is one of the first things required.
	• It is folly to make repentance a distinct means of salvation and a distinct sacrament from baptism.

â€¢ ••Promise of Salvation••
	• The promise of salvation belongs only to those who turn and believe.
	• It is a falsehood to imagine that the promise of salvation belongs generally to all mankind.

â€¢ ••Use of Baptism••
	• Many baptized persons live as if they had not been baptized, committing common sins.
	• This highlights the need for ongoing repentance and faith after baptism.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Problem of Profaneness••
	• Many people are baptized but do not know the order of God's covenant in baptism.
	• They prioritize God's promise over repentance and faith, leading to profaneness.

â€¢ ••The Order of Baptism••
	• First, become a disciple of Christ.
	• Then, lay hold of God's promises.

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Ignorance••
	• Bringing up children in ignorance of God's covenant in baptism.
	• Barred from God's mercies and benefits.

â€¢ ••The Working of Salvation••
	• Keep the order of God's covenant in baptism.
	• First, turn to God according to His laws.
	• Second, lay hold of God's promises and confirm them through sacraments.

â€¢ ••The Significance of Baptism••
	• Baptizing in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	• Signifies the commandment and authority of God.
	• Signifies the invocation of the true God's name.

â€¢ ••The Invocation of Christ's Name••
	• Doing everything in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ.
	• Invoking Christ's name in baptism.

â€¢ ••The Meaning of "In the Name"••
	• To wash with water, signifying the name of God is on the baptized.
	• To receive the baptized into the household of God as a child of God.

â€¢ ••The Covenant of Grace••
	• Solemnified between God and the baptized.
	• God's actions: making a promise of reconciliation and sealing it.

â€¢ ••God's Actions in Baptism••
	• Making a promise of reconciliation (remission of sins and life everlasting).
	• Sealing the promise through the washing of water and the earnest of God's Spirit.

â€¢ ••The Obligation of the Baptized••
	• Binding themselves to give homage to the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost.
	• Through faith and obedience to God's commandments.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Trinity••
	• There is one God in three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Ghost
	• The three persons are distinct, with the Father first, Son second, and Holy Ghost third

â€¢ ••Baptism••
	• Baptism is administered in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost
	• This is the true form of baptism, as prescribed by Scripture

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Baptism••
	• Baptism serves as a pledge of God's graces and mercies
	• It is a sign of Christian profession before the world
	• It is a means of entrance into the visible church
	• It is a means of unity among believers

â€¢ ••The Efficacy of Baptism••
	• Baptism has efficacy for the whole life of man
	• It takes away all sins past, present, and to come
	• The party baptized must turn to God, believe in Christ, and continue in faith and repentance

â€¢ ••The Covenant of Grace••
	• The covenant of grace is everlasting
	• The covenant is the foundation or substance of baptism

â€¢ ••The Ends of Baptism••
	• Baptism serves as a pledge of union with Christ
	• It serves as a means of remission of sins
	• It serves as a means of mortification (death to sin)

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Baptism and Faith••
	• Baptism is not a substitute for faith, but rather a means of expressing faith
	• Faith is necessary for baptism to be effective

â€¢ ••The Importance of Knowing God••
	• We are to know God as He has revealed Himself in the covenant of grace
	• We are to acknowledge the Father as our Father, the Son as our Redeemer, and the Holy Ghost as our Comforter

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Baptism and Worship••
	• The Father, Son, and Holy Ghost are jointly to be worshiped together
	• Baptism is a means of worshiping God in the three persons of the Trinity

â€¢ ••The Authority of Scripture••
	• Scripture is the ultimate authority for understanding baptism and the Trinity
	• Scripture teaches that baptism is a means of expressing faith and union with Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism's Purpose and Duration••
	• Baptism is not tied to a specific time, but its force remains as long as the covenant is in effect.
	• The Galatians' example shows that baptism can instruct and direct even after falling away.

â€¢ ••Papists' Doctrine on Baptism••
	• The papists believe baptism takes away all sins before its administration, but not after.
	• They argue that sins after baptism are forgiven through the sacrament of penance.

â€¢ ••Argument 1: Circumcision and Baptism••
	• Circumcision had no use after its administration for abolishing sin.
	• However, circumcision was still a reminder of the need to circumcise the heart (Jer. 4:4).

â€¢ ••Argument 2: Apostles' Practice••
	• The apostles called sinners to confession and repentance after baptism (Acts 8:22; 1 John 1:9).
	• This practice brings people back to their baptismal order, which involves turning to God and believing in Christ.

â€¢ ••Argument 3: Hebrews 6:6••
	• The text speaks of those who fall away by universal apostasy, not those who fall after baptism.
	• Repentance is still possible after baptism, but only where Christ is not renounced.

â€¢ ••Argument 4: Penance as a Second Table••
	• Penance is a second table or board for returning to baptism after falling away.
	• Repentance pertains to the inward baptism, making baptism the true sacrament of repentance.

â€¢ ••The Use of Baptism••
	• Baptism provides comfort in times of misery or distress.
	• Remembering the obligation of baptism can help in times of temptation.

â€¢ ••Baptism and Original Sin••
	• The papists believe baptism abolishes original sin, leaving no guilt or fault.
	• However, the author argues that baptism only abolishes the punishment and guilt of original sin, not the sin itself.

â€¢ ••Baptism's Effect on Original Sin••
	• Baptism abolishes the punishment and guilt of original sin in two respects:
		- Imputation: God does not impute original sin to those in Christ.
		- Dominion: Original sin does not reign in the regenerate.

â€¢ ••Remaining Sin••
	• Original sin remains in those baptized and is still properly sin.
	• Paul's statement in Romans 7:15 illustrates the ongoing struggle with sin.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's view of concupiscence••
	• Paul considers concupiscence a sin that dwells in him (Rom. 7:20)
	• He calls it "sin" because it makes men sin
	• He advises mortifying evil concupiscence (Col. 3:5)

â€¢ ••Remaining sin after baptism••
	• Some portions of the old man or original sin remain after baptism
	• St. John says that if we say we have no sin, we deceive ourselves (1 John 1:8)
	• This implies that concupiscence is a sin even in infants

â€¢ ••Christ's view of sin••
	• Christ says that even those who are washed must have their carnal affections washed (John 13:10)
	• This implies that defilements of sin remain in those who are baptized

â€¢ ••Popish doctrine on concupiscence••
	• They make three degrees of concupiscence: proneness to evil, first motions to sin, and acts of lust
	• They say the first two are not sins, but defects or infirmities

â€¢ ••Rebuttal to popish doctrine••
	• Concupiscence with first motions to evil is condemned in the moral law
	• Original concupiscence is a cause of actual sin and is therefore forbidden

â€¢ ••Nature of original sin••
	• Original sin remains after baptism as a potential guilt
	• It is a fault in nature, even if not the fault of the individual

â€¢ ••Use of original sin doctrine••
	• If original sin remains after baptism, we must humble ourselves and rely on Christ's mercy
	• We must deny ourselves and rest on Christ's pardon

â€¢ ••Difference between the godly and ungodly••
	• The godly have a sorrow for their inward corruptions and sins past
	• They have a purpose to sin no more and an endeavor to please God

â€¢ ••Baptism and grace••
	• Baptism confers grace because it is a means of receiving God's forgiveness
	• However, the text does not elaborate on how baptism confers this grace

â€¢ ••Implications of original sin doctrine••
	• If original sin remains after baptism, it implies that we are sinners to the death
	• This has implications for our understanding of the Christian life and our reliance on Christ's mercy.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism's Purpose••
	• To exhibit Christ and His benefits to the believing mind
	• To assure the baptized person of forgiveness of sins and eternal salvation

â€¢ ••Baptism as a Certificate••
	• Serves as a particular and infallible certificate of forgiveness and salvation
	• The minister applies the promise of mercy to the baptized person, making it a personal promise from God

â€¢ ••Baptism as a Token of Grace••
	• The outward washing of the body is a token or pledge of God's grace
	• Faith is confirmed through this pledge, which is an instrument to receive God's grace

â€¢ ••The Process of Confirmation••
	• The mind uses reasoning to confirm faith and receive the thing signified (e.g. remission of sins)
	• The party baptized says, "I use the sign aright in faith and repentance, therefore I shall receive the thing signified"

â€¢ ••The Sacraments Confer Grace••
	• The sacraments confer grace by signifying the will of God
	• They are a pledge of God's mercy and goodness, made visible to the eye

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sacraments••
	• A sacrament is not only a sign and seal, but also an instrument to convey God's grace
	• It is not a physical instrument, but a moral one, requiring the right use to confer grace

â€¢ ••The Papist Doctrine••
	• The sacrament confers grace by the work done (i.e. the minister's action)
	• This doctrine is a fiction of the human brain, and not supported by Scripture

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Baptisms••
	• John the Baptist distinguishes between his baptism (washing with water) and Christ's baptism (washing with the Holy Ghost)
	• This distinction shows that baptism does not confer grace by the washing of water alone

â€¢ ••The Role of Faith and the Word••
	• Baptism confers grace because the word of promise is believed and received
	• The washing of water is not the source of grace, but rather a means of receiving the word of promise

â€¢ ••The Limitations of Human Action••
	• The apostles are called "fellow-workers with God", but their work is not the source of regeneration and life
	• The washing away of the flesh does not save, but rather a good conscience makes to God (1 Peter 3:21)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Regeneration is a work of creation••
	• Regeneration is a work of God's creation, not a sacramental work
	• It is a direct work of God, not mediated through sacraments

â€¢ ••Virtue to give life is in the Godhead, not the flesh of Christ••
	• The flesh of Christ is elevated, but virtue to give life is in the Godhead
	• Sacraments do not have virtue to convey grace

â€¢ ••Faith justifies, but not by its own virtue••
	• Faith serves as a means to apprehend justification, but does not cause it
	• Sacraments cannot cause justification in the same way

â€¢ ••Ministers of sacraments are not workers of miracles••
	• The outward washing of the body in baptism is a miracle, but not a common occurrence
	• Ministers of sacraments are not workers of miracles

â€¢ ••Doctrine of sacraments is erroneous••
	• The doctrine teaches that the outward act in the sacrament confers grace, regardless of faith
	• This is contrary to Scripture, which says that faith is necessary to receive God's gift

â€¢ ••Faith and repentance are necessary for sacraments to be effective••
	• Faith and repentance serve to dispose the party, but do not enable them to receive God's gift
	• Without faith and repentance, sacraments are ineffective

â€¢ ••Using sacraments without faith is like using charms and spells••
	• Using sacraments without faith is no more effective than using charms and spells
	• Both are ineffective and may even be diabolical

â€¢ ••Baptism imprints a visible and invisible mark••
	• The visible mark is a distinction between Christians and non-Christians
	• The invisible mark is the eternal election of God and the gift of regeneration

â€¢ ••The papist doctrine of the "indelible character" is a fiction••
	• The papist doctrine teaches that an indelible character is imprinted in the souls of all men
	• This doctrine is not supported by Scripture and is a mere human invention

â€¢ ••Baptism is necessary, but not absolutely necessary for salvation••
	• Baptism is necessary in part, due to the commandment of God and our weakness
	• However, being in the covenant of God is absolutely necessary for salvation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism is not necessary for salvation••
	• The lack of baptism does not condemn if it cannot be had
	• Contempt for baptism when it can be had is pardonable if repented later
	• Children of believing parents are born holy and inherit the kingdom of God

â€¢ ••Examples of salvation without baptism••
	• The thief on the cross
	• Many holy martyrs who died without baptism
	• Children of believing parents who die before baptism

â€¢ ••Objection: Circumcision and baptism••
	• The text in Genesis 17:14 is spoken of men of years, not infants
	• Infants do not despise the ordinance of God, but their parents or men of years do

â€¢ ••Christ's statement on baptism••
	• "Except a man be born of water and the Holy Ghost, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God" (John 3:5)
	• Christ alludes to the washings of the Old Testament and gives an exposition of them
	• Baptism is necessary for admission and entrance into the church, not eternal life

â€¢ ••Circumstances of baptism••
	• The seventh point concerns the circumstances of baptism
	• There are five circumstances to consider

â€¢ ••Administration of baptism••
	• Only ministers of the word can administer baptism
	• Preaching and baptizing are joined together in the public ministry
	• Private persons have no calling to administer baptism

â€¢ ••Example of Zipporah••
	• Zipporah circumcised her child, but her example is discommendable
	• She did it in haste, anger, and without faith

â€¢ ••Private persons and baptism••
	• Private persons may teach, but not baptize
	• Private teaching and ministerial teaching are distinct in kind
	• Ministers are moved to teach by special calling and authority

â€¢ ••Authority of ministers••
	• Ministers have authority as ambassadors in the room of Christ
	• Private persons have authority as masters or fathers, but not as ministers

â€¢ ••Faith and baptism••
	• The administration of baptism by private persons is without faith
	• There is no precept or fit example for private persons to administer baptism in the Word of God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Can a wicked or heretic administer baptism?••
	• Yes, if they follow the right form and institution of baptism.
	• The example of the scribes and Pharisees, who were heretics but still taught Moses' doctrine, is cited.

â€¢ ••Is baptism valid if administered by someone who cannot preach?••
	• Yes, if they stand in the place of a lawful pastor and follow the form of baptism.
	• It is desirable for ministers to preach, but not necessary for baptism to be valid.

â€¢ ••Is intention necessary for baptism to be valid?••
	• No, as long as the institution of baptism is observed.
	• The example of Paul rejoicing that Christ was preached, even by those with malicious intentions, is cited.

â€¢ ••What is the duty of the minister in baptism?••
	• To stand in the place of God and administer the sacrament according to the institution.
	• The minister's actions are as if God Himself were performing the baptism.

â€¢ ••What is the effect of baptism?••
	• The promise of remission of sins and life everlasting is applied to the baptized person.
	• This is as if God were saying to the person, "I freely give unto you the pardon of your sins and life everlasting."

â€¢ ••What is the role of faith in baptism?••
	• The baptized person must receive the promise of mercy by special faith.
	• This faith is motivated by the consideration of what God has done for us in baptism.

â€¢ ••Who should be baptized?••
	• All those who are in the covenant, either in likelihood or in the judgment of charity.
	• This includes men of years and infants.

â€¢ ••What is required of men of years before baptism?••
	• They must make confession of their faith and promise amendment of life.
	• This is based on Scripture (Acts 2:38; 8:37-38).

â€¢ ••What is the significance of baptism?••
	• It is a sacrament that seals the covenant between God and the baptized person.
	• It is a sign and pledge of the promise of remission of sins and life everlasting.

â€¢ ••What is the effect of baptism on the baptized person?••
	• They are moved to turn to God and keep His laws.
	• They are affected by the merciful promises of God and are encouraged to rest on them.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Grounds for baptizing infants••
	• The commandment of God to baptize all nations (Matt. 28:19)
	• Baptism succeeds circumcision in the New Testament (Col. 2:11-12)
	• Infants of believing parents are in the covenant of grace

â€¢ ••Infants in the covenant of grace••
	• God promises to show mercy to thousands of those who love Him (Ex. 20)
	• The promises belong to believing parents and their children (Acts 2:39)
	• If parents believe, their children are holy and in the covenant (1 Cor. 7:14)

â€¢ ••Baptism for infants••
	• Because infants are in the covenant, they are to be baptized
	• Baptism is a seal of the covenant and a means to admit them into the fellowship of the visible church

â€¢ ••Objections to baptizing infants••
	• We cannot tell if infants are indeed children of God
	• We may not baptize them if they are not children of God

â€¢ ••Response to objections••
	• We may presume that infants of believing parents are children of God
	• God begins to manifest His election in conception and birth

â€¢ ••Faith and baptism••
	• Infants have no faith, but the faith of the parents is also the faith of their children
	• To be born in the church of believing parents is instead of the profession of faith

â€¢ ••Baptism for children of non-believers••
	• Children of Turks and Jews are not to be baptized because their parents are not in the covenant
	• Children of professed papists may be baptized, but with certain cautions

â€¢ ••Cautions for baptizing children of professed papists••
	• The parents must desire this baptism
	• There must be sureties that promise the child will be raised in the faith

â€¢ ••Baptism as a seal of the covenant••
	• Baptism has its use in infants, even if they do not understand what is happening
	• Baptism admits them into the fellowship of the visible church

â€¢ ••Ancient and received doctrine••
	• The doctrine of baptizing infants is the ancient and received doctrine of the church

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism of children of wicked Christians••
	• Children of wicked Christians can be baptized
	• The wickedness of the parent does not prejudice the child's salvation
	• There must be someone to answer for the child besides the parents

â€¢ ••Baptism of children of excommunicated parents••
	• Children of excommunicated parents can be baptized
	• The parents remain members of the church, but with condition
	• There must be someone to answer for the child besides the parents

â€¢ ••Time of baptism••
	• Baptism should only be administered once
	• The efficacy of baptism extends to the whole life of man
	• Regeneration is not repeated, as baptism is a sacrament of initiation

â€¢ ••Gift of regeneration••
	• The gift of regeneration is never utterly extinguished
	• A person cannot be born again multiple times
	• Baptism is a one-time sacrament of initiation

â€¢ ••Living members of Christ••
	• All members of the mystical body of Christ are living members
	• A person cannot die as a member of Christ
	• Believers are part of the bone and flesh of Christ

â€¢ ••Place of baptism••
	• Baptism should take place in the public assembly or congregation of the people of God
	• The congregation benefits from the explanation of baptism
	• The congregation prays for the salvation of the baptized child

â€¢ ••Use of baptism••
	• Baptism reminds us of our admission into the family of God
	• We must live as servants of God
	• Our lives should be divided into past and future

â€¢ ••Life past••
	• We must examine ourselves for past sins
	• We must confess our sins with sorrowful hearts
	• We must deplore our sins and seek forgiveness

â€¢ ••Life to come••
	• We must have a purpose of not sinning
	• Our purpose must be lively and distinct
	• We must renew our purpose daily

â€¢ ••Purpose of not sinning••
	• Our purpose must be a daily resolution
	• We must not sin against our purpose and resolution
	• Our purpose is a means of living a holy life

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Baptism as a Daily Reminder••
	• Baptism should be a daily reminder of our commitment to God's commandments
	• We should think about our baptism as often as we think or speak of our names

â€¢ ••Baptism and Conformity to Christ••
	• We must be conformable to the death and resurrection of Christ (Rom. 6:3-4)
	• Baptism teaches us to acknowledge God as our Father in Christ

â€¢ ••Baptism and Acknowledging God••
	• Baptism teaches us to acknowledge God's presence and providence
	• We should cast our care on God and acknowledge His goodness and mercy

â€¢ ••Baptism as a Source of Comfort••
	• In times of temptation, doubt, or weakness, we should recall our baptism
	• Our baptism reminds us of God's promise of pardon and eternal life

â€¢ ••Baptism as a Guide for Decision-Making••
	• We should look to our baptism for guidance in times of doubt or uncertainty
	• Our baptism reminds us of God's will and promise to be our God

â€¢ ••Baptism as a Commentary on the Scriptures••
	• Our baptism is the best commentary on the Scriptures
	• Understanding our baptism helps us understand the whole of Scripture

â€¢ ••The Gift of Adoption••
	• The gift of adoption is described as putting on Christ (Gal. 3:27)
	• This phrase is borrowed from the custom of putting on new garments after baptism

â€¢ ••Union with Christ••
	• Our union with Christ is a real and spiritual conjunction
	• We are not one with Christ in conceit, imagination, or substance

â€¢ ••Communion with Christ••
	• Our communion with Christ is a real and spiritual fellowship
	• We are not one with Christ by consent of heart or affection, but by a real union

â€¢ ••The Nature of Union with Christ••
	• Our union with Christ is not a mixture of substances or properties
	• We are distinct persons united with Christ, not merged into one entity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Unity with Christ••
	• Believers are one with Christ through the same Spirit dwelling in them (1 Cor. 6:17)
	• Christ makes Jews and Gentiles "one new man" (Eph. 2:15)
	• All believers are connected through the Spirit, like different parts of the body connected by a single soul

â€¢ ••How believers are made one with Christ••
	• Through a donation from God, where Christ is given to believers
	• Through a receiving on the part of believers, where they accept Christ and His benefits

â€¢ ••The donation of Christ••
	• Christ Himself and His whole person are given to believers (John 6:54, 56)
	• The Godhead of Christ is given to believers, but not in respect of substance (only in operation)
	• The flesh or manhood of Christ is really given to the believing heart
	• Christ gives His merit and satisfaction to believers, covering their sins (Ps. 32:1, Rev. 7:14)
	• Christ gives the efficacy of His Spirit to make believers holy and new (Eph. 4:22, 24)

â€¢ ••The receiving of Christ••
	• Believers must receive Christ where God offers and gives Him, through the word and sacraments
	• Faith is the hand by which believers receive Christ, through a supernatural act of the mind

â€¢ ••Communion with Christ••
	• Communion with Christ is when believers possess and enjoy Christ and His benefits
	• This communion is partly experienced in this life and fully in the life to come

â€¢ ••The use of considering our nakedness••
	• We are reminded to consider our fearful nakedness
	• There is a nakedness of creation, and a nakedness following the fall of man

â€¢ ••The nakedness of creation••
	• The body without covering is in health, full of glory and majesty in respect of other creatures
	• This nakedness is not inherently evil, but rather a natural state

â€¢ ••The nakedness following the fall••
	• Inward nakedness: the want of the image of God, innocence, good conscience, and favor of God
	• Outward nakedness: the body being uncovered and full of deformity and shame

â€¢ ••Laboring to see and feel our nakedness••
	• We must labor to see and feel our inward nakedness in ourselves
	• This is a special evil that makes us deformed and odious in the eye of God

â€¢ ••Caring for the trimming and garnishing of our soul••
	• We are put in mind to have a special care of the trimming and garnishing of our soul
	• This involves laboring to see and feel our nakedness and seeking to cover it with Christ's benefits

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Putting on Christ••
	• To "put on the Lord Jesus" (Rom. 13:14) means to cover our shame and nakedness before God
	• This is done by praying for God to cover our shame and by subjecting ourselves to the word and Spirit of God

â€¢ ••Uncovering and Covering Shame••
	• To uncover our shame is the way to cover it (Ps. 32:1-3)
	• This means acknowledging and confessing our sins to God

â€¢ ••Subjecting Ourselves to God••
	• We must subject ourselves to the word and Spirit of God
	• This means conforming to Christ's life and death

â€¢ ••The Wedding Garment••
	• The "wedding garment" is Christ Himself
	• Those who are not clothed with Christ will be cast into darkness

â€¢ ••The Merits of Christ••
	• The merits and obedience of Christ are like swaddling clothes and swaddling bands
	• We must lap and enfold ourselves in them to live

â€¢ ••Spiritual Drunkenness••
	• Many people are intoxicated with a spiritual drunkenness or madness
	• They are trifling about their bodies and letting their souls lie naked

â€¢ ••Baptism and the Garment of Christ••
	• We have all put on Christ in baptism
	• However, many people have worn this garment loosely, as if they were living in the warm sunshine

â€¢ ••Coming to God in Christ's Name••
	• We must come clothed with Christ into the presence of God
	• We must present the merit of Christ unto the Father, as if we were one and the same person with Him

â€¢ ••Protection and Safety••
	• The garment of Christ serves not only for a covering of our shame, but also for protection
	• If we are taken away in any common judgment, being clothed with Christ, there is no more hurt done to us than to Him

â€¢ ••Two Garments••
	• In this life, we are clad with the justice of Christ (1 Cor. 1:30)
	• In the life to come, we shall be clad with immortality, which is the second garment to be upon the former.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Two Estates••
	• The first estate is the regiment of this world, in civil society
	• The second estate is the regiment of the kingdom of heaven, characterized by justice, peace of conscience, and joy in the Holy Ghost

â€¢ ••Differences in the Two Estates••
	• In the first estate, there are differences of persons (e.g. fathers, mothers, masters, servants)
	• In the second estate, all are members of Christ and one in Him

â€¢ ••Objection 1: God's Spirit vs. Civil Authority••
	• Believers are governed by God's Spirit, but hypocrites are not
	• Civil authority is necessary to govern hypocrites and protect the church

â€¢ ••Objection 2: Freedom from Sin••
	• True believers are freed from sin, but subjection is not necessarily a result of sin
	• Subjection can be politic (for one's own good) or servile (for the good of others)

â€¢ ••Subjection and Bondage••
	• Subjection can be politic or servile, and is not necessarily a result of sin
	• Bondage, in the form of slavery, can exist in countries where it is established by positive laws, if used with mercy and moderation

â€¢ ••Objection 3: Liberty in Christ••
	• Christians have liberty by Christ, but this does not necessarily mean no bondage
	• Christians obtain spiritual liberty in this life and bodily liberty in the life to come

â€¢ ••Objection 4: Law of Nature••
	• Bondage is against the law of pure nature, created in innocence
	• However, bondage is a result of corrupt nature, and is not against the law of nature

â€¢ ••Unity in Christ••
	• All are one in Christ, but this does not mean all are one in the outward man or in civil order
	• Distinctions of men exist in respect of nation, condition, sex, and authority

â€¢ ••The Use of the Text••
	• The text can be used to expound other passages, such as "God would have all men to be saved" (1 Tim. 2)
	• The text shows that all kinds of people (men of all nations, conditions, and sexes) are one in Christ

â€¢ ••Expounding Scripture••
	• The name "Jew" can signify not only men of the tribe of Judah, but all circumcised persons of all tribes
	• The words "Judah" and "Israel" can be used synonymously or opposed, depending on the context

	• This means that they must not live as if they are bound to the world, but rather as pilgrims seeking the kingdom of heaven.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Paul's Response to Objection••
	• Paul responds to the objection that the Jews are also children of God and heirs of Christ
	• He argues that if the time of bondage is ended and the time of liberty has come, then they are sons and not servants
• ••The Time of Bondage is Ended••
	• Paul uses a similitude from the civil law to explain that heirs in their minority live in subjection to tutors and governors
	• When they come of age, they are at their own liberty
	• Similarly, the people of God before Christ were in their infancy under the law as under a tutor
	• When the fullness of time was come, they entered into the fruition of their liberty
• ••Confirmation of the Minor Premise••
	• Paul confirms the minor premise with two reasons
	• ••Reason 1: Liberty Procured by Christ••
		- Paul argues that the liberty of the people of God is procured by Christ
		- Therefore, the time of their liberty is come
	• ••Reason 2: The Spirit of Adoption••
		- Paul argues that the people of God have received the Spirit of adoption
		- Therefore, the time of their liberty is come
• ••Authority of the Parent••
	• Paul explains that the authority of the parent is a natural and universal principle
	• The parent has authority over the child in two areas: marriage and calling
	• The parent has the right to dispose of the child's marriage and to take wives for their sons
• ••Marriage and Calling••
	• Paul argues that the parent has authority to command the child to marry
	• The parent has the right to make void the contract secretly made by their children without their consent
• ••Scriptural Support••
	• Paul cites Scripture to support the authority of the parent
	• He references Colossians 3:20, Job 1:12-18, Exodus 21:7, Deuteronomy 21:21, and other passages to illustrate the authority of the parent
• ••Questions on Parental Authority••
	• Paul asks three questions to further illustrate the authority of the parent
	• The questions are: whether the father may command his child to marry, whether parents may make void the contract secretly made by their children, and whether the father may command his child to marry a specific person.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Marriage and Consent••
	• A young man who deflowers a maid must marry her if the father consents (Deut. 22:28)
	• However, God's law does not require marriage without the father's consent (Ex. 22:17)
	• A marriage made without parental consent is a "politic or civil marriage" but not a "divine or spiritual conjunction"

â€¢ ••Parental Authority••
	• Parents have authority over their children's callings and marriages
	• Children must be subject to their parents and yield to their authority

â€¢ ••Roman Religion••
	• The Roman religion sets up a hierarchy that undermines the authority of parents and princes
	• It ratifies clandestine contracts and allows children to choose their own religion against their parents' wishes

â€¢ ••Parental Duty••
	• Parents must provide meet overseers and tutors for their children after they depart
	• This is a necessary duty, as seen in Christ's example with His mother (John 19:26)

â€¢ ••Child's Duty••
	• Children must be subject to their tutors and governors as to their own fathers and mothers
	• Examples of children obeying their parents or guardians include Ruth, Christ, and the sons of the prophets

â€¢ ••The Old Testament Church••
	• The Jews were children in respect of the Christian church in the New Testament
	• They were in bondage due to the many laws they were subject to, but they had the right of sons

â€¢ ••The Law of Moses••
	• The law of Moses was a "rudiment of the world" compared to the full doctrine of the New Testament
	• It was a primer or ABC in which Christ was revealed to the world in a dark and obscure manner

â€¢ ••Blessings of God••
	• The people of the Old Testament were heirs to the same blessings as the people of the New Testament
	• The difference is only in the manner in which God dispensed these blessings

â€¢ ••The Role of Parents••
	• Parents must maintain their authority and use it for the good of their children
	• This includes providing for their children's spiritual well-being and salvation

â€¢ ••The Relationship between the Old and New Testaments••
	• The Old Testament church was a precursor to the New Testament church
	• The people of the Old Testament were children in respect of the Christian church in the New Testament

â€¢ ••The Church's Progress••

	• This time was appointed by God's providence and is the only fit time for enjoying God's blessings

â€¢ ••Prayer and Providence••

â€¢ ••The Day of Grace••

	• We should pray to God for grace and mercy today, not tomorrow

â€¢ ••Christ's Purchase of Liberty••

	• This liberty is now available to us, and we should strive to live in it.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Sending of the Son••
	• The Father sent the Son in the fullness of time
	• This is a great mystery that requires understanding

â€¢ ••What is Meant by God?••
	• The Father is the first person of the Godhead
	• He is called "God" because He is the first in order of the three divine persons
	• He has no beginning of His own person, unlike the Son and Holy Ghost

â€¢ ••How Does the Father Send the Son?••
	• The Father sends the Son by His counsel and eternal decree
	• The Son was designed to be a mediator and to become man
	• This sending implies no alteration or change of place

â€¢ ••Was the Son Sent with His Own Consent?••
	• Yes, the Son was sent with His own consent
	• The decree of the Father is the decree of the Son and the Holy Ghost
	• They are all one in nature and will

â€¢ ••How Can the Father Send the Son?••
	• The Father and Son are one in nature (Godhead), but distinct in person
	• The Father is not the Son, nor the Son the Father
	• The Father sends the Son because of their distinct persons

â€¢ ••Why is the Son Called?••
	• The Son is called because He was begotten of the Father by a perfect and eternal generation
	• This generation is not to be conceived in a carnal or human manner
	• The Son is coeternal with the Father and not one before or after the other

â€¢ ••Is the Son God?••
	• Yes, the Son is God
	• He is God because He was with God the Father before all worlds
	• He came from the bosom of His Father

â€¢ ••Objection I: The Son is Inferior to the Father••
	• Sending does not imply inequality
	• The Son is not inferior to the Father in nature, but only in condition
	• The Son voluntarily abased Himself and took on human form

â€¢ ••Objection II: God Has His Begotten••
	• The Son is not inferior to the Father in nature, but only in condition
	• The Son is begotten of the Father, but not in a human sense
	• The Father and Son are distinct persons, but one in Godhead

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The Son is God and was sent by the Father
	• The Father and Son are distinct persons, but one in Godhead
	• The Son's sending is a mystery that requires understanding and faith

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Son's Beginning and Godhead••
	• The Son has His beginning from His Father
	• The Son's Godhead is not begotten, like the Father's
	• The Son is God of Himself, not of another

â€¢ ••The Son's Lordship••
	• The Son was not made Lord in time, but is an eternal Lord
	• The Son's Lordship is declared and made manifest in the flesh after His resurrection
	• The Son's Lordship is in both His divine and human natures

â€¢ ••God's Love and the Sending of the Son••
	• God's sending of the Son declares His infinite love
	• The sending was for the sake of those who were enemies of God
	• The love of God must move us to love God again and be thankful

â€¢ ••The Son's Mediatorship••
	• The Son takes not to Himself the office of a mediator, but is called and sent by the Father
	• The office of a mediator was appointed by the Father in the eternal counsel of the blessed Trinity
	• We must have a double assurance in our consciences: that our offices and callings are good and pleasing to God, and that we are designed and called by God to those offices and callings

â€¢ ••The Son's Humiliation••
	• The Son comes from His Father, lays aside His majesty, and takes on the condition of a servant
	• We must humble and abase ourselves before God, think better of others than of ourselves, and be content with our condition

â€¢ ••The Greatness of Our Sin and Misery••
	• The Son's sending shows the greatness of our sin and misery
	• We must hate and detest our sins, which fetched the Son of God from heaven
	• We must humble ourselves with David and pray to God for His mercy

â€¢ ••The Incarnation of the Son••
	• The Son was made flesh, or made man, or made flesh of a woman
	• The Word was made flesh (John 1:14)

â€¢ ••The Reasons for the Son's Incarnation••
	• The order of divine justice requires that God's wrath be appeased and a satisfaction made in the same nature in which His majesty was offended
	• The mediator between God and man must be both God and man, in the mean between God and man

â€¢ ••The Extent of the Son's Incarnation••
	• The Son took unto Himself the whole and perfect nature of man in respect of essence
	• The Son took the entire substance of a reasonable soul and human body

â€¢ ••The Significance of the Son's Incarnation••
	• The Son's incarnation is a perfect mediator between God and man
	• The Son's incarnation shows the greatness of our sin and misery, and the love of God for us

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Body of Christ••
	• The body of Christ is of the same substance as every other body
	• Therefore, it cannot be contained in a small piece of bread
	• The body of Christ is of a substantial size, unlike a small piece of bread

â€¢ ••The Nature of Christ••
	• The Son of God took on the properties of human nature
	• These properties include life, sense, motion, reason, will, and affection
	• Christ took on the infirmities and miseries of human nature, but not sin

â€¢ ••Caveats about Christ's Nature••
	• Christ did not take on sins, only infirmities
	• Christ did not take on personal infirmities, such as dropsies or gouts
	• Christ took on the nature of all men, with all its appurtenances except sin

â€¢ ••How Christ was Made Flesh••
	• Christ's flesh was first framed, then sanctified, and finally united to the Godhead
	• The framing of Christ's flesh was an extraordinary work of the Holy Ghost
	• The sanctifying of Christ's flesh was a work of divine power, filling it with the gifts of the Holy Ghost

â€¢ ••The Matter of Christ's Flesh••
	• Christ's flesh was made of the substance of a woman
	• This distinguishes Christ from all other men, who are made of both man and woman
	• Christ's flesh was not begotten of a man, but made of a woman

â€¢ ••The Manner of Christ's Framing••
	• Christ's flesh was made without natural generation
	• This means that Christ did not come of Adam by generation, and therefore did not inherit sin and corruption

â€¢ ••Christ's Sinlessness••
	• Christ did not inherit sin and corruption from Adam
	• Christ's flesh was made of the substance of a woman, and therefore did not come of Adam by generation

â€¢ ••The Sanctifying of Christ's Flesh••
	• The sanctifying of Christ's flesh was a work of divine power
	• This filled Christ's flesh with the gifts of the Holy Ghost above measure
	• Christ's flesh was perfect and without imperfection from the moment of its framing

â€¢ ••Christ's Increase in Grace••
	• Christ increased in grace as He grew in age and stature
	• This increase was without imperfection, and was a result of the sanctifying of Christ's flesh
	• Christ received a full measure of grace fit for each stage of His life

â€¢ ••The Perfection of Christ's Flesh••
	• Christ's flesh was perfect and without imperfection from the moment of its framing
	• This perfection was a result of the sanctifying of Christ's flesh by the Holy Ghost
	• Christ's flesh remained perfect throughout His life, increasing in grace and stature.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Increase of Christ's Humanity••
	• The increase of Christ's humanity is not just in experience and manifestation, but also in habit or gift.
	• This increase is unique and has no parallel in the world.

â€¢ ••The Union of Flesh to the Godhead••
	• The union of flesh to the Godhead of the Son is a unique mystery.
	• The manhood of Christ has no personal subsistence, but is sustained by the second person.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Christ's Humanity••
	• Christ's humanity is a nature, not a person.
	• This is an exaltation to the nature of man, not a degradation.

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Believers and Christ••
	• Believers are joined to the Son of God, but in a lower degree and by communication of grace.
	• This is different from the union of Christ's humanity with the Godhead.

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Christ's Incarnation••
	• Christ's incarnation is to make us partakers of the divine nature.
	• He becomes bone of our bone and flesh of our flesh through regeneration.

â€¢ ••The Abasement of Christ••
	• Christ's abasement is an example for us to follow.
	• We must abase ourselves and become like Christ to be filled with the good things of God.

â€¢ ••The Barrier between God and Us••
	• Our sins create a wall of partition between God and us.
	• Christ's incarnation is a means of access to God, making Him "God with us".

â€¢ ••The Importance of Drawing Near to God••
	• We must draw near to God through prayer, obedience, and the use of sacraments.
	• Failure to do so results in great wickedness and punishment.

â€¢ ••The Foundation of Our Comfort••
	• The incarnation of Christ is the foundation of all our comfort and good things.
	• It is the means by which God comforts us and provides for us.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Hungering after Christ••
	• We must hunger and thirst after Christ to receive true comfort.
	• This involves faith in Christ and eating His flesh and drinking His blood.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Who is made subject to the law?••
	• The Son of God
	• As our pledge and surety, He represents the person of all the elect

â€¢ ••Why is the Son of God subject to the law?••
	• By voluntary abasement and condition of will
	• Not by nature, but by choice

â€¢ ••How was the Son of God subject to the law?••
	• By a twofold obedience:
		- Obedience of His passion
		- Obedience in fulfilling the law

â€¢ ••What is the obedience of His passion?••
	• A satisfaction for the breach of the law
	• The curse of the law laid on the Son of God, including the first death and the pains of hell

â€¢ ••What is the foundation of the passion?••
	• The Son of God was made sin for us, with all our sins imputed to Him

â€¢ ••What is the second obedience in fulfilling the law?••
	• The Son of God performed all things contained in the law, so we might have right to life everlasting

â€¢ ••Is it necessary for the justification of a sinner to fulfill the law?••
	• Yes, every jot and tittle of the law must be fulfilled
	• The sum and substance of the law must be fulfilled, which cannot be done by us as sinners

â€¢ ••Why must the law be fulfilled in the person of the mediator?••
	• To avoid the curse of the law and come to life everlasting
	• To pay the double debt or tribute owed to God

â€¢ ••What is the double debt or tribute owed to God?••
	• Homage or subjection to be performed with all the powers of the soul
	• Satisfaction by death for the breach of the law

â€¢ ••Why did the Son of God need to perform homage for us to God?••
	• To fulfill the law and pay the double debt or tribute owed to God
	• To be the end of the law for righteousness (Rom. 10:4)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Blood of Christ••
	• Purges us from all sin
	• Excludes the blood of beasts and other means of salvation outside of Christ
	• Excludes the obedience of men, but not the obedience of Christ
• ••Justification••
	• Is an alteration from evil to good, from life to death

	• Is not achieved by abolishing sin, but by covering sin and importing righteousness
• ••Christ's Obedience••
	• Was to the death, according to the duties of love in the first table

	• Was a work of supererogation, exceeding the requirements of the law
• ••The Law••
	• Is good, perfect, and excellent
	• Was natural to man before the fall
	• Is a natural property of a reasonable creature to do homage to the Creator
• ••The Redemption of Man••
	• Is the redemption of those under the law
	• Involves five key points:
		- What it means to be under the law
		- Who is under the law
		- The nature of the law as a rule of life and a grievous yoke
		- The consequences of being under the law
		- The purpose of Christ's redemption
• ••Being Under the Law••
	• Can be considered in two ways: as a rule of life and as a grievous yoke
	• As a rule of life, all who obey the law are under it
	• As a grievous yoke, the law is a burden that none can bear
• ••The Curse of the Law••
	• Increases sin and is the strength of sin
	• Is a yoke that binds offenders to everlasting death
	• Is a burden that is increased by the wicked nature of man
• ••The Israelites and the Law••
	• Were commanded to go to Canaan, but preferred to stay in the wilderness
	• Were forbidden to go to Canaan, but then wanted to go
	• Were burdened by the law and its ceremonies
• ••Circumcision••
	• Was loathed by all nations when commanded
	• Was adopted by men of sundry nations when abolished
	• Was a burden that was increased by the law
• ••The Purpose of Christ's Redemption••
	• Is to redeem those under the law
	• Involves the redemption of those who are subject to the curse of the law

	• The law is a system of ceremonies that Jews followed before Christ

â€¢ ••The Law vs. the Gospel••

	• The gospel adds an exception to the law, allowing for redemption through Christ

â€¢ ••Who Are Partakers of Redemption?••

	• Those who flee from the law's sentence to the throne of grace for mercy

â€¢ ••The Misery of Those Who Don't Know Themselves to Be Under the Law••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Intercession of Christ••
	• The intercession of Christ is effective with God.
	• Christ prayed for the sending of the Spirit, and it was accomplished (John 14:16).

• ••The Teaching of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit inwardly teaches the same things as the Son revealed through the prophets and apostles.

• ••The Distinction of Persons in the Trinity••
	• The Father, Son, and Spirit of the Son are distinct persons in the Trinity.
	• The action of sending forth argues order and distinction of persons, not superiority or inferiority.
• ••The Manner of the Sending of the Spirit••
	• The sending of the Spirit is not a change of place, but a manifestation of His presence.
	• The Spirit is everywhere, but He is said to be sent forth when He manifests His presence through special gifts.
• ••The Place of the Spirit••
	• The heart is the place or mansion of the Spirit.
	• The heart is the sink of sin, but the Spirit chooses it as His abode.
• ••The Beginning of Our New Birth••

• ••The Principal Part of Our Change••
	• The most principal part of our change or renovation is in the heart, where the Spirit abides.
	• The end of all teaching is "love out of a pure heart, good conscience, and faith unfeigned" (1 Tim. 1:5).
• ••The Beginning and Principal Part of God's Worship••
	• The beginning and principal part of God's worship is in the heart.
	• He that serves God in the righteousness of his heart, in peace, and joy in the Holy Ghost is accepted (Rom. 14:17).
• ••The Office of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit's office is to make believers cry, "Abba."
	• The means whereby this cry is caused are four works of the Spirit: conviction, illumination, regeneration, and sanctification.
• ••The Nature of the Cry "Abba"••
	• The cry "Abba" is a natural expression of the heart, caused by the Spirit's work.
	• The nature of the cry is one of affection and intimacy with God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. The Nature of Ignorance••
	• Ignorance of God is a lack of knowledge about God's nature and character.
	• This ignorance is a result of the fall of humanity's first parents.
	• It is a punishment for their sin and is passed down to all of humanity.

••2. The Consequences of Ignorance••
	• Ignorance of God leads to superstition and vanity.
	• It prevents people from knowing God as He truly is.
	• It is a sin that is punishable by God.

••3. The Sin of Ignorance••
	• All people are bound to know God by the first commandment.
	• Ignorance of God is a defect of the image of God in the mind.
	• This defect is a branch of original sin.

••4. The Degree of Sin••
	• The degree of sin is excused, but not the sin itself.
	• The Galatians' ignorance is a sin, but it is not the only sin they commit.

••5. The Spread of Ignorance••
	• Ignorance of God spreads to all of humanity as a punishment for the first sin.
	• It is not God's fault that people do not know Him, but rather the fault of humanity's first parents.

••6. The Greatness of the Sin••
	• Ignorance of God is a great and grievous sin.
	• It is the mother of superstition and idolatry.
	• It must be detested and sought to be known.

••7. The Warning to Ministers••
	• Ministers of the word must be careful to root out ignorance from the minds of the people.
	• They must plant the knowledge of God in its place.
	• This is a warning against the false teaching that "ignorance is the mother of devotion."

••8. The Second Sin of the Galatians••
	• The Galatians served false gods, which are not gods by nature.
	• This is a sin of idolatry, where people worship something that is not God.

••9. The Nature of False Gods••
	• False gods are set up in two ways:
		• When something that is not God is worshiped in place of the true God.
		• When people acknowledge the true God, but conceive Him in a false manner.

••10. The Consequences of False Worship••
	• False worship given to God presupposes a false opinion of God.
	• A false opinion of God sets up an idol or false god in the place of the true God.
	• This is a common sin that is bred in the bone, and is seen in various forms of idolatry.

â€¢ ••Promise of Life Everlasting••

â€¢ ••Distinct Knowledge of God••
