Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Knowing God••
	• Knowing God is essential for spiritual growth and understanding
	• It involves recognizing God's providence, will, and power in our lives

â€¢ ••Aspects of Knowing God••
	• Knowing God in respect of His providence (Ps. 56:8)
	• Knowing God in respect of His will (Rom. 12:2; Eph. 5:17)
	• Knowing God in respect of His power (Eph. 1:17)

â€¢ ••Effectual Knowledge••
	• This knowledge must be effectual and lively, working in us new affections and inclinations
	• It involves obeying God's commandments (1 John 2:4; Titus 2:14)

â€¢ ••The Importance of Knowing God••
	• We cannot serve God unless we know Him (John 17:3)
	• We must desire to know God to have eternal life (Jer. 9:24)

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Not Knowing God••
	• The earth's trembling is a sign of God's anger at our ignorance of Him
	• We must acknowledge God's majesty, anger, and justice, and humble ourselves for our sins

â€¢ ••Remembering God and Christ••
	• We must remember God and Christ, but not in a worldly or carnal manner
	• We must not remember Christ according to the flesh (2 Cor. 5:16)

â€¢ ••God's Election••
	• God's election is the root of all the gifts of God in us
	• We are elected to be holy, not for our faith or according to our faith (Eph. 1:4)

â€¢ ••The Execution of Election••
	• God's election involves calling, justifying, and sanctifying us (Titus 2:14)
	• We are made God's peculiar people through this process

â€¢ ••The Knowledge of God••
	• This knowledge involves recognizing God's special love and election (2 Tim. 2:19)
	• It is the foundation of our relationship with God

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Knowing God••
	• To have a right understanding of God and His will
	• To obey God's commandments and live a holy life (Rom. 12:2; Eph. 5:17)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Preventing Grace••
	• God must first acknowledge us before we can acknowledge Him (John 10:14)
	• Preventing grace is twofold: first, in our initial conversion, and second, after we are regenerate
	• Read Ezekiel 36:26 for more on preventing grace

â€¢ ••Works of Grace••
	• The works of God imprint His image in the hearts of those who belong to Him
	• This knowledge brings forth another knowledge in us, whereby we know God as our God
	• There is an election in God that works in the elect to choose God as their God

â€¢ ••Christ's Apprehension••
	• Christ first apprehends us, and this apprehension works faith in us
	• When Christ makes intercession for us in heaven, there is another intercession wrought in our hearts by the Spirit
	• We cry "Abba, Father" (Rom. 8:15, 26) as a result of Christ's intercession

â€¢ ••The Death of Christ••
	• The death of Christ has a virtue to work in us the death of sin
	• The Spirit of God seals us to the day of our redemption
	• We can know we belong to God if we find an impression of God's grace in us

â€¢ ••True Comfort••
	• Our faith does not save us because it is a perfect virtue, but because it apprehends a perfect object (Christ's obedience)
	• A weak faith that is rightly fixed on the true causes of salvation is still a true faith and justifies
	• The weakness of our faith shall not hinder our salvation

â€¢ ••God's Knowledge of Us••
	• God's knowledge of us is special, knowing all the elect by name (Ex. 33:17)
	• God's knowledge of us is perpetual and unchangeable, for whom God once knows, He never forgets (Isa. 49:15)

â€¢ ••The Galatians' Revolt••
	• The Galatians' estate in their revolt or apostasy is described as turning back to "impotent and beggarly rudiments"
	• These rudiments are circumcision, Jewish sacrifices, and all the ceremonies of the law of Moses

â€¢ ••The Rudiments••
	• The rudiments are considered three ways: with Christ, without Christ, and against Christ
	• With Christ, they are types and figures of Christ to come and signs of grace
	• Without Christ, they are used only for custom, and against Christ, they are esteemed as meritorious causes of salvation

â€¢ ••Paul's Warning••
	• Paul warns the Galatians that turning back to the rudiments is an intolerable offense
	• They are called impotent and beggarly rudiments because they are considered insufficient for salvation

â€¢ ••The Sufficiency of Christ••
	• Christ alone is sufficient for salvation, and the justification of a sinner is placed in Him alone
	• The rudiments are insufficient and should not be esteemed as meritorious causes of salvation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Galatians' apostasy••
	• They returned to the rudiments of the law
	• They served the law in opinion, conscience, and affection
	• This was a voluntary sin after knowing the truth

â€¢ ••The nature of the Galatians' sin••
	• It was a voluntary sin, but not due to obstinacy or malice
	• It was a particular sin, not a universal apostasy
	• It did not involve crucifying Christ or treading underfoot the Son of God

â€¢ ••The sin against the Holy Ghost••
	• This sin is a universal apostasy in respect of all articles of religion
	• It involves crucifying Christ and treading underfoot the Son of God
	• The Galatians' sin was not this kind of sin

â€¢ ••The significance of the Galatians' sin••
	• It was as if they had served false gods
	• It was a return to their old superstitions

â€¢ ••The conclusion of Paul's argument••
	• Christ stands alone in the work of redemption
	• He has no colleague, partner, deputy, or substitute

â€¢ ••The uniqueness of Christ's work••
	• He is a perfect savior, considering He is God and man
	• He needs no partner or deputy in His work of redemption

â€¢ ••The nature of Christ's work••
	• It is a compound work, human and divine
	• It involves the operations of both Christ's natures

â€¢ ••The limitations of human and angelic action••
	• No action pertaining to redemption can be performed by a mere creature
	• This is because Christ's work is unique and cannot be replicated

â€¢ ••The role of the apostles and ministers••
	• They remit sins by preaching and declaring remission
	• They do not procure our reconciliation with God

â€¢ ••The proper role of Christ••
	• He is the mediator who merits and confers pardon
	• He is the one who procures our reconciliation with God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Objection to Christ's exclusivity••
	• Some argue that baptism saves, so Christ is not the only one who can save
	• Answer: Baptism saves by signifying and sealing God's grace, but Christ is the one who effects our salvation

â€¢ ••Primacy of the Pope••
	• The Pope claims to have Christ's authority and power
	• But this is an "impotent and beggarly invention" because only God and Christ can perform such actions

â€¢ ••Roman Catholic additions to Christ's sacrifice••
	• The Mass is seen as a sacrifice for the living and the dead
	• Intercession of saints and angels is also emphasized
	• Human satisfactions and works are seen as meritorious
	• But these are "impotent and beggarly devices" because Christ's sacrifice is perfect and complete

â€¢ ••Clinging to Christ alone••
	• A papist argues that Protestants only cling to the body of the tree (Christ), while papists cling to both the body and the branches (works and traditions)
	• But it's safer to cling only to Christ, as the branches can be unreliable

â€¢ ••Setting up idols••
	• To set up anything other than Christ as a meritorious cause of salvation is to serve idols
	• This is because people put their trust in these things, making them idols

â€¢ ••Justification by works••
	• This doctrine maintains idolatry, as people put their trust in works rather than Christ
	• Works must be deified in order to merit at God's hand

â€¢ ••Distinction between latria and dulia••
	• Papists distinguish between worship (latria) and service (dulia) for God and saints
	• But this distinction falls to the ground, as Paul condemns even service to the heathen gods

â€¢ ••Returning to former errors••
	• Those who have given their names to God and Christ must not return to former errors
	• This is like looking back to Egypt while trying to reach the Promised Land

â€¢ ••Warning against outward profession vs. inner commitment••
	• Many in England have been delivered from popery, but still cling to worldly things
	• Our duty is to go forward in thought, conscience, will, affection, word, and deed, without looking back

â€¢ ••Apostasy of the Galatians••
	• Paul condemns the apostasy of the Galatians, who returned to the rudiments of the law
	• This is a warning to those who would return to former errors.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Criticism of Observing Days••
	• Paul criticizes the Galatians for observing Jewish and heathenish days
	• He condemns their observance of days, months, and years as a form of superstition

â€¢ ••Types of Observing Days••
	• Natural: observing days according to the course of the sun and moon
	• Civil: observing set times for husbandry, household affairs, and commonwealth
	• Ecclesiastical: observing set days for worship and order's sake
	• Superstitious: observing days with an opinion that they are bound to conscience or for good/bad success

â€¢ ••Ecclesiastical Observation of Days••
	• Observing set days for worship and order's sake is not forbidden
	• Examples include Jewish feasts and Christian observance of the Lord's Day

â€¢ ••Superstitious Observation of Days••
	• Jewish: observing days with an opinion that they are bound to conscience
	• Heathenish: observing days for good or bad success

â€¢ ••Paul's Intent••
	• Paul's intent is to condemn the Jewish and heathenish manner of observing days

â€¢ ••Objection: Romans 14:6••
	• Paul excuses the Romans for observing days, but notes it as a fault
	• The Galatians, however, observed days after being informed of their liberty in Christ

â€¢ ••The Galatians' Error••
	• They placed their salvation, in part, in the observation of days
	• They mixed the gospel with the law

â€¢ ••Objection: The Lord's Day••
	• Some argue that the Lord's Day was appointed by the apostles for order's sake
	• They argue that all days are equal and that the church has liberty to appoint the Sabbath on any day

â€¢ ••Response to Objection••
	• The Lord's Day was not appointed by the apostles, but rather is a Christian tradition
	• The church has authority to appoint the Sabbath, but it should not be based on superstition or Jewish law

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Paul's criticism of observing days is aimed at the Jewish and heathenish manner of doing so
	• The Galatians' error was in placing their salvation in the observation of days, rather than in Christ alone.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Sabbath Day••
	• The author argues that the Sabbath day is not just any day, but a day set apart for holy rest unto God.
	• The fourth commandment requires that every seventh day in the week be set apart for rest.

â€¢ ••The Lord's Day••
	• The author suggests that the Sabbath day in the New Testament is limited to the first day of the week, which is called the Lord's Day.
	• This day is dedicated and consecrated to the honor of Christ our Lord.

â€¢ ••Paul's Teaching••
	• Paul and the apostles observed the first day of the week as a Sabbath day (Acts 20:7).
	• Paul's teaching is that the first day of the week is the Lord's Day, and it is to be observed as a day of rest and worship.

â€¢ ••The Commandment••
	• The commandment to observe the Sabbath day is not wholly ceremonial, but contains a perpetual moral truth.
	• The words "Six days shalt thou labor, and the seventh day is the Sabbath of the Lord thy God" are moral and apply to all people.

â€¢ ••Popish Superstition••
	• The author criticizes the popish church for appointing many other Sabbaths besides the Lord's Day.
	• They bind men's consciences to the observation of their holy days, which is forbidden by Paul (Col. 2:16).

â€¢ ••Worship of God••
	• The popish church places the worship of God in the observation of their holy days, but this is vain (Matt. 15).
	• God is worshiped in vain by men's precepts, not by the observation of holy days.

â€¢ ••Festival Days••
	• The popish church places a great holiness in their festival days, more than in other days.
	• They dedicate many of their holy days to the honor of saints and angels, rather than to the honor of God.

â€¢ ••Protestant Observance••
	• Some Protestant churches do not observe holy days, following the example of the church in the apostles' days.
	• The Church of England observes holy days, but without the popish superstition.

â€¢ ••Keeping Holy Days••
	• The Protestant church keeps holy days only for order's sake, to bring people to church to hear God's word.
	• They do not place holiness or the worship of God in these days, but rather in the preaching of God's word.

â€¢ ••Cutting Off Superstition••
	• The Protestant church has cut off superstition in holy days, such as the conception and assumption of the Virgin Mary.
	• They retain the names of saints' days, but give no worship to saints, only to God alone.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Respect for the Lord's Day••
	• The Lord's Day is not respected by some people.
	• Men will not be dissuaded from attending fairs on that day.
• ••Avoiding Heathenish Practices••
	• Observing days of good and bad success according to the constellations is a heathenish fashion to be avoided.
	• This practice is condemned in the Galatians.
• ••Planetary Hours••
	• Men suppose that the hours of the day are ruled by the planets.
	• Some hours are thought to be good and lucky, while others are unlucky.
	• This is a heathenish conceit and should be avoided.
• ••Horoscope and Birth Sign••
	• The time of birth and the constellation of the heavens should not be respected.
	• This is not a reliable way to know what will happen in life.
• ••Critical Days and Climacterical Years••
	• The seventh and fourteenth days after a person becomes sick are not to be observed.
	• These days are grounded on the aspects of the moon, which are not to be regarded.
• ••Observation of the Signs••
	• The twelve signs of the zodiac are not to be observed as having different effects.
	• These signs are just twelve parts of a supposed heaven.
• ••Fear of God, Not the Stars••
	• We are to fear God, not the stars.
	• God's commandment is to "Fear not the signs of heaven" (Jer. 10:2).
• ••Limitations of Human Knowledge••
	• No man can learn the operation of the stars.
	• The lights and operations of the stars are mixed together in all places on earth.
• ••Operation of the Stars••
	• The operation of the stars is by their light, which has no operation but in heat or cold, moisture or dryness.
	• It is foolishness to ascribe the regiment of our affairs to the stars.
• ••Malignant and Unfortunate Stars••
	• It is a great oversight to hold some stars to be malignant and unfortunate in respect of us.
	• The stars are the creatures of God and their light serves for the good of man.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Ministers of the Word••
	• Must preach for the sake of gaining souls to God, not for personal gain or praise
	• Must be laborers and workmen, showing care and industry in their work
	• Must be watchmen, preserving and keeping those who are already called to God

â€¢ ••The Condition of the Church••
	• Visible churches on earth are subject to apostasy
	• There are four kinds of believers:
		- Those who hear the word without zeal (stony ground)
		- Those who hear, know, and approve the word
		- Those who hear, know, approve, and have a taste of the power of the word
		- Those who hear, know, approve, and keep the word (true believers)

â€¢ ••The Danger of Apostasy••
	• Three out of four believers in a visible church may fall away
	• True believers cannot fall away, but they can be surrounded by those who do

â€¢ ••The Importance of Laboring in the Lord••
	• Ministers must labor to be "sealed up to the day of our redemption"
	• We must labor to have good things in us, such as unfeigned faith in Christ

â€¢ ••The Vain Labor of Paul••
	• Paul's labor was in vain in respect of his own desire to save all the Galatians
	• It was in vain in respect of the whole body of the church, where many were hypocrites
	• However, it was not in vain in respect of the elect or the counsel of God

â€¢ ••Following God's Command••
	• We must follow God's command, whether we have good success or not
	• We must remember that our work is pleasing to God, even if it is in vain in respect of men

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• Paul says, "Be you as I, for I am even as you" (Galatians 4:12)
	• This means that we should not be estranged from one another, but rather be tender and humble

â€¢ ••The Nature of Friendship••
	• Paul's speech is like the proverb, "A man's friend is all one with himself"
	• This means that true friends are like one another, and should strive to be humble and tender with one another

â€¢ ••The Importance of Remembering God's Pleasure••
	• We must remember that our work is pleasing to God, even if it is in vain in respect of men
	• This should establish our minds against all events and circumstances

â€¢ ••The Call to Humility and Obedience••
	• We must labor to be humble and obedient to God's command
	• We must remember that our work is not in vain, even if it is in vain in respect of men.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's love for the Galatians••
	• He loves them as his own self
	• He wants them to love him as well
	• He commands them to be like him, but in a gentle and loving way

â€¢ ••The basis of Paul's love••
	• He has done no wrong to the Galatians
	• He has no reason to hate them
	• He wants to show them his love and tender care

â€¢ ••The importance of mutual love••
	• Teachers must show love to their people
	• People must show love to their teachers
	• This mutual love is essential for effective ministry

â€¢ ••How to show love to teachers••
	• Pray for them as you would for yourself
	• Value the work of the ministry
	• Receive wholesome doctrine to repair the image of God

â€¢ ••The use of moderation in reproof••
	• Paul shows moderation in his reproof of the Galatians
	• He tells them his mind plainly, but also shows his love for them

â€¢ ••Paul's meekness••
	• He quietly bears the crosses and wrongs laid upon him
	• He forgives those who wrong him
	• He is a perfect example of meekness, like Christ

â€¢ ••The importance of meekness••
	• We must exercise ourselves in meekness
	• We must have a sense of our spiritual poverty and faith in God's mercy

â€¢ ••Paul's willingness to suffer••
	• He is content to suffer any wrong to win souls to God
	• True ministers of the gospel must be willing to suffer for the sake of the gospel

â€¢ ••The Galatians' response to Paul••
	• They received Paul as an angel of God
	• They received him as Christ Jesus
	• They were willing to give up their eyes to give them to Paul

â€¢ ••Paul's relationship with the Galatians••
	• He is not their enemy, despite telling them the truth
	• He loves them and wants them to love him in return

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's condition••
	• He preached in weakness of the flesh
	• He preached the first (i.e., he was the first to preach to the Galatians)
	• He preached having the trial of himself in his own flesh

â€¢ ••The Galatians' love and reverence••
	• They did not despise him
	• They received him as an angel or as Christ himself
	• They would have plucked out their eyes to do him good

â€¢ ••The significance of Paul's infirmity••
	• It was a mean and base estate, without the show of human wisdom and authority
	• It was subject to many miseries
	• It was a necessary condition for the apostles to preach the gospel

â€¢ ••The order of God••
	• The word must be dispensed in the infirmity of man's flesh
	• This is for several reasons:
		- To prevent exalting teachers above their condition
		- To ascribe the work of conversion to God alone
		- To confound the wisdom of the world
		- To assure that the doctrine of the apostles is of God

â€¢ ••The nature of God's love••
	• It is shed abroad in the hearts of men
	• It is conferred and continued in us in the weakness of the flesh
	• It is made perfect through weakness

â€¢ ••The relationship between weakness and strength••
	• We are strongest when we are weakest
	• We should rejoice in our miseries and troubles
	• We should not think ourselves forsaken of God in times of distress

â€¢ ••The works of God••
	• They are in and by their contraries
	• This is the manner of God's dealing

â€¢ ••Paul's privilege••
	• He was the first to preach to the Galatians
	• He claims his privilege as a master-builder who laid the foundation of the church of Galatia

â€¢ ••The condition of God's church••
	• It is like a field where the husbandman sows good seed
	• The devil then comes and sows tares
	• This is evident in the church of Galatia

â€¢ ••The contrast between true and false apostles••
	• True apostles plant churches and sow good seed
	• False apostles corrupt churches and sow tares

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Trials and Afflictions••
	• We must not think it strange when we are afflicted
	• We must look for trials and be content when they come
	• Trials are a work of God to discover the corruption or grace in our hearts

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Trials••
	• To purge us and make us gold indeed
	• To reveal hidden corruption in our hearts
	• To test our faith, patience, and obedience

â€¢ ••The Importance of Soundness of Grace••
	• We must have soundness of grace to bear the trials of God
	• We must show forth some measure of faith, patience, and obedience

â€¢ ••Reverence for Paul and Christ••
	• The Galatians did not despise Paul in his base condition
	• They received Paul as an angel of God or as Christ Jesus

â€¢ ••The Authority of Apostles••
	• Apostles were called, taught, and inspired immediately by God
	• They were governed by the Spirit and could not err in their preaching and writing
	• They were to be heard even as Christ Himself

â€¢ ••The Role of Ordinary Teachers••
	• Ordinary teachers are to be heard as angels and as Christ, so far as they follow the doctrine of the apostles
	• They are called the "angels" of the covenant and "ambassadors in the stead of Christ"

â€¢ ••The Papacy and the Church••
	• The papists say we know Scripture is the Word of God by the testimony of the church
	• However, the principal means of assurance is the authority of the writers of Scripture

â€¢ ••The Goodness of God••
	• God does not speak to us in His majesty, but appoints men as His ambassadors
	• These men are to beseech us to be reconciled to God

â€¢ ••Fidelity and Obedience••
	• There must be fidelity in the church to the doctrine of the apostles
	• We must obey the doctrine of the apostles and follow their teaching

â€¢ ••The Importance of Authority••
	• The authority of the apostles and Scripture is to be maintained
	• We must consider the authority of the writers of Scripture and the doctrine of the apostles.

Here are 10 concise bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. The Role of Teachers••
• Teachers must only deliver the will of Christ.
• They must have a special care for holiness of life.
• They speak in the name and room of God.

••2. Reverence for Teachers••
• The people are to hear their teachers with all reverence.
• They should treat teachers as if they were hearing angels or Christ Himself.

••3. Comfort of the Ministry••
• The comfort of the ministry is sure and certain.
• It is as if an angel or Christ Himself came down to comfort us.
• We must truly turn to God and repent to receive this comfort.

••4. The Galatians' Love for Paul••
• The Galatians thought themselves happy to receive Paul's ministry.
• They would have parted with their own eyes for Paul's good.
• This shows the special love and reverence they had for Paul.

••5. True Happiness••
• True happiness stands in our reconciliation with God in Christ.
• This reconciliation is offered and given to us through God's word and promise.
• We receive it by turning to God and resting on His promise.

••6. The Kingdom of God••
• The kingdom of God is when we resign ourselves to God's will and word.
• The preaching of the word is the key to this kingdom.
• When we receive the word by faith, heaven is opened to us even in this life.

••7. Error of Philosophers••
• Philosophers have erred in placing happiness in honors, riches, pleasures, or civil virtue.
• These things do not bring true happiness.

••8. Error of Common People••
• Common people are deceived if they think true happiness stands in civil conversation.
• True happiness is not found in dealing justly with men, but in serving God.

••9. Natural Atheism••
• Many people think it a vain thing to serve God and hear His word.
• This natural atheism is a corruption in the heart of man.
• It leads people to think they cannot live by their religion.

••10. Importance of the Gospel••
• The only way to establish a kingdom or commonwealth is to plant the gospel there.
• The obedience of the gospel makes every man prosper in his trade, office, and calling.
• Living without the gospel makes people wretched and miserable.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Privilege to Preach••
	• He had the privilege to preach the truth without error
	• He preached with authority to correct rebellious offenders
	• He preached with diligence and dedication
	• He had the power to give extraordinary gifts of the Holy Spirit to new disciples

â€¢ ••The Measure of Love••
	• The command to love our neighbor as ourselves does not mean we must love ourselves first
	• We must love our neighbor as heartily and unfeignedly as our own selves
	• The measure of love is expressed in Christ's command to love one another as He loved us

â€¢ ••Loving Our Neighbor••
	• We must consider our neighbor as a special instrument of God
	• We are to love and prefer our neighbor before ourselves in certain cases
	• This is demonstrated by Paul's willingness to be accursed for the Israelites

â€¢ ••Speaking the Truth••
	• We must speak the truth to all men, as Paul did
	• We must be willing to hear the truth, even when it is against us
	• Self-love can make us hate those who speak the truth

â€¢ ••Self-Examination••
	• We must search our hearts and lives to know our own flaws
	• We must be willing to acknowledge our own weaknesses and vices

â€¢ ••Jealousy and Zeal••
	• The false apostles were jealous of Paul and wanted to exclude him from the love of the Galatians
	• This jealousy was pretended and not true
	• True jealousy is a spiritual marriage between Christ and His church

â€¢ ••The Importance of Love••
	• It is good to love earnestly and always in a good cause
	• We must not love only when someone is present, but always

â€¢ ••The Role of Ministers••
	• Ministers of Christ are friends of the bridegroom and suitors for Him
	• They are zealous for Christ and His church

â€¢ ••The Difference between True and Pretended Zeal••
	• True zeal is a spiritual marriage between Christ and His church
	• Pretended zeal is when men falsely pretend to love the church for Christ's sake

â€¢ ••Paul's Opposition to False Apostles••
	• Paul opposed the false apostles who wanted to exclude him from the love of the Galatians
	• He stood in opposition to them, even in the face of their jealousy and pretended love.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Zeal for the Galatians••
	• Paul's zeal for the Galatians is a good thing, as it is for Christ's sake.
	• He is jealous over them not only when he is present, but also when he is absent.

â€¢ ••The Scope of Paul's Message••
	• Paul is addressing a conceit of the Galatians, who might think their new teachers loved them exceedingly.
	• He answers by a comparison, showing that jealousy for the Galatians is good, but the false apostles' jealousy is amiss.

â€¢ ••The Use of Paul's Message••
	• Paul sets out the fashion of men in the world, who do good things for wrong ends.
	• This is seen in false teachers who preach the word for envy, contention, or profit.

â€¢ ••Doing Good for the Right Reasons••
	• We must do good for the right reasons, not just for ourselves.
	• We must set before us the will and commandment of God, and this must move us to do good.

â€¢ ••Conformity of Action and Motions••
	• Our outward actions must be conformable to the inward motions of our hearts.
	• Both our actions and motivations must be in line with God's will.

â€¢ ••Intending to Obey God••
	• We must directly intend to obey God in all things we do.
	• We must approve our hearts and doings to Him.

â€¢ ••Nature Can Counterfeit Grace••
	• Nature can counterfeit the grace of God, and hypocrites can do good things for wrong reasons.
	• This is seen in examples such as Pharaoh, Ahab, and Judas.

â€¢ ••Praying and Examining Our Hearts••
	• We must pray and examine our hearts, lest we be deceived in ourselves.
	• We must observe two things: a universal hatred of all sin, and a change and renewal in our minds, consciences, and affections.

â€¢ ••The Property of Envy and Ambition••
	• Envy and ambition are properties of the false teachers, who want to exclude Paul from the love of the Galatians.
	• This is seen in examples such as Joshua, John's disciples, and the disciples of Christ.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• We must be humble and willing to decrease, as seen in examples such as Moses and John.
	• We must be willing to give up our own desires and ambitions for the sake of God's will.

â€¢ ••The property of deceivers••
	• Make a division between pastors and people
	• Use jealousy as a pretense to manipulate others

â€¢ ••The apostle's jealousy••
	• A good jealousy that comes from a desire to serve Christ

â€¢ ••The importance of good ends••

	• Constantly striving for the good that is done

â€¢ ••The value of jealousy••

	• This jealousy is a sign of a desire to serve Christ and form Christ in others

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Jealousy and Love••
	• Paul's jealousy over the Galatians is good because it's in a good cause
	• He declares his love and desire for them, even in his absence

â€¢ ••The Meaning of "I Travail"••
	• The word "I travail" means the painful bearing of a woman in childbirth
	• It signifies Paul's ministerial pains and his desire to bring the Galatians back to the truth

â€¢ ••Paul's Motherly Affection••
	• Paul takes on the role of a mother, showing his tender and motherly affection to the Galatians
	• He rejoices in their prosperity and mourns their apostasy

â€¢ ••God's Love and Compassion••
	• God's love and compassion are greater than Paul's
	• God has pity and compassion on His children, even when they are weak and fall

â€¢ ••The Mercy of God••
	• Our weaknesses and falls do not abolish the mercy of God
	• God's mercy is illustrated by our weaknesses and falls

â€¢ ••The Dignity of the Ministry••
	• The ministry is an instrument appointed by God for the work of regeneration
	• Paul compares himself to a woman in travail, highlighting the dignity of the ministry

â€¢ ••The Possibility of Second Repentance••
	• If men fall after their first initial repentance, there is still a possibility of mercy and place for a second repentance
	• The parable of the prodigal son shows that those who fall from God may again be recovered through new repentance

â€¢ ••Objection: Second Regeneration••
	• Paul's second travail presupposes a second regeneration in the Galatians
	• If they are born again the second time, it raises questions about the nature of regeneration

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Ministry••
	• Paul's ministry is to help the Galatians become new creatures like Christ.
	• He travailed with them to restore the image of Christ in their hearts.

â€¢ ••The End of Paul's Ministry••
	• The end of Paul's ministry is to form Christ in the hearts of believers.
	• This involves two parts: a right knowledge of Christ and conformity to Christ.

â€¢ ••Conformity to Christ••
	• Conformity to Christ has two aspects: conformity in quality and conformity in practice.
	• Conformity in quality involves:
		- Conformity to the death of Christ (dying to sin and suffering with Christ).
		- Conformity to the resurrection of Christ (living a spiritual life).

â€¢ ••Conformity in Practice••
	• Conformity in practice involves:
		- Carrying oneself as a prophet (confessing Christ and teaching others).
		- Acting as a priest (offering oneself as a sacrifice to God).
		- Behaving as a spiritual king (ruling over one's own heart).

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Ministry••
	• The purpose of the ministry is to make sinful men become new creatures like Christ.
	• The doctrine that tends to this purpose is sound and wholesome.

â€¢ ••The Rule of Christ••
	• There is only one rule and order for all men: the rule of Christ.
	• This rule is to "take up thy cross and follow me."

â€¢ ••Degrees of God's Children••
	• There are two degrees of God's children:
		- Those who are begotten of God and Christ is formed in them.
		- Those who are begotten of God but are still unformed.

â€¢ ••Unformed Christians••
	• Unformed Christians are those who have not yet been fully informed about Christ.
	• Examples of unformed Christians include the apostles, Rahab, and the Corinthians.

â€¢ ••The Process of Conversion••
	• The conversion of a sinner is not wrought in one moment, but by little and little.
	• This process is like the development of an infant, where different parts are formed and developed over time.

â€¢ ••God's Work in Us••
	• God outwardly prevents us with various means to help us become new creatures like Christ.
	• This process involves growth, nourishment, and the development of different aspects of our being.

• ••Paul's Desire to be with the Galatians••

	• The earthquake and sickness of the past winter should stir us up to repentance and sorrow
	• We should consider the changes in the world and their impact on our lives

â€¢ ••Importance of Learning••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Argument••
	• Paul returns to his doctrine of justification by faith, without the works of the law
	• He uses the example of Abraham's family to illustrate this point

â€¢ ••The Argument's Structure••
	• The argument has four parts: a preface, a history of Abraham, the application of the history, and a conclusion
	• The preface is found in verse 21

â€¢ ••The Meaning of "Law"••
	• The word "law" can refer to the moral and ceremonial law of Moses, or the books of Moses (including Genesis)
	• In this context, "under the law" means holding oneself bound to fulfilling the law and seeking eternal life through it

â€¢ ••The Pride of Man••
	• Paul notes that the Galatians, like the Jews and some modern Christians, try to establish their own righteousness through the law
	• This pride is rooted in ignorance of the true scope of the law

â€¢ ••The Servile Disposition••
	• Paul notes that many people prefer to be in bondage under the law rather than in perfect liberty under God's grace
	• This is seen in people who claim to follow Christ but still live according to their own desires

â€¢ ••The Cause of Spiritual Pride••
	• Paul identifies ignorance of the true scope of the law as the cause of spiritual pride and the servile disposition
	• The Galatians, like some modern Christians, mistakenly believe that observing the law gives life and justice

â€¢ ••The History of Abraham••
	• Paul considers three aspects of Abraham's story: his taking of two wives, the event that resulted from this, and the condition of his sons

â€¢ ••Marriage and the Law••
	• Paul notes that marriage is the indivisible conjunction of one man and one woman
	• Christ teaches that this is the way God created marriage (Matthew 19)

â€¢ ••The Condition of Abraham's Sons••
	• Paul will apply the story of Abraham's sons to the Galatians in the next section

â€¢ ••The Conclusion••
	• Paul will conclude his argument by applying the story of Abraham's sons to the Galatians and emphasizing their freedom from the law.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Polygamy in the Bible••
	• The Bible does not explicitly condone polygamy, but rather tolerates it as a lesser evil.
	• Some learned men argue that Abraham and the patriarchs had a dispensation from God to marry multiple wives.

â€¢ ••God's Commandments••
	• God commanded that a king should not multiply his wives (Deut. 17:17).
	• The child of a hated wife (second wife) should be the heir if it is the firstborn (Deut. 21:15).

â€¢ ••Occasions for Polygamy••
	• The patriarchs wanted to multiply their posterity to ensure the Messiah's lineage.
	• The common custom of men in Eastern countries to marry multiple wives.

â€¢ ••Common Error and Ignorance••
	• A common custom bred a common error, leading to ignorance about the sinfulness of polygamy.
	• This error was perpetuated by the patriarchs and their descendants.

â€¢ ••Repentance for Unknown Sins••
	• Known sins require particular repentance, but unknown or unconsidered sins may be repented of with a general repentance.
	• This is illustrated in Psalm 19:12.

â€¢ ••Abraham's Situation••
	• Abraham took Hagar with Sarah's consent, but this does not excuse his actions.
	• Marriage is more than a civil contract, and God's authority is involved.

â€¢ ••Polygamy vs. Adultery••
	• Polygamy is not the same as adultery, as the patriarchs did not take wives for lustful reasons.
	• Their polygamy was a result of a conscience not rightly informed about God's ordinance.

â€¢ ••The Two Sons of Abraham••
	• Abraham's two wives, Sarah and Hagar, bore him two sons, Ishmael and Isaac.
	• These two sons were ordained by God as types of true believers and hypocrites.

â€¢ ••The Condition of the Two Sons••
	• Ishmael was born of a bondwoman (Hagar) and was therefore a bondman.
	• Isaac was born of a free woman (Sarah) and was therefore a free man and the heir.

â€¢ ••Types of Wives••
	• There were two types of wives in ancient times: mistresses of the house (joint governors) and bondwomen (servants or strangers).
	• Sarah was a mistress of the house, while Hagar and Keturah were bondwomen.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Birth of Ishmael and Isaac••
	• Ishmael was born through Hagar, a handmaid of Sarah
	• Isaac was born through Sarah, according to the promise of God
	• Ishmael was born according to the flesh, in bondage, while Isaac was born according to the promise of God

â€¢ ••The Condition of the Godly••
	• The godly intend and desire the best things, but fail in the matter of doing
	• The Spirit stirs up good motions, but the flesh corrupts them
	• This is why we must humble ourselves for our best works

â€¢ ••The Importance of Patience••
	• We must not make haste to accomplish our desires, but wait for God's leisure
	• We must live in subjection to God's providence
	• Sarah's haste could not prevent God's providence, and Ishmael was born in bondage

â€¢ ••The Virtue of the Promise of God••
	• When the promise of God is mixed with our faith, it makes things possible that are otherwise impossible
	• We must rest on the promises of God in silence and patience
	• Our desire shall indeed be accomplished when we wait on God's promise

â€¢ ••The Two Testaments••
	• The two mothers, Hagar and Sarah, represent the two testaments
	• Hagar represents the Old Testament, which is the covenant of works
	• Sarah represents the New Testament, which is the covenant of faith

â€¢ ••The Old Testament as Bondage••
	• The Old Testament, represented by Hagar, makes all who look to be justified and saved by the works of the law bondmen
	• It is a place of bondage, where people are enslaved to the law

â€¢ ••Hagar as Sinai••
	• Hagar is not just a person, but also represents the place of Sinai, where the law was delivered to the Israelites
	• Sinai is a place of bondage, where people are enslaved to the law

â€¢ ••Jerusalem as Bondage••
	• Jerusalem, represented by Hagar, is in spiritual bondage, refusing Christ and looking to be saved by the law
	• It makes bondmen, teaching the law and enslaving people to it

â€¢ ••The Allegory of the Two Mothers••
	• The story of Hagar and Sarah is an allegory, representing the two testaments
	• It teaches us about the condition and effect of the Old and New Testaments

â€¢ ••The Use of Allegory••
	• The story of Hagar and Sarah is said by allegory, meaning one thing is said and another thing is meant
	• The allegory teaches us about the two testaments and their effects on people.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Scripture has multiple senses••
	• Literal sense: can be proper (straightforward) or figurative (using borrowed terms)
	• Spiritual senses: allegorical, tropological, and anagogical

â€¢ ••Allegorical sense••
	• Applies Old Testament events to New Testament events
	• Example: Jerusalem can represent the church of the New Testament

â€¢ ••Tropological sense••
	• Applies Scripture to moral teachings
	• Example: Jerusalem can represent a well-ordered state

â€¢ ••Anagogical sense••
	• Applies Scripture to eternal life
	• Example: Jerusalem can represent the estate of eternal life

â€¢ ••There is only one full and entire sense of Scripture••
	• The literal sense is the primary meaning
	• Other senses are applications or uses of Scripture

â€¢ ••Figurative language in Scripture••
	• Used to convey spiritual truths
	• Examples: the Song of Solomon, the 45th psalm, the book of Daniel, and the Revelation

â€¢ ••Parables and allegories••
	• Used to convey spiritual truths
	• Examples: the parables of the Old and New Testaments

â€¢ ••Determining when Scripture speaks properly or by figure••
	• If the literal meaning is against reason, faith, or good manners, it is figurative

â€¢ ••Examples of figurative language••
	• Jesus' statement "I am the true vine" is figurative
	• Jesus' statement "Take, eat, this is my body" is figurative

â€¢ ••Critique of excessive allegorizing••
	• Some interpreters make too much of allegory, turning the entire Bible into allegory
	• This can lead to absurd interpretations and undermine the authority of Scripture

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Two Testaments••
	• The law and the gospel are two distinct testaments
	• The law is the covenant of works, promising life eternal to those who follow the law
	• The gospel is the covenant of grace, promising remission of sins and life everlasting through faith in Christ

â€¢ ••Differences between the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law requires inward and perfect righteousness, while the gospel offers imputed justice through faith in Christ
	• The law promises life upon condition of works, while the gospel promises life everlasting upon condition of faith in Christ

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law was written in tables of stone, while the gospel is written in the fleshy tables of our hearts
	• The law was in nature by creation, while the gospel is above nature and was revealed after the fall

â€¢ ••Mediators of the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law had Moses as a mediator, while the gospel has Christ as the mediator
	• The law was dedicated by the blood of beasts, while the gospel was dedicated by the blood of Christ

â€¢ ••The Two Testaments are Distinct••
	• The law and the gospel are two in nature, substance, or kind
	• The difference between the two lies not in the presence or absence of the Spirit

â€¢ ••Papists' Error••
	• Papists teach that the law and the gospel are one law for substance, with the difference being the presence or absence of the Spirit
	• This is false, as the two testaments are distinct and the difference lies in their nature and substance

â€¢ ••Justification••
	• The law propounds only one way of justification, while the gospel offers a second way of justification through faith in Christ
	• The doctrine that combines both is compounded of both

â€¢ ••God's Providence••
	• God uses the things He does not approve to signify the greatest mysteries of our religion
	• This is the foundation of our patience and a means of true comfort

â€¢ ••The Law and the Gospel••
	• The law came from Mount Sinai, while the gospel came from Zion or Jerusalem
	• The law genders to bondage, making people bondmen who look to be saved and justified by works

â€¢ ••The Ministry of Death••
	• The law is called the ministry of death, as it reveals sin and the punishment thereof, leading to everlasting death
	• The law convinces all men of their sins and of their deserved condemnation

â€¢ ••The Law vs. The Gospel••
	• The law leads to bondage, while the gospel leads to life
	• The law is an instrument of the Spirit, but only an occasion for God's grace

â€¢ ••The Free Jerusalem••

â€¢ ••The Church is not a visible company under one pastor••

â€¢ ••The Church's glory and beauty are inward••
	• The church's inward glory and beauty are what make it the church, not its outward appearance

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Where to find the mother church••
	• The author asks where to find the true mother church
	• The advocates of the popish church say it is the Church of Rome

â€¢ ••Interpretation of Scripture••
	• The Church of Rome claims to have sure and certain interpretation through tradition, councils, and fathers
	• The author argues that Scripture is its own best interpreter, and that the analogy of faith, words, scope, and circumstances of a passage are the best means of interpretation

â€¢ ••Public vs. private interpretation••
	• The author argues that the Church of Rome's interpretations are not always public or certain
	• The author claims that their own interpretations are based on Scripture and are therefore sure and certain

â€¢ ••Consent of fathers and councils••
	• The author argues that they can justify their interpretation of Scripture by the consent of fathers and councils, just like the Church of Rome

â€¢ ••Divine and infallible authority••
	• The Church of Rome claims to have divine and infallible authority in matters of religion
	• The author argues that the canonical Scriptures of the prophets and apostles are the source of divine and infallible authority

â€¢ ••Ministry vs. authority••
	• The author argues that the church has no divine and infallible authority distinct from the authority of Scripture
	• The church's ministry is to speak in the name of God according to the written Word

â€¢ ••Limitations of opinion and affection••
	• The Church of Rome claims to have limitations on opinion and affection
	• The author argues that they suffer themselves to be limited by the analogy of faith and the written Word, and that the Church of Rome uses false means of limitation

â€¢ ••The Roman religion••
	• The Church of Rome draws the multitude, but does not turn them from darkness to light
	• The author argues that the Roman religion is a natural religion, but not a spiritual one

â€¢ ••Alterations of religion••
	• The Church of Rome claims that there have only been two alterations of religion
	• The author argues that there will be a third alteration, a departure from the faith that will occur before the end

â€¢ ••Judge to end controversies••
	• The Church of Rome claims to have a judge to end controversies
	• The author argues that Christ is the ultimate judge, and that Scripture is the best means of resolving controversies

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome is not the true church••
	• It abolishes the second and tenth commandments
	• It overturns articles of faith, including the nature of Christ and the Trinity
	• It joins partners and associates with Christ, which is contrary to Christian doctrine

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome is not a true church because of its ministers••
	• The offices of the first gospel ministers were ordinary, not new
	• They departed from common abuses, not their callings
	• The Church of Rome's ministers have new callings, making them schismatics

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome's baptism is not true baptism••
	• Baptism in the papacy is not a mark of a true church
	• The Church of Rome overturns the inward justification of a sinner by imputation of Christ's obedience
	• Baptism must be joined with the preaching of the gospel to be a true mark of a church

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome's antiquity and succession are not marks of a true church••
	• Antiquity and succession are not enough to make a church true
	• The kingdom of darkness also has antiquity, succession, universality, and unity

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome is a stepmother, not a true mother••
	• The Lord says "Come out of her, my people" (Rev. 18)
	• The true church is to be sought in the visible churches with certain marks

â€¢ ••The certain marks of a true church are three••
	• The preaching of the word of God out of the writings of the prophets and apostles with obedience
	• True invocation of God the Father in the only name of Christ by the assistance of the Spirit
	• The right use of the sacraments, baptism and the Lord's Supper

â€¢ ••The church is called our mother, but not in the sense that its commandments must be obeyed••
	• The precepts of the father and mother must be one
	• The church is called the mother of all true believers, not of wicked men

â€¢ ••The church is our mother, teaching us to be born again••
	• We must despise our first birth and seek to be born again unto God
	• We must feed on the milk of the word and suck the breast of our mother

â€¢ ••The true church is a great privilege••
	• It is a new birth, a new creation
	• It is a great blessing to be a member of the new Jerusalem

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome is not the true church, despite its claims••
	• The Church of Rome is a stepmother, not a true mother
	• The true church is to be sought in the visible churches with certain marks

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Church's Relationship with Scripture••
	• The Church of Rome claims absolute power to judge Scripture and its sense.
	• However, the apostle Paul, guided by the Spirit, follows the rule of the written Word.

â€¢ ••The Authority of the Apostles••
	• Paul's authority is divine and infallible due to his guidance by the Spirit.
	• He uses Scripture to settle controversies, as seen in his dispute with false apostles.

â€¢ ••The Condition of the Church••
	• The Christian church is "barren" because it bears no spiritual children to God.
	• This is true in three senses:
		- By nature, the church is weak and unable to produce spiritual fruit.
		- Historically, the church was small and struggling until the Spirit was poured out after Christ's ascension.
		- In the latter times, faith will be scarce on earth.

â€¢ ••The Church's Sign of Barrenness••
	• The church is barren because it neither brings forth spiritual children nor bears fruit.
	• This is a sign of its spiritual condition.

â€¢ ••The Church's Desolation••
	• The church is "desolate" because it appears to be without a husband (Christ) due to the cross and affliction.
	• This is true in its outward estate, but not in its inward estate.

â€¢ ••The Church's Twofold Estate••
	• The church has two estates: inward and outward.
	• The inward estate is glorious and never desolate, characterized by true knowledge of God, comfort, and spiritual gifts.
	• The outward estate is visible and may be in desolation, characterized by public ministry, sacraments, and government according to God's word.

â€¢ ••Examples of the Church's Desolation••
	• The church in paradise after the fall of Adam and Eve.
	• The Israelites at Mount Horeb when they worshiped the golden calf.
	• The church in Elijah's time.
	• The church after Christ's ascension, when all the earth worshiped the beast.

â€¢ ••The Church's Visible Head••
	• The church is not a visible estate or company of men under one visible head.
	• This is because its outward estate may be in desolation.

â€¢ ••The Church's Relationship with Christ••
	• The church is "barren" because it bears no spiritual children to God.
	• Christ is the husband of the church, and His presence is what makes the church fruitful.

â€¢ ••The Church's Future Hope••
	• The church's barrenness and desolation are temporary.
	• Christ will find faith on earth in the latter times, and the church will be fruitful and glorious.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Church's Condition••
	• The church is married to God, her husband (Ezek. 16:8-9; Hos. 2:19)
	• This is a condition of the Jewish church, but also applies to other churches, including the Church of England

â€¢ ••The Church's Duty••
	• Dedicate bodies and souls to God and Christ (Ps. 45:11)
	• Adorn and trim ourselves with grace to please God (Ps. 45:11)
	• Subject ourselves to Christ and His laws (1 Cor. 11:7)

â€¢ ••The Church's Promise••

	• The church is a mother of all believers, both Jews and Gentiles (Gal. 4:26)

â€¢ ••The Church's Joy••
	• The church should rejoice in the redemption of Christ and the fruit thereof
	• The church should rejoice in the conversion of sinners to God
	• The foundation of all joy lies in reconciliation with God in Christ

â€¢ ••The Use of Joy••
	• Religion does not abolish mirth, but rectifies it
	• True and perfect joy comes from being in God's favor and reconciled to God through Christ

â€¢ ••The Source of Joy••
	• Our first and principal joy should be that we are in God's favor and reconciled to God through Christ
	• All other petty joys should flow from this and be suitable to it

â€¢ ••The Nature of Joy••
	• Joy is mixed with sorrow in this life
	• We must rejoice in trembling, as the paschal lamb was eaten with sour herbs (Ps. 2:11)

â€¢ ••The Call to Joy••
	• The church is called to break forth and rejoice in the midst of present grief
	• Joy is sown for those who are upright in heart (Ps. 97:11)

â€¢ ••The Angels' Joy••
	• The angels in heaven greatly rejoice at the conversion of a sinner
	• The angels rejoice at the return of the prodigal son, as symbolized by the fat calf being killed.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Measure of Joy••
	• We should be moderate in our earthly joys, lest we surfeit.
	• However, in spiritual joys, we should rejoice without measure.

â€¢ ••The Children of Promise••
	• Isaac is a figure of all true believers, the children of God.
	• The promise made to Abraham is the same promise made to the church.

â€¢ ••The Cause of Election and Adoption••
	• The mere grace of God is the cause of our election and adoption.
	• The promise of God makes us God's children, not anything in us.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Election and Adoption••
	• Election and adoption are according to God's mere grace, not our faith or obedience.
	• We cannot elect ourselves to be children of God through our own freewill and faith.

â€¢ ••The Absolute Will of God••
	• The children of God are called the children of the promise, which is absolute and effectual.
	• The absoluteness of God's will cannot be resisted.

â€¢ ••The Power of God's Promise••
	• The promise of God is joined with the infinite power of God.
	• God makes men to do that which He commands, without all resistance.

â€¢ ••The Liberty of the Will••
	• The liberty of the will is free from compulsion, not necessity.
	• The determination of man's will by the will of God is perfect liberty.

â€¢ ••The Perpetual Enmity••
	• There is a perpetual enmity and opposition between true believers and hypocrites.
	• The world hates those who are chosen out of the world.

â€¢ ••The Example of Abraham's Family••
	• Ishmael (born after the flesh) persecuted Isaac (born after the Spirit).
	• This is an example of the opposition between true believers and hypocrites.

â€¢ ••The Answer to an Objection••
	• We are hated by the Jews, but this is not a reason to doubt our status as children of promise.
	• The opposition between true believers and hypocrites is a perpetual enmity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Who persecutes?••
	• Carnal Ishmaelites, those who are of the same religion and family as Isaac
	• Examples: Jews persecuted their own prophets, Thessalonians were persecuted by their own countrymen

â€¢ ••Who are persecuted?••
	• Spiritual men, the children of the promise
	• They suffer wrong, but do not do wrong
	• They are blessed for suffering wrong for a good cause (Matt. 5:10)

â€¢ ••Kind of persecution••
	• Scorning or mocking
	• This can be a form of murder (Matt. 5:22)
	• Examples: Cain's contempt for Abel, Ishmael's mocking of Isaac

â€¢ ••Hatred of God's grace••
	• The beginning of all persecution
	• Deriding the grace of God is as much as spoiling goods and seeking lives
	• Example: Cain hated Abel because of the grace of God in him (1 John 3:12)

â€¢ ••Persecution of Christ••
	• He was mocked for His confidence in God (Ps. 22:8; Matt. 27:43)
	• The children of Bethel mocked Elisha, calling him "bald pate" (2 Kings 2:24)

â€¢ ••Profane scorning••
	• Used to mock those who practice a pure and precise religion
	• A sign of a lack of God's grace among people
	• To be lamented and avoided

â€¢ ••Scriptural basis••
	• The words "Cast out the bondwoman" are from Scripture (Gen. 21:12)
	• They were uttered by Sarah, but approved by God

â€¢ ••Sarah's words••
	• She speaks as the voice and mouth of God
	• Her words are to be esteemed as the commandment of God
	• Her case is extraordinary and not to be followed

â€¢ ••Use of Scripture••
	• All carnal hypocrites, mockers of God's grace, shall be cast out
	• This is a warning to those who hate the children of promise

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Persecution is a result of hatred and contempt for God's grace
	• Those who mock and scorn the children of promise shall be cast out
	• The children of promise are blessed for suffering wrong for a good cause

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••I. Repentance••
	• The people of God should repent of their mocking and become lovers of God's grace.
	• This is in line with Christ's example (Mark 10:21).
• ••II. Consolation••
	• The persecution of the people of God will not be perpetual.
	• The wicked will not have power over the righteous (Ps. 125:3).
• ••III. Rejection of the Law••
	• Those who rely on the law and their own works for salvation are rejected by God.
	• This is illustrated by the story of Hagar and Ishmael (Genesis).
• ••IV. Children of the Free Woman••
	• Those who are children of the promise are children of the free woman, not the bondwoman.
	• This means they are justified and saved by faith, not by works of the law.
• ••V. Indentured Servitude••
	• The concept of indentured servitude is used to illustrate the relationship between God and humanity.
	• Children born to servants are freed from bondage and considered legitimate.
• ••VI. The Belief••
	• The Apostles' Creed is likely the belief being referred to.
	• This creed is a statement of faith that is widely accepted.
• ••VII. Physical and Spiritual Bodies••
	• The concept of physical bodies and their measurements is used to illustrate the nature of humanity.
	• The language of essence, accident, and substance is used to describe the relationship between God and humanity.
• ••VIII. The Redeemer••
	• The concept of a redeemer is used to describe Jesus Christ.
	• The Hebrew word for redeemer implies a kinsman.
• ••IX. Worship and Honor••
	• The distinction between latria (worship of God alone) and dulia (honor given to people) is made.
	• This distinction is used to illustrate the proper way to worship God.
• ••X. Salvation and Justification••
	• Salvation and justification are not achieved through works of the law, but through faith in God.
	• This is illustrated by the story of Christ's purchase of humanity's liberty.

â€¢ ••Textual Variations••
	• The Geneva Bible (1562) reads "But it is a good thing to love earnestly always in a good thing."
	• Perkins' text diverges from the Geneva Bible in various places.

â€¢ ••Greek Manuscripts••
	• Perkins refers to different readings in various Greek manuscripts of the New Testament.
	• The Greek word for "you" (á½‘Î¼á¾¶Ï‚) differs from the word for "us" by only one letter.

â€¢ ••Biblical References••
	• Perkins references various biblical passages, including Isaiah 24:16, Jeremiah 4:19, and 1 Corinthians 5:1-2.

â€¢ ••Theological Terms••
	• Perkins uses terms such as "counterfeit" (copy or imitation), "drift" (aim or intent), and "foppery" (foolishness).

â€¢ ••Perkins' Commentary••
	• Perkins comments on verbs used in a Latin translation of Scripture.
	• He uses terms such as "justiciaries" (those acting on the principles of the law) and "genders" (begets, produces offspring).

â€¢ ••Scriptural Interpretation••
	• Perkins interprets Scripture in various ways, including the use of the definite article in Greek.
	• He notes that the Geneva Bible reads "But as then he that was born after the flesh persecuted him that was born after the spirit, even so it is now."

â€¢ ••Chapter 5 of Galatians••
	• The chapter begins with a repetition of the principal conclusion of the epistle.
	• Perkins notes that the words are a collection and inference of the conclusion of the last argument used in the last chapter.

â€¢ ••The Nature of Bondage••
	• Perkins explains that our spiritual bondage stands in three things: bondage under sin, the sign of it, and the use.
	• He notes that bondage under sin is a result of original sin, which has two parts: guiltiness in the first offense of Adam and the disposition of all the powers of the soul to all manner of evil.

â€¢ ••The Sign of Bondage••
	• Perkins notes that the sign of bondage is the yoke of bondage, which is a repetition of the principal conclusion of the epistle.

â€¢ ••The Use of Bondage••
	• Perkins explains that the use of bondage is to be entangled again with the yoke of bondage, which is contrary to the liberty in Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••Bondage to Sin••

• ••Definition of Bondage••
	• A rebellious disposition that tempts and draws a person to sin
	• A tyrant that reigns over the soul, making it impossible to do good
• ••Types of Bondage••
	• Bondage under Satan, who rules in the heart and blinds the mind
	• Bondage under an evil conscience, which accuses and judges the heart
	• Bondage under the wrath of God and the fear of eternal death
• ••Signs of Bondage••
	• A life led in sin, with a pattern of sinful behavior
	• A life led according to the customs and fashions of the world

••Deliverance from Bondage••

• ••Recognizing Bondage••
	• We must learn to see, feel, acknowledge, and bewail our bondage
	• Deliverance belongs only to those who know themselves to be captives
• ••Prayer for Deliverance••
	• We must pray earnestly for deliverance from bondage
	• We must be willing to labor and travail for freedom
• ••Detesting Sin••
	• We must learn to detest everything that tends to bondage
	• We must be willing to give up our own desires and wills
• ••Contentment with Affliction••
	• We must be content with any affliction that God lays on us
	• We must recognize that we are by nature slaves of sin and Satan

••Christian Liberty••

• ••Definition of Christian Liberty••
	• A spiritual right or condition that pertains to the conscience
	• A deliverance from misery and freedom in good things
• ••Author of Christian Liberty••
	• Christian liberty is a gift from God, given through Christ
• ••Persons to Whom Liberty Belongs••
	• Christian liberty belongs to the people of God, who are in Christ
• ••Duty Touching Liberty••
	• We must learn to use our liberty in a way that honors God
	• We must be willing to give up our own desires and wills for the sake of Christ

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••Christian Liberty••

• ••Deliverance from the Law••
	• Freedom from the rigor of the law and its curse
	• Ability to appeal to God's mercy and grace for pardon
• ••Conscience Settled••
	• God accepts the will and endeavor to believe, repent, and obey
	• He spares those who fear Him and strive to do good
• ••Deliverance from Ceremonial Law••
	• Freedom from the bondage of human tradition
	• Ability to live without the burden of unnecessary rules
• ••Deliverance from Sin••
	• Freedom from the tyranny and dominion of sin
	• Ability to live under the power of God's grace

••Christian Liberty in Good Things••

• ••Freedom in Voluntary Service••
	• Ability to serve God in righteousness and holiness without fear
	• Gift of the free Spirit of God, which establishes us in liberty
• ••Freedom from the Law••
	• The law is not given to the righteous man, who is a law to himself
	• Ability to freely do good duties without being bound by the law
• ••Freedom in the Use of Creatures••
	• All things are pure to the pure
	• Dominion over creatures lost by Adam is restored by Christ
• ••Freedom from Bondage••
	• The service of God is perfect liberty, not bondage
	• The more we are bound to obedience, the freer we are

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Christian Liberty••
	• Refers to the freedom to come unto God the Father in the name of Christ
	• Allows for prayer to be heard by God
	• Is a result of Christ's blood making a way for us to enter heaven
• ••Characteristics of Christian Liberty••
	• Exempts the conscience from the power of all creatures, men, and angels
	• Allows for the free use of all God's creatures
	• Is not bound by the laws and traditions of the church
• ••Magistracy and Christian Liberty••
	• Magistrates' authority deals with the use of Christian liberty, not the liberty itself
	• Magistrates restrain the abuse and moderate the over-common use of God's creatures for the common good
• ••Conscience and Christian Liberty••
	• Conscience is exempt from the power of all creatures, men, and angels
	• The laws and traditions of the church do not bind conscience as truly and certainly as the Word of God
• ••Objections to Christian Liberty••
	• "Be subject to the higher powers for conscience" (Rom. 13:5)
		- Answer: Conscience is in respect of the law of God, not the laws of the magistrate
	• "Obey them that have the oversight of you, and be subject" (Heb. 13:17)
		- Answer: We must be subject to them because they deliver the Word of God
	• A thing indifferent becomes necessary upon the commandment of the magistrate
		- Answer: Necessity is two-fold: external and internal. The law of the magistrate only makes a thing indifferent necessary in respect of external necessity.
• ••The Use of Christian Liberty••
	• The apostles made a law that the Gentiles should abstain from certain things, but later said that all things are lawful
	• The intent was not to bind the law, but only in the case of offense
• ••Comfort for Believers••
	• Nothing can hurt believers, and no evil can befall them
	• All things shall in the end turn to their good, though in reason and sense they seem hurtful
• ••Duties of Christian Liberty••
	• To embrace, love, and make much of Christian liberty
	• To use Christian liberty in a way that honors God and avoids offense
	• To trust in God's sovereignty and goodness, even in difficult circumstances

â€¢ ••The Means of Christian Liberty••

	• The Bible is the charter that contains our liberties

â€¢ ••The Author of Christian Liberty••

â€¢ ••The Danger of Hypocrisy••

â€¢ ••The Importance of Gratitude••

	• Not being thankful is a height of wickedness

â€¢ ••Christian Liberty••
	• Is a fundamental aspect of Christianity
	• Frees us from the law in three ways: condemnation, obedience, and justification

â€¢ ••Circumcision••

â€¢ ••False Apostles••

â€¢ ••Condemnation by Works••

	• Those who maintain this doctrine cannot be saved

â€¢ ••The Importance of Faith••
	• Faith is the foundation of Christian liberty

â€¢ ••The Call to Stand Fast••
	• Christians are called to stand fast in their faith and defend Christian liberty

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Justification by the Law is Not Justified by the Law••
	• Paul argues that justification by the law is not justified by the law (v. 4)
	• This is because the law is not a means of justification, but rather a means of condemnation (Gal. 3:10)

â€¢ ••Circumcision is an Obligation to the Whole Law••
	• Circumcision is not just a physical act, but also an obligation to keep the whole law (v. 3)
	• This includes the ceremonial, judicial, and moral law

â€¢ ••Works of Nature vs. Works of Grace••
	• Paul argues that even works of grace (regeneration) are not a means of justification (v. 4)
	• The Galatians were regenerate, but still looked to be justified by works of nature (the law)

â€¢ ••Adding to Christ's Merit is Unprofitable••
	• Adding human merits and satisfactions to Christ's merit makes Him unprofitable (v. 4)
	• This is because Christ must be a perfect Savior, or no Savior at all

â€¢ ••Popish Religion is Damnable••
	• The popish religion (Catholicism) is a damnable religion because it adds human merits and satisfactions to Christ's merit (v. 4)
	• This makes the death of Christ void and dishonors Christ

â€¢ ••Popish Religion is Not to be Tolerated••
	• Popish religion is not to be tolerated where it may be abolished (v. 4)
	• It is to be wished that it were banished from town and country

â€¢ ••Students Should be Warned Against Popish Writers••
	• Students should be warned with great circumspection to read popish writers (v. 4)
	• No good can be looked for from a religion that makes Christ unprofitable

â€¢ ••Contentment with Christ Alone••
	• We are to content ourselves with Christ alone and His works, merits, and satisfactions (v. 4)
	• In Him we are complete (Col. 2:10)

â€¢ ••The Apostles' Authority••
	• The apostles had the divine and infallible assistance of the Spirit in writing and preaching (v. 5)
	• This must be held as a principle in religion, and denying it leads to no certainty of the Bible

â€¢ ••The Consequence of Justification by the Law••
	• Those who are justified by the law are abolished from Christ (v. 4)
	• This means that Christ is become an idle and empty Christ unto them

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Argument Against Justification by Works••
	• Paul argues that if you are justified by the law, you are abolished from Christ and fallen from grace.
	• This is a thunderbolt against the popish church and its religion.

â€¢ ••Papists' Response••
	• Papists argue that the words are to be understood of works of the law that are from nature and go before faith, not of works that are from grace and follow faith.
	• They claim that such works are from Christ and stand with Him.

â€¢ ••Paul's Rebuttal••
	• Paul argues that his words are to be understood of all works of the law, whether they are from nature or from grace.
	• He points out that the Galatians were already regenerate persons when he wrote to them, and therefore they were not justified by works of nature.

â€¢ ••Scriptural Support••
	• Paul cites Ephesians 2:9-10, which says that we are not saved by works that God has ordained.
	• He also cites Titus 3:5, which says that we are saved by God's mercy, not by works of righteousness.

â€¢ ••The Church of Rome's Error••
	• The Church of Rome is no church because it maintains justification by works, which means it is abolished from Christ and fallen from grace.

â€¢ ••Paul's Argument Against the Papists••
	• He who is justified by works is debtor to the whole law.
	• Therefore, he who is justified by works has no part in Christ.

â€¢ ••The Papists' Response••
	• Papists argue that a double fulfilling of the law is possible, one for this life and one for the life to come.
	• They claim that this fulfilling of the law is to love God above all creatures in truth.

â€¢ ••Paul's Rebuttal••
	• Paul argues that no man can fulfill the law, and therefore it is a great inconvenience to be bound to keep the whole law.
	• He points out that Augustine's view of two fulfillings of the law is imperfect and a sin.

â€¢ ••The Abuse of Circumcision••
	• The false apostles abuse circumcision by making it a meritorious cause of salvation.
	• Papists today make baptism a physical cause that contains and confers grace.

â€¢ ••The Papists' Deception••
	• Papists turn the works of the Spirit, alms, prayer, fasting, and contrition into meritorious causes of justification and life.
	• They also turn their own traditions, confession, and satisfaction into meritorious causes of justification and life.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Obligation of Baptism••
	• Baptism is an obligation to live according to all of God's laws (Matt. 28:19-20)
	• Few people think about or perform this obligation
• ••The Condition of the Law••
	• The law is wholly copulative, meaning all parts are linked together
	• Breaking one commandment is equivalent to breaking all of them (James 2:10)
• ••True Regeneration••
	• True regeneration involves a reformation and change according to the whole law of God
	• It contains the seeds of all good duties
• ••The Importance of Keeping All Commandments••
	• Keeping one commandment is equivalent to keeping all of them
	• Breaking one commandment is equivalent to breaking all of them
• ••The Relationship Between the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law and the gospel are not one in substance of doctrine
	• The gospel is not just a more perfect and plain version of the law
• ••The Merit of Christ's Passion••
	• Christ's merit and the merit of our works are not the same
	• Christ's merit is based on the personal union of the Godhead with the manhood
• ••Falling from Grace••
	• Men can be said to be under grace in two ways: in the judgment of infallibility and in the judgment of Christian charity
	• Falling from grace does not mean being cast out of God's favor, but rather being revealed as never having been in it
• ••The Condition of Being Justified by the Law••
	• Being justified by the law means being abolished from Christ
	• This is not an absolute statement, but rather a conditional one
• ••The Importance of True Christianity••
	• No religion, but true Christianity, saves
	• Being abolished from Christ means being quite out of the favor of God
• ••The Dangers of Sin••
	• One sin can lead to perdition as easily as many sins
	• The devil can bring a man to perdition through one sin as easily as through many

â€¢ ••The Christian's Hope••

â€¢ ••The Resolution••

	• This is confirmed by the consent of all churches and the property of faith

â€¢ ••The Inward Exercises of Faith••

â€¢ ••The Christian's Duty••

â€¢ ••Faith and outward things••

â€¢ ••The justification of a sinner••

	• Faith is the means of justification, and love is the fruit of faith

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Faith justifies, not love••
	• Faith is the means of justification, not love
	• Love is a result of justification, not a means of it
	• Faith apprehends Christ in the promise, not love

â€¢ ••Faith and love are distinct••
	• Faith is a hand that receives Christ and His benefits
	• Love is a hand that gives out and communicates itself to others

â€¢ ••Love follows justification••
	• Justification comes first, followed by sanctification and love
	• Love is a part of sanctification, not justification

â€¢ ••Paul's words on faith and love••
	• "Faith works by love" (Galatians 5:6) shows how true faith is discerned, not how it justifies
	• Faith and love are joined in one person or subject, but not in justification

â€¢ ••James' words on justification••
	• "Faith and works" (James 2:24) refers to a historical knowledge of religion, not justification by works
	• Justification of the person is separate from justification of the faith of the person

â€¢ ••Justification and sanctification are distinct••
	• Justification delivers from hell and gives a right to life everlasting
	• Sanctification is a fruit of justification and serves to make us thankful to God

â€¢ ••Love is not necessary for justification••
	• Justification is not dependent on love or good works
	• Love is a part of sanctification, not justification

â€¢ ••True faith is fruitful in good works••
	• All true faith is fruitful in good works and love
	• Failing to obey the truth is a sign of a lack of true faith

â€¢ ••The warning to obey the truth••
	• The warning is a reproof or complaint for failing to obey the truth
	• The truth is the doctrine of Paul and the word of God

â€¢ ••The scope of the text••
	• The text is a repetition of the principal conclusion of the argument
	• The conclusion is that faith justifies, not love, and that true faith is fruitful in good works.

â€¢ ••The Sum of Paul's Doctrine••
