â€¢ ••The Calling to Liberty••
	• The calling to liberty is from God the Father in Christ by the Spirit
	• This calling is the reason for the good duties and rules that follow

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Calling of God••
	• God alone calls the things that are not as though they were (Rom. 4:17)
	• The calling of God is directed to the elect and those who are not elect

â€¢ ••Who are Called?••
	• All who answer the calling of God, whether in profession, heart, or both
	• The calling of God is directed to the elect, and then to those who are not elect

â€¢ ••The Calling to Liberty••
	• An action of God that translates men from the kingdom of darkness to His own kingdom
	• Has two parts: invitation and admission
		- Invitation: God offers remission of sins and life everlasting through the preaching of the gospel and the inspiration of heavenly desires
		- Admission: men are entered into the kingdom of grace, either outwardly through baptism or inwardly through faith

â€¢ ••Why Paul Mentions the Calling to Liberty••
	• It is the ground of all comfort (1 Cor. 1:9)
	• It is the ground of good life (1 Peter 1:15, Eph. 4:1)

â€¢ ••The Abuse of Liberty••
	• Using spiritual liberty as an occasion to live according to the flesh
	• This is done three ways:
		- Making more things indifferent than God ever made
		- Using things that are indifferent as if they were not
		- Using things that are not indifferent as if they were

â€¢ ••The Right Use of Liberty••
	• Not using spiritual liberty as an occasion to live according to the flesh
	• Distinguishing between things that are indifferent and things that are not

â€¢ ••The Corruption of the Soul••
	• The corruption of all the powers of the soul, including reason and conscience
	• This corruption is referred to as "fleshiness"

â€¢ ••The Meaning of "Flesh"••
	• Refers to the corruption of the soul, not just sensuality or carnal appetites
	• Paul says that the wisdom of the flesh is enmity to God (Rom. 8:7)

â€¢ ••The Need for Renewal••
	• The need to be renewed in the spirit of the mind (Eph. 4:23)
	• The need to be transformed by the renewing of the mind (Rom. 12:2)

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Unbelief••
	• The lack of movement to change and amend one's life
	• The prevalence of atheism and unbelief in England, where few have heard the calling of God for over 40 years

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Abuse of Liberty••
	• Liberty is abused when people excuse sinful behavior by saying it's "indifferent"
	• This leads to an immoderate use of God's gifts, such as food, drink, and clothing

â€¢ ••Immoderate Use of God's Gifts••
	• The use of God's gifts is immoderate in three ways:
		- In respect of time (e.g., turning recreation into an occupation)
		- In respect of themselves (e.g., excessive eating and drinking)
		- In respect of one's calling and condition (e.g., living beyond one's means)

â€¢ ••Abuse of Blessings••
	• The blessings of God are made instruments of riot, vanity, and pride
	• This leads to the condemnation of things that are otherwise indifferent

â€¢ ••Abuse in England••
	• The abuse of liberty is prevalent in England
	• People take a "toleration of sinning" based on God's patience, election, or mercy

â€¢ ••Misuse of the Death of Christ••
	• The death of Christ is seen as a license to commit sin
	• People persuade themselves of mercy despite their sinful behavior

â€¢ ••Abuse of Food, Drink, and Apparel••
	• There is excessive consumption of food, drink, and clothing
	• This leads to a lack of self-control and a focus on worldly pleasures

â€¢ ••Right Use of Christian Liberty••
	• Christian liberty is used correctly when:
		- We are renewed and sanctified
		- We use God's gifts in a spiritual and holy way

â€¢ ••Spiritual and Holy Use of God's Gifts••
	• We must receive and use God's gifts as from His hand
	• We must observe five rules:
		- Sanctify the creatures of God by the word and prayer
		- Be circumspect lest we sin in the use of the creatures
		- Use the gifts of God with thanksgiving
		- Remember God in all things
		- Worship God and sanctify the earth and its creatures

â€¢ ••Sanctifying the Earth and Its Creatures••
	• We must sanctify the earth and its creatures to God's use
	• This involves worshiping God and remembering Him in all things

â€¢ ••Observing the Rules••
	• We must observe the five rules for using God's gifts correctly
	• This involves sanctifying the creatures of God, being circumspect, using the gifts with thanksgiving, remembering God, and worshiping Him.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Rule 1: We must suffer ourselves to be limited and moderated in the use of our liberty••
	• This means we must be willing to limit our freedom in certain situations
	• We must be moderated in our use of liberty, partly by human law and partly by the law of charity

â€¢ ••Rule 2: Our liberty must be used for right ends••
	• The right ends are the glory of God, the preservation of nature, and the good of our neighbor
	• We must not use our liberty for selfish or fleshly purposes

â€¢ ••Consequences of abusing spiritual liberty••
	• Those who abuse spiritual liberty are like thieves who sell valuable things for little value
	• They are not true owners of spiritual liberty and are still in the kingdom of darkness

â€¢ ••The second main rule: "Serve one another by love"••
	• This rule is propounded in this place because it sets down the end of all apostolic doctrine as love out of a pure heart

â€¢ ••What is the love of our neighbor specified in this rule?••
	• It is an affection renewed whereby we are moved to wish well to our neighbor in the Lord
	• This love is not a natural habit, but a gift from God
	• It is an affection that inclines us to wish well to our neighbor, not just in body but also in soul

â€¢ ••Why must love be in the Lord?••
	• We must love our neighbor because they are a creature of God and bear His image
	• Love for other ends, such as honor, profit, or pleasure, is self-love

â€¢ ••What is the use of love?••
	• Love serves to make us serviceable to our neighbor
	• It seeks not its own things, but the good of others

â€¢ ••Christ's example of love••
	• Christ was a servant to His enemies in bearing their sins upon the cross
	• We are to follow Christ's example and serve our neighbor in love

â€¢ ••Love and service••
	• Love seeks to serve others, not itself
	• We are to serve our neighbor, and this is not against our liberty

â€¢ ••The purpose of all apostolic doctrine••
	• The end of all sound doctrine is love out of a pure heart
	• All our preaching must tend to this end, to love and serve others in the Lord.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Rule of Liberty••
	• We must use our outward liberty as servants to others
	• This is a test of our power of religion

â€¢ ••Six Sorts of Men in Breach of the Rule••
	• Usurers: lend for personal gain instead of helping those in need
	• Engrossers: gather commodities for personal enrichment
	• Idle persons: spend time on leisure activities instead of being productive
	• Riotous persons: waste resources and neglect family and country responsibilities
	• Tradesmen: prioritize personal gain over fairness and justice
	• Drowsy and carnal Protestants: prioritize worldly interests over spiritual growth

â€¢ ••The Common Fault of the World••
	• Men prioritize their own interests over serving others
	• The phrase "Every man for himself, and God for us all" reflects this fault

â€¢ ••The Need for Repentance and Reform••
	• We must acknowledge our offense and ask for pardon
	• We must change our lives to conform to the rule of liberty

â€¢ ••The Two Callings of Every Man••
	• A general calling: to be a Christian and do good to all men
	• A particular calling: to serve in a specific estate or role (e.g. magistrate, minister, master of the family)

â€¢ ••The Responsibilities of Each Calling••
	• Magistrate: maintain the gospel and execute justice
	• Minister: preach sound religion and love the souls of men
	• Master of the family: cause household members to embrace the gospel and frequent religious exercises
	• Tradesmen and office holders: prioritize the good of the country and be helpful to all with whom they deal

â€¢ ••The Ideal of Righteousness••
	• We should be "trees of righteousness" whose fruit is beneficial to others
	• Our actions should be guided by love for our neighbors

â€¢ ••The Fulfillment of the Law••
	• The law is fulfilled in one word: "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself"
	• This precept includes the love of God and the love of our neighbor

â€¢ ••The Relationship between Love of God and Love of Neighbor••
	• The love of God is practiced in the love of our neighbor
	• We love God by loving our neighbor, and vice versa

â€¢ ••The Scope of Love••
	• Love includes both affection and duties
	• Love is owed to anyone who is near to us in respect of human nature, even if they are our enemies

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Meaning of "As Thyself"••
	• The phrase "as thyself" refers to the quality of love, not the quantity.
	• It means to love our neighbor as truly and earnestly as we love ourselves.
• ••The Scope of the Second Rule••
	• To serve our neighbor in duties of love is the keeping of the whole law.
	• This service must be carefully performed.
• ••The End of a Man's Life••
	• The end of a man's life is to serve God in serving others.
	• This is the sum of the whole law.
• ••True Religion and Godliness••
	• True religion and godliness are shown in loving and serving God in serving others.
	• This is demonstrated in duties of love and the edification of our neighbor.
• ••Hypocrisy in the Church••
	• Some people are holy in the church but devils at home.
	• True religion is shown in private dealings and in the course of one's life.
• ••The Dangers of Contentions••
	• Contentions and dissensions breed the destruction and desolation of the church.
	• This is a second reason for the second rule: to serve one another by love.
• ••The Causes of Contentions••
	• Contentions in the church of Galatia were caused by differences in points of religion.
	• Similar contentions existed in the church of Corinth.
• ••The Importance of Unity••
	• Unity is not an infallible and inseparable mark of the church of God.
	• Unity may be out of the church, as seen in the example of the Galatians.
• ••The Consequences of Biting and Devouring••
	• "Biting" refers to injuries in words, such as railing, cursing, and slandering.
	• "Devouring" refers to injuries in deed or violence, even to the shedding of blood.
• ••The Call to Service••
	• We are called to serve one another by love, rather than engaging in contentions and dissensions.
	• This service is the keeping of the whole law.

â€¢ ••Dissensions in the Church••

â€¢ ••Quality of Dissensions••

â€¢ ••Maintaining Christian Peace••
	• We must study and use means to maintain Christian peace and concord
	• We must remember the general rule: "Have peace with all men" (Rom. 12:18)

â€¢ ••Cautions for Maintaining Peace••
	• We must observe the caution to have peace with all men, if possible, with good conscience
	• We must also observe the caution to defend ourselves, if necessary, and not compromise our faith

â€¢ ••Pacification with Papists••
	• Protestants are content to make a pacification with papists in civil society
	• However, we cannot yield in any point of our religion to the papists

â€¢ ••Types of Peace••
	• There are three types of peace: church peace, civil peace, and household peace
	• All three are to be maintained

â€¢ ••Church Peace••
	• Church peace requires conferences in a free or Christian council to end differences in religion
	• This is a thing much to be desired, especially in the western world

â€¢ ••Council of Trent••
	• The Protestants did not join with the papists at the Council of Trent
	• This was because the papists would not yield on any point of their religion

â€¢ ••Importance of Christian Peace••
	• Christian peace is essential for the health and prosperity of the church
	• We must strive to maintain peace and concord in all aspects of life.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Council of Trent••
	• The council was not a free council because the Pope was both party and judge.
	• The Protestant princes were dismissed twice due to safe conduct being revoked.
	• The third time, they refused to send their divines due to being "mocked" twice.

â€¢ ••Christian Toleration••
	• A toleration presupposes an error or defect in one's brother.
	• An error in judgment can be tolerated if it's not in the foundation of religion.
	• Errors in manners can be tolerated if they're not open offenses.

â€¢ ••Toleration in Practice••
	• Toleration must tend to the good and edification of men.
	• We must not tolerate vices or betray God's truth.
	• Popery is not to be tolerated due to its heretical and schismatic nature.

â€¢ ••The Role of the Magistrate••
	• The publishing of faith and profession of conscience is not free from the power of the magistrate.
	• Faith and conscience in the heart are free from man's authority.

â€¢ ••Building the Church••
	• Every man, especially teachers, must set themselves to build the church.
	• The truth must be confessed without opposition, and then contentions laid aside.
	• Ministers in England must remember this due to the papist threat.

â€¢ ••Civil Peace••
	• Civil peace is maintained when men yield subjection to the gospel of Christ.
	• The preaching of the gospel, invocation of God's name, and sacraments are essential.

â€¢ ••Household Peace••
	• Governors of families must urge and compel all under them to practice religion.
	• A family is the school of God where faith, invocation, love, and patience are exercised.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Family••
	• There is more virtue in the well-ordering of a family than in monkish cloisters.
	• A family is where faith, love, and patience are put into practice.

â€¢ ••The Role of Teachers••
	• Teachers must set themselves to build the church.
	• They must confess the truth without opposition and then lay aside contentions.

â€¢ ••The Threat of the Papist••
	• While we strive among ourselves, the papist gets ground.
	• Ministers in England must remember this and focus on building the church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Virtue of the Spirit in the Bond of Peace••
	• Paul gives 7 reasons to maintain the virtue of the Spirit in the bond of peace (Eph. 4:4-6)
	• The reasons include: one body, one Spirit, one hope of eternal life, one Lord, one faith, one baptism, one Father of all
• ••The Devil's Peace vs. God's Peace••
	• The peace of many is peace in drunkenness, profaneness, and wickedness (the devil's peace)
	• The peace of which Paul speaks is in the Lord and in the true worship of God
• ••Walking in the Spirit••
	• Paul returns to the first rule (v. 13) and shows the way to observe it
	• The rule is: "If ye walk in the Spirit, ye shall not fulfill the lusts of the flesh"
• ••The Spirit as a Gift of Regeneration••
	• The Spirit is a gift of regeneration, lost by Adam, restored by Christ
	• It is a gift that makes a distinction between it and civil virtue
• ••The Spirit's Properties••
	• The Spirit is a rich and liberal grace of God
	• It contains the seed of all virtues and all necessary graces of God
• ••The Spirit's Properties (continued)••
	• The Spirit is in all the powers of the regenerate (mind, conscience, will, affections, and sensual appetite)
	• Those who are sanctified in one part are sanctified in all
• ••The Spirit's Properties (continued)••
	• The Spirit is sincere, without falsehood or guile
	• This sincerity arises from the difference between the godly man and a hypocrite
• ••The Spirit's Properties (continued)••
	• The Spirit is not present in those who desire God's favor deceitfully
	• The Spirit is not present in those who mourn for sin without repentance
• ••The Spirit's Properties (continued)••
	• The Spirit is not present in those who pretend to love God without true devotion
	• True love for God comes from the Spirit, not from self-interest
• ••The Spirit's Properties (continued)••
	• The Spirit has a fourth property: excellence, which is not fully explained in this text

â€¢ ••The Spirit of Grace in Christians••
	• Is more excellent than the grace of creation
	• Has two advantages: beginning and constancy

â€¢ ••Advantages of the Spirit of Grace••
	• Begins with Christ, the second Adam, who is both God and man

â€¢ ••Properties of the Spirit••
	• Liveliness: the Spirit is effectual in operation

	• Works by certain degrees

â€¢ ••Degrees of the Spirit's Operation••
	• First degree: making us feel our need of Christ and desire reconciliation with God
	• Second degree: casting down everything that exalts itself against God
	• Third degree: writing the law in our hearts

â€¢ ••Actions of the Spirit••
	• Casts down erroneous reason and rebellious affection
	• Kindles a care and desire for reconciliation with God in Christ
	• Writes the law in our hearts

â€¢ ••Walking in the Spirit••
	• Ordering our lives according to the direction and motion of the Spirit
	• Renewing our nature within and changing our actions

â€¢ ••Effects of Walking in the Spirit••
	• Doing actions in faith, believing that they please God in Christ
	• Doing actions in obedience to the written Word
	• Intending and desiring to honor God in the things we do

â€¢ ••The Use of Walking in the Spirit••
	• Reveals our failure in our duties
	• Shows us that we must become spiritual men, making conscience of every sin

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Carnal Men••
	• Professing Christ but living according to the laws of nature
	• Minding earthly things and being carnal

â€¢ ••The Call to Spiritual Living••
	• We must become spiritual men, doing things in faith and obedience
	• We must intend and desire to honor God in the things we do

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Importance of Being Spiritual••
	• To be a Christian, one must be spiritual (Rom. 8)
	• A natural man can perform outward duties of religion, but it's not enough
	• A spiritual man's life is characterized by a consistent walk with God

â€¢ ••Judging a Man's Estate••
	• Don't judge a man's spiritual state by a single action or a few actions
	• Instead, look at the overall course of his life
	• If his life is carnal, it shows he is carnal; if it's spiritual, it shows he is spiritual

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Fulfilling Lusts••
	• Fulfilling lusts means accomplishing them with love, pleasure, and full consent
	• This is not just doing evil, but persevering in evil by adding sin to sin

â€¢ ••The Child of God and Lust••
	• The child of God is assaulted by lusts, but does not accomplish them
	• There are five degrees of lust: suggestion, delight, consent, the act, and perseverance
	• The child of God may experience suggestion and delight, but not consent or perseverance

â€¢ ••The Role of Consent and Perseverance••
	• Consent is not ordinary in the child of God
	• If the child of God does consent to lust, it's only in part and against their purpose
	• Perseverance in evil does not befall the child of God, as they recover themselves by new repentance

â€¢ ••The Use of the Rule••
	• The lust of the flesh is in the child of God to the death
	• They do not fulfill the law or achieve justification through it
	• Popish doctrine is incorrect in this regard

â€¢ ••Our Duty Regarding Lust••
	• Our duty is not to accomplish lusts, but to resist them to the uttermost
	• We must wage battle against them and recover ourselves by new repentance

â€¢ ••Comfort for the Servants of God••
	• If you hate and detest lusts, resist them, and recover yourself by new repentance, you are a child of God
	• Lusts in the child of God are not laid to their charge to condemnation

â€¢ ••The Spiritual Combat••
	• The flesh and Spirit are contrary to each other, making it impossible to do good or evil as we would
	• The flesh signifies the corruption of human nature, while the Spirit is the gift of regeneration

â€¢ ••The Parties in the Spiritual Combat••
	• The flesh and Spirit are mixed together in the whole man regenerate
	• They are not separate entities, but qualities that are present in the soul of man

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Spiritual Combat••
	• It's a battle between the flesh and Spirit, with the flesh resisting the Spirit and vice versa
	• This battle is present in all the powers of the soul of man, making it a constant struggle.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Regenerate Mind••
	• The regenerate mind is not divided into flesh and Spirit, but is a mixture of both
	• The whole mind, will, and affections are partly spiritual and partly carnal

â€¢ ••The Combat••
	• The combat arises from the mixture of flesh and Spirit in the regenerate mind
	• The powers of the soul are carried and disposed in different ways due to this mixture

â€¢ ••The Means of the Combat••
	• The combat is made through a twofold concupiscence: the flesh lusting against the Spirit and the Spirit against the flesh
	• The flesh has two actions: defiling and repressing good motions of the Spirit, and bringing forth wicked cogitations and inclinations

â€¢ ••The Lust of the Flesh••
	• The flesh defiles and represses good motions of the Spirit
	• The flesh is compared to the disease of ephialtes, where a heavy weight is felt that cannot be removed

â€¢ ••The Lust of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit has two actions: curbing and restraining the flesh, and engendering good motions and inclinations
	• The Spirit's voice is an inward voice that guides and teaches the regenerate mind

â€¢ ••The Cause of the Combat••
	• The combat arises from the contrariety of the flesh and the Spirit
	• The flesh is opposed to the Spirit, which is the gift of righteousness

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Flesh••
	• The flesh is a want of righteousness and a proneness to all unrighteousness
	• The flesh is flatly contrary to the Spirit and has no power to bring forth good works

â€¢ ••The Cause of the Combat (continued)••
	• Man has no freedom of will in good duties before conversion, because he is then wholly flesh
	• The flesh cannot prepare itself for justification, just as darkness cannot prepare for the entrance of light

â€¢ ••The Persons Involved in the Combat••
	• The combat is found in believers, not unbelievers or wicked men
	• The combat is between the natural conscience and the rebellious affection in unbelievers, but is of a different kind in believers

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Combat in Believers••
	• The combat in believers is between the mind, will, and affections being carried against themselves
	• The combat is a result of the mixture of flesh and Spirit in the regenerate mind.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The nature of the combat••
	• The combat is a struggle between the spiritual and carnal aspects of a person's nature.
	• It is a characteristic of believers who are of a certain age, but not of infants.

â€¢ ••Who experiences the combat••
	• Only believers who are of a certain age experience the combat.
	• Infants do not experience the combat because they lack the exercise of their spiritual nature.

â€¢ ••Duration of the combat••
	• The combat is present in the godly for the duration of their life on earth.
	• In death, the flesh is abolished, and the combat ceases.

â€¢ ••Where the combat shows itself••
	• The combat shows itself in all the actions of regenerate men.
	• It is present in prayer, faith, and good actions, but also in doubts and deadness of spirit.

â€¢ ••Paul's experience of the combat••
	• Paul describes his own experience of the combat in Romans 7.
	• He says that he wills to do good, but evil is present, and he does not do the good he wills.

â€¢ ••The effect of the combat••
	• The combat hinders the godly from doing what they would.
	• It prevents them from sinning with full consent of will, but they may still fall into sin.

â€¢ ••The combat's impact on good works••
	• The combat means that even good works are mixed and not perfectly good.
	• They are partly spiritual and partly carnal, and therefore sinful.

â€¢ ••The sinfulness of good works••
	• All the works of regenerate men are sinful and deserve damnation in the rigor of justice.
	• This is because the mind and will of man are partly carnal and partly spiritual.

â€¢ ••Objection to the sinfulness of good works••
	• The objection is that good works are not a transgression of the law.
	• The answer is that the transgression of the law is twofold: direct and indirect.

â€¢ ••The nature of indirect transgression••
	• Indirect transgression is when a person does something that is not directly against the law, but is still sinful.
	• This is the case with the works of regenerate men, which are mixed and sinful.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Nature of Good Works••
	• Good works can become sinful if done imperfectly due to the flesh.
	• The law requires good works, but they are not done perfectly.
• ••The Spirit's Role in Good Works••
	• Good works proceed from the Spirit of God, but also from the mind and will of man.
	• When an effect has multiple causes, it takes on the nature of the second cause.
• ••Pleasing God••
	• Good works please God because the doer is in Christ.
	• They are accepted because God pardons the defects in our work.
• ••The Allegation that Good Works are Sins••
	• This is not true, as good works are not simply sins.
	• They are good because God commands them, and they are good in part because godly men do them.
• ••The Consequences of this Doctrine••
	• There is no justification by works.
	• There is no fulfilling of the law for the time of this life.
• ••The Mixture of Grace and Corruption••
	• The grace of God is mixed with the corruption of the flesh.
	• This mixture is felt by the godly, causing them great grief.
• ••The Importance of Industry and Pains in Religion••
	• We must use industry and pains to do what we ought to do.
	• We must ask, seek, and knock (Matt. 7:7).
	• We must strive against ignorance and blindness.
• ••The Combat of the Flesh and Spirit••
	• We must use sobriety and watchfulness over our own corruptions.
	• We must pray instantly, lest we fall into temptation.
• ••The Role of the Spirit••
	• If we are led by the Spirit, we are not under the law.
	• We must walk in the Spirit to avoid giving occasion to the flesh.
• ••The Curse of the Law••
	• Those who walk according to the Spirit are freed from the curse of the law.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Office of the Spirit••
	• Regenerates and renews the powers of the soul
	• Guides and conducts those who are regenerate (Ps. 143:10)
	• Has four actions:
		- Preservation: maintains the gift of regeneration
		- Cooperation: works together with the regenerate will of man
		- Direction: orders and establishes the mind, will, and affections in good duties
		- Excitation: stirs and moves the will and mind after regeneration

â€¢ ••The Need for Subsequent Grace••
	• We cannot do good without God's second grace
	• First grace makes us able to do good, but second grace makes us do it

â€¢ ••The Office of All True Believers••

	• The Spirit works in and by the word of God
	• Subjection has two parts:
		- Making trial, inquiry, and examination of God's will
		- Denying oneself and conforming to God's will

â€¢ ••The Privilege of Believers••
	• Not under the law in terms of curse and condemnation
	• God accepts imperfect obedience as perfect and absolute
	• This serves to comfort believers who struggle with sanctification

â€¢ ••The Works of the Flesh••
	• Manifest in various forms, including:
		- Adultery, fornication, and uncleanness
		- Idolatry, witchcraft, and hatred
		- Envy, murders, and drunkenness

â€¢ ••The Importance of Avoiding the Flesh••
	• Paul's rule: "Give no occasion to the flesh" and "Fulfill not the lusts of the flesh"
	• We are prone to blindness and hypocrisy, and must be careful not to esteem ourselves spiritual when we are carnal

â€¢ ••The Need for Examination and Conformity••
	• We must make trial, inquiry, and examination of God's will
	• We must deny ourselves and conform to God's will in all things

â€¢ ••The Role of the Word of God••
	• The Spirit works in and by the word of God
	• The word of God is the rule of good life

â€¢ ••The Importance of Conscience and Good Duties••
	• We must have an earnest care and conscience of good duties
	• God accepts the will and endeavor to obey for obedience

â€¢ ••The Comfort of God's Acceptance••
	• God accepts imperfect obedience as perfect and absolute
	• This serves to comfort believers who struggle with sanctification

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The purpose of a catalogue of sins••
	• To discover the corruption of man's heart by the fruits of sin
	• To better know oneself and take a manifest view of one's sinful condition
• ••The condition of the works of the flesh••
	• They are manifest, not only to God, but also to men with reason and natural conscience
	• This means that there is sufficient evidence for condemnation, even for those who have never known the gospel
• ••The importance of confessing sins••
	• Sins are manifest, whether confessed or not
	• Confessing sins is the way to cover them (Ps. 32:1, 4)
• ••The kinds of works of the flesh••
	• There are 17 kinds, which can be reduced to four heads:
		- Against chastity
		- Against religion
		- Against charity
		- Against temperance
• ••Works of the flesh against chastity••
	• There are four kinds:
		- Adultery
		- Fornication
		- Other sins (not specified)
	• These sins are placed in the first rank because they lead to reprobate minds and a lack of sense or feeling
• ••Adultery••
	• It is the breach of wedlock or the incontinency of persons married or betrothed
	• It is a great sin because it breaks the covenant of marriage made in the presence of God
	• It is punished by God and leads to the ruin of families and society
• ••Fornication••
	• It is the incontinency of single persons
	• It is not a light matter or a thing indifferent, as some have taught
	• It is a manifest work of the flesh that leads to condemnation
• ••Objections to the condemnation of fornication••
	• It may be argued that fornication is numbered among things indifferent
	• It may be argued that the Lord commanded Hosea to take a harlot as a type or figure
	• These objections are confuted by Paul and the judgment of the church
• ••The consequences of sin••
	• Sin leads to condemnation and punishment
	• Sin ruins families and society
	• Sin brings destruction and death
• ••The importance of taking sin seriously••
	• Sin is not to be taken lightly or excused
	• Sin must be confessed and punished in order to be covered (Ps. 32:1, 4)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Interpretation of "Take unto thee a wife of fornications"••
	• The phrase means to prophesy and publish that one is like someone who takes a wife of fornication
	• This is not a command to engage in fornication, but rather a call to repentance

â€¢ ••Toleration of fornication••
	• Fornication is a foul and manifest work of the flesh
	• Magistrates should not tolerate fornication, as it is against God's ordinance

â€¢ ••Definition of "uncleanness"••
	• Refers to incontinency against nature, including incest and the sin of Sodom
	• These sins are specifically named in certain passages, but not in others to avoid teaching them

â€¢ ••Definition of "wantonness"••
	• Refers to the open profession and ostentation of incontinency through unchaste words, gestures, and apparel
	• We should detest all signs of incontinency and be chaste in deed, word, and behavior

â€¢ ••Punishment of these sins••
	• Those who commit these sins will not inherit the kingdom of God (1 Corinthians 6:9-10)
	• This threat must be applied to every one of these sins specifically

â€¢ ••Warning to those who live in secret sin••
	• Those who live in adultery, fornication, etc. and come to church and the Lord's Table deceive themselves
	• God's threats will be accomplished, and no adulterer, fornicator, or unclean person will enter the kingdom of God

â€¢ ••Admonition to flee these sins••
	• These sins are not just "tricks of youth," but rather serious offenses against God
	• We should terrify ourselves from these offenses by setting God's judgments before us

â€¢ ••Help for those overtaken by these sins••
	• Their case is dangerous, but there is one way to help them: to cease from these sins and do the contrary
	• This will lead to mercy and forgiveness from God

â€¢ ••God's promise of mercy••
	• Those who confess their sins and forsake them will have mercy (Proverbs 28:13)
	• This promise is not contrary to God's threat, but rather a call to repentance and change

â€¢ ••Mortifying the passion of concupiscence••
	• We must stock up the root of these sins by mortifying the passion of concupiscence (Colossians 3:5; 1 Thessalonians 4:5)
	• This involves overcoming the desire for sin and living a life of sanctification and honor

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is an inordinate inclination to vices
	• The fear of God cleanses the heart and life
	• Without holiness, no man can see God or have fellowship with Him (Heb. 12:14)

â€¢ ••The Second Rule: Cutting Off Occasions of Sin••
	• Idleness is a special occasion of sin
	• The pampering of the body is also a special occasion of sin
	• Examples: David, Israelites, Sodom and Gomorrah

â€¢ ••The Third Rule: Avoiding Signs of Sin••
	• Avoid light talk and wanton behavior
	• Avoid curiousness and excess in trimming the body
	• Avoid suspected company or company that may be suspected

â€¢ ••Idolatry: A Sin Against Godliness••
	• Idolatry is the worship of idol gods
	• An idol is a fiction or a thing merely devised
	• Idolatry is twofold: when something is set up in the room of God, or when the affections of the heart are given to the creature

â€¢ ••Types of Idolatry••
	• When the Godhead is ascribed to a creature (e.g. Herod)
	• When any property of the Godhead is ascribed to the creature
	• When the affections of the heart are given to the creature (e.g. covetousness)

â€¢ ••Idolatry of Devised Worship••
	• Worshiping the true God with devised worship (e.g. images)
	• Forbidden in the second commandment (Deut. 4:15-16)
	• Examples: Aaron's calf, Jehu's destruction of idols

â€¢ ••The Romish Religion as Idolatrous••
	• Teaches idolatry four ways
	• Enjoins men to give the name and honor of God to the consecrated host
	• Teaches men to invocate angels and saints departed

â€¢ ••Invoking Angels and Saints••
	• Gives the searching of the heart, hearing, and helping of all men to the creature
	• Properties of the Godhead are given to the creature
	• Flat idolatry because the honor of God is given to the creature

â€¢ ••Putting Confidence in Works••
	• Teaches that we may put confidence in works, so it be
	• This is a form of idolatry because it gives the creature the place of God

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The Romish religion is a carnal religion that teaches idolatry
	• Idolatry is a sin against Godliness that must be avoided

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Idolatry in Images••
	• The author argues that worshiping God in images is idolatry
	• Papists claim they worship only the true God, but the author disputes this
	• The author says that God's will, not human intention, determines how God should be worshiped

â€¢ ••Idolatry in the Heart••
	• The author notes that many people are carnal and worship idols in their hearts
	• The heart's affections should be for God in Christ, not for the world and its riches

â€¢ ••Witchcraft••
	• The author defines witchcraft as all curious arts worked by the operation of the devil
	• Witchcraft involves a league or compact with the devil, either express or secret

â€¢ ••Ground of Witchcraft••
	• The ground of witchcraft is a league or compact with the devil
	• This can be either an express or open league, or a secret league

â€¢ ••Kinds of Witchcraft••
	• There are three kinds of witchcraft: superstitious divination, juggling, and charming
	• Superstitious divination involves telling fortunes or revealing secrets through various means
	• Juggling involves working wonders or feats beyond the order of nature
	• Charming or enchanting involves using words to procure hurt or help

â€¢ ••Superstitious Divination••
	• This involves using various means to tell fortunes or reveal secrets
	• Examples include consulting with familiar spirits, observing stars, and using entrails of beasts

â€¢ ••Juggling••
	• This involves working wonders or feats beyond the order of nature
	• The author notes that this is similar to the magic of the magicians of Egypt

â€¢ ••Charming or Enchanting••
	• This involves using words to procure hurt or help
	• The author notes that this is a form of witchcraft and should be avoided by Christians

â€¢ ••Use of Witchcraft••
	• The author notes that many people use figure-casting and charming in times of distress
	• However, these practices are full of superstition and folly, and should be avoided

â€¢ ••Christian Response••
	• Christians should avoid all forms of witchcraft and superstition
	• The author notes that God's will, not human intention, determines how God should be worshiped

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Discerning charms and avoiding them••
	• Keep in mind that charms have no force from God's institution
	• Their efficacy comes from a compact with the devil
	• Examples of charms include scratching to cure witchcraft

â€¢ ••Using approved means in distress••
	• Use ordinary and approved means to address problems
	• Leave the outcome to God and live by faith
	• Making haste for a cure before the appointed time is a lack of faith

â€¢ ••Gifts of God vs. devilish cures••
	• Some people claim to have a gift of God to cure diseases
	• This is actually a curse that allows the devil to work through them
	• These cures are based on satanic and superstitious means

â€¢ ••Defining a witch••
	• A witch is someone who knowingly and willingly uses the devil's assistance
	• This includes revealing secrets, working mischief, or effecting strange cures

â€¢ ••Distinguishing between witches and superstitious people••
	• Superstitious people use charms but don't knowingly work with the devil
	• They are not witches in the biblical sense, but are close to being so
	• They should be admonished to stop their superstitious practices

â€¢ ••The efficacy of things••
	• Only God's ordinance can give efficacy to things
	• If efficacy comes from elsewhere, it is through satanic operation

â€¢ ••The power of faith••
	• We must believe, hope, and act only according to God's word
	• False faith is believing in charms and spells, which is serving the devil

â€¢ ••Discovering a witch••
	• Discovery is difficult, as witches can work in secret
	• Signs of witchcraft include foul cursing, fair speaking, and praising things

â€¢ ••Signs of witchcraft••
	• Five special things can serve to discover a witch:
		- Free confession of the accused
		- Confession of associates with the witch
		- Invocation of the devil
		- Evidence of a familiar spirit
		- Evidence of actions that presuppose a league with the devil

â€¢ ••Warning against witchcraft••
	• Witchcraft is a serious sin that can lead to death
	• We must be vigilant and avoid all forms of witchcraft and superstition

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Signs of Witchcraft••
	• Suspicions of bewitchment are not proof
	• Testimony of a wizard is unreliable and devil-inspired
	• Sickness and death can have natural causes
	• Marks on the body are uncertain signs of witchcraft

â€¢ ••False Accusations••
	• Neighbors may fall out and accusations of witchcraft follow
	• Threatening words can be misinterpreted as evidence of witchcraft
	• Great circumspection is needed to avoid false accusations

â€¢ ••Heresy••
	• Heresy is an error in the foundation of Christian religion
	• It involves teaching and defending an error with obstinacy
	• Heresy is distinct from errors in philosophy or smaller points of divinity

â€¢ ••Defining Heresy••
	• Heresy involves an error in doctrine, not manners or order
	• It is an error in the foundation of religion, not just a minor mistake
	• Heresy is maintained with obstinacy, even after conviction

â€¢ ••Types of Errors••
	• Errors in smaller points of divinity are not heresy
	• Opinions about times and places in the Bible are not heresy
	• Heresy involves an error in the main doctrine, conviction, and obstinacy

â€¢ ••The Nature of Heresy••
	• Heresy is a work of the flesh, involving corruption of the mind and conscience
	• It is distinct from sensuality, which is a lower form of corruption

â€¢ ••Duty to Detest Heresy••
	• We must detest and eschew heresies
	• We must preserve ourselves from heresies through right principles of religion

â€¢ ••Right Principles of Religion••
	• The foundation of all true faith is the inspiration of Scripture
	• We must propound unto ourselves the right principles of religion
	• This involves acknowledging the inspiration of Scripture as the foundation of faith

â€¢ ••Consequences of Heresy••
	• Heresy involves obstinacy and conviction, even against one's own conscience
	• It leads to corruption of the mind and conscience
	• It is a work of the flesh, involving corruption of the higher powers

â€¢ ••Preserving Ourselves from Heresy••
	• We must observe three rules to preserve ourselves from heresy
	• These rules involve propounding unto ourselves the right principles of religion
	• We must preserve ourselves from heresy through right principles and duty.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Highest Stay and Stop••
	• The principle of the highest stay and stop is the demonstration of all doctrines and conclusions.
	• It has no principle above itself to confirm it.
	• Human reason is not a principle of religion.

â€¢ ••Limitations of Human Reason••
	• Human reason is imperfect and erroneous.
	• It serves only to make men without excuse.
	• We can dispute the creation of the world, but certainty comes from faith in the Word (Heb. 11:3).

â€¢ ••The Authority of the Church••
	• The papists make the authority of the church a principle.
	• This is no principle in religion, as faith cannot exist without the Word of God.
	• Scripture is the best interpreter of itself.

â€¢ ••Interpreting Scripture••
	• Scripture is both the gloss and the text.
	• The sense of Scripture is to be derived from itself, not from tradition or human authority.
	• The proper and infallible sense of Scripture is the one that agrees with the words, scope, and analogy of faith.

â€¢ ••Reading and Obeying Scripture••
	• Reading and obeying Scripture is the key to avoiding heresy.
	• God's promise is that if we obey, we will know whether His doctrine is from God or not (John 7:17).
	• The secret of the Lord is revealed to those who fear Him (Ps. 25:14).

â€¢ ••Philosophy and Reason••
	• Paul warns against being spoiled by philosophy (Col. 2:8).
	• Philosophy can be used with circumspection, but it must be used carefully to avoid corruption by errors.
	• Natural philosophy gives too much to nature and too little to God.

â€¢ ••Moral Philosophy••
	• Moral philosophy places happiness in civil virtue, outside of Christ.
	• It teaches that virtue is a mean or mediocrity of affection, rather than a renovation of affections by regeneration.
	• It teaches that urbanity and magnanimity are virtues, but these are contrary to Christian humility.

â€¢ ••Freedom of Will••
	• Moral philosophy teaches that man has a freedom of will in good actions.
	• This doctrine is false and erroneous when applied to matters of religion.

â€¢ ••Sins Against Charity••
	• Sins against charity are listed as eight in number.
	• The first sin is "enmity", which is considered in three aspects.

â€¢ ••Understanding Enmity••
	• Enmity is not a sin in itself, but rather a hatred of God's enemies in respect of their sins.
	• David's hatred of God's enemies is commendable, not in respect of their persons but in respect of their sins.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••What is hatred?••
	• Not simply commanded, but only in a certain respect (opposed to God and Christ)
	• Utterly to be despised in comparison to God and Christ

â€¢ ••Objection: God chose Jacob and hated Esau••
	• Answer: We are to be like God in holiness, not in sovereignty or lordship over creatures

â€¢ ••What is enmity?••
	• A perverse disposition of mind to remember injuries and desire to requite like for like
	• Examples: Esau and Jacob, Absalom and Amnon

â€¢ ••Where is enmity found?••
	• Everywhere among people, causing division and strife
	• Remedy: all being of one mind, desire, and affection in receiving and furthering the gospel of Christ

â€¢ ••Debate••
	• A contention in words, striving to show courage and get the victory
	• Condemned in Ephesians 4:31, as it disregards equity and truth

â€¢ ••Emulations••
	• Good emulation: striving to be like those who excel in virtue or to go beyond them
	• Carnal emulation: grieving at another's excellency or being equal to them

â€¢ ••The use of rejoicing in others' excellency••
	• Examples: Moses, John the Baptist, and Paul

â€¢ ••Anger••
	• Three points:
		- Is there any lawful anger?
		- When is anger a sin?
		- The remedy for anger

â€¢ ••Lawful anger••
	• Yes, when there is a just cause, such as a manifest offense against God

â€¢ ••When is anger a sin?••
	• When men are hasty to be angry or keep no measure in their anger

â€¢ ••Contention••
	• Sundry kinds of lawful contention: just war, disputations, and school debates
	• Contention is carnal and sinful in respect of matter and manner (e.g., without forgiving or suffering)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Defending one's own cause••
	• People often defend their own causes without regard for truth or justice
	• This can lead to opposing truth, justice, or both
	• Examples include wars and suits between quarrelsome individuals

â€¢ ••The use of forgiveness••
	• We should forgive and yield our rights as much as possible
	• This is in line with Philippians 2:3, "Do nothing by contention"

â€¢ ••Seditions and schisms••
	• Seditions refer to dissensions that separate people from each other
	• Schisms occur when people separate from the church or commonwealth
	• We are not schismatics if we separate from the Church of Rome due to their idolatry and heresies

â€¢ ••Rules for avoiding schism and sedition••
	• Rule 1: Do not separate from a church or people as long as they remain connected to Christ
	• Rule 2: Do not make alterations against the laws of God and the king

â€¢ ••The role of the supreme magistrate••
	• Alterations in the estate, either civil or ecclesiastical, belong to the supreme magistrate

â€¢ ••Envy••
	• Envy is a compound of carnal grief and hatred
	• It makes people grieve and hate the good things of others
	• Envy is often disguised as a criticism of hypocrisy

â€¢ ••Departing from envy••
	• We must love those who fear God and love the gifts and graces of God, even in our enemies
	• This helps us to depart from envy

â€¢ ••Murder••
	• Killing a man is a sin because God has given liberty for the killing of plants and beasts, but has restrained us from killing humans
	• The life of man is a spiritual substance, unlike plants and beasts

â€¢ ••Objections to the definition of murder••
	• Objection 1: Why is it a sin to kill a man if it's not a sin to kill plants and beasts?
	• Answer: God has given liberty for the two former, but has restrained us from killing humans
	• Objection 2: The magistrate kills without sin
	• Answer: The killing by the magistrate is not murder, but public revenge
	• Objection 3: Samson killed himself and sinned not
	• Answer: Samson was a judge and took public revenge, and his example is special

â€¢ ••The use of faith in temptation••
	• Death is not a remedy for avoiding great danger or sin
	• Faith in the promise of God is the remedy, which provides an issue in every temptation (1 Corinthians 10:13)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Importance of Life••
	• Life is a treasure that allows us to glorify God, do good to others, and save our souls.
	• We should prefer both our own and our neighbors' lives.
• ••The Sins of the Flesh••
	• The sins of the flesh are against temperance.
	• The two main sins against temperance are drunkenness and gluttony.
• ••Drunkenness••
	• Drunkenness is when men drink beyond the measure that makes them fit for service to God and their duties.
	• Excessive drinking and the distempering of the soul are two aspects of drunkenness.
	• Drunkenness can lead to intoxication, wicked imaginations, and disordered affections.
• ••Gluttony••
	• Gluttony is when men eat beyond the measure that makes them fit for service to God and their duties.
	• Gluttony is often accompanied by drunkenness and is considered a sin.
	• Gluttony can lead to reveling, rioting, and swaggering.
• ••The Consequences of Drunkenness••
	• Drunkenness can lead to plague, pestilence, famine, and captivity.
	• Drunkenness can destroy the body and lead to unnatural thirst, dropsy, consumptions, and death.
	• Drunkenness can hurt the mind and lead to wicked imaginations and disordered affections.
• ••The Example of the Brute Beast••
	• The horse and the ass can be considered schoolmasters for us, as they eat and drink in moderation.
	• We should learn from their example and avoid excessive eating and drinking.
• ••The Importance of Self-Control••
	• If we cannot control ourselves in small things, we may never be able to control ourselves in weighty matters.
	• We should be willing to give up small pleasures, such as a cup of wine or beer, for the sake of Christ.
• ••The Example of Noah••
	• Noah's drunkenness is remembered in Scripture, but it is not condoned.
	• Moses sets down the foul effects of Noah's drunkenness and condemns it.
	• Noah's example is noted in Scripture as a warning to all ages following.
• ••The Example of Joseph••
	• Joseph and his brothers drank and were drunk together, but this is not an example to be followed.
	• The text means that they drank liberally, but not excessively.
• ••The Call to Flee Vices••
	• We should detest and flee drunkenness and gluttony.
	• We should be mindful of God's commandment to avoid drunkards and gluttonous persons.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••I. The Sin of Drunkenness••

• The sin of drunkenness is a work of the flesh (Galatians 5:21)
	• It involves drinking excessively or drinking the best drink (Hag. 1:6)
	• It is not a good thing, even if it is done in the company of others

••II. Objections to the Sin of Drunkenness••

• Some learned physicians say it is good for health to drink occasionally
	• But this is not a good reason to sin against God
• Some say it is a sign of good fellowship
	• But this is a false fellowship, not a true one based on doctrine and prayer

••III. The Punishment of Drunkenness••

• The punishment for drunkenness is not inheriting the kingdom of God
	• This means being in torment in hell after death
	• There are only two states after death: heaven or hell

••IV. The Importance of Seeking God's Kingdom••

• We must seek God's kingdom and establish it in our hearts
	• This involves knowing God's will and yielding to it in repentance and obedience

••V. The Nature of the Kingdom of God••

• The kingdom of God comes by inheritance, not by merit of good works
	• This means that we cannot earn our way into heaven by our good deeds

••VI. The Difference between the Godly and the Ungodly••

• The godly man falls into sin, but repents and recovers
	• The ungodly man lies in sin and makes a practice of evil

••VII. Warning to Those Who Have Fallen••

• Those who have fallen into sin must bewail their offenses and forsake them
	• If we are found to be doers of sin, there is no hope of salvation

••VIII. Warning to Those Who Have Turned to God••

• Those who have turned to God must be constant and take heed of going back
	• Lest they lose the kingdom of God and fall back into sin

••IX. The Fruit of the Spirit••

• The fruit of the Spirit is love, joy, peace, longsuffering, gentleness, goodness, faith
	• Meekness and temperance are also part of the fruit of the Spirit

••X. The Rule for Living••

• We must walk in the Spirit, not in the flesh
	• This involves following the rule of the Spirit, which is outlined in Galatians 5:16-26

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The Fruit of the Spirit••
	• The property of the works of God's Spirit in us is called "the fruit of the Spirit"
	• This signifies that the church is the garden of God, and believers are trees of righteousness
• ••The Kinds of Works of the Spirit••
	• There are 9 kinds of works of the Spirit
	• The text only discusses two of them: love and joy
• ••Love••
	• Love is a fruit of the Spirit that follows faith and regeneration
	• It is a holy affection that loves God in Christ for Himself
	• There are three signs of love of God:
		- Desire for fellowship with God and Christ
		- Love for the Word of God above all earthly treasure
		- Desire for God's will to be preferred in all things
• ••Love of Neighbor••
	• Love of neighbor is to love them simply, in and for the Lord
	• The sign of this love is to love not "in word," but "in deed"
• ••Joy••
	• Joy is twofold: joy of glory after this life, and the joy of grace in this life
	• The text does not provide further explanation of joy
• ••The Efficacy of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit makes men fruitful or bearing trees of righteousness
	• Good works are made acceptable to God by His grace
• ••The Role of Free Will••
	• Free will of itself is like a dead or rotten piece of wood
	• It bears no fruit but as it is quickened by the Spirit
• ••The Importance of Regeneration••
	• Virtues and good affections are impossible without the grace of regeneration
	• The virtues of the heathen are but shadows of virtue and serve only to restrain the outward man
• ••The Acceptability of Good Works••
	• Good works are acceptable to God even by His grace
	• They are called "the fruits of the Spirit"
• ••The Need for Spiritual Renewal••
	• The most of us are barren trees that bear no fruit but the bad fruits of the flesh
	• We must look to be stocked up with the Spirit's life and sap

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Joy of Grace••
	• Moderates all sorrows, allowing us to rejoice in afflictions (1 Thess. 5:16)
	• Causes us to rejoice at the good of our neighbors (Rom. 12:15)
	• Opposed to envy and emulation

â€¢ ••Fruit of Joy••
	• Shows we are bad trees, prone to iniquity and fleshly works
	• Common sin is to pine away with grief, rather than rejoicing in others' blessings

â€¢ ••Peace••
	• A care and desire to maintain concord, if possible (Rom. 12:18)
	• Excellent virtue, essential for the kingdom of God (Rom. 14:17)
	• Rules for maintaining peace:
		- Do not take offense, nor give offense
		- Seek to edify one another, doing good or taking good (Rom. 14:19)

â€¢ ••Longsuffering••
	• Moderating anger and desire for revenge when wronged
	• Takes hard work, but is an excellent fruit
	• Remedies for longsuffering:
		- God's commandment forbids rash anger (James 1:19)
		- Example of God's and Christ's patience and meekness
		- All wrongs come by God's providence, to which we must subject ourselves
		- God forgives more than we can forgive
		- Danger of God's anger if we do not forgive
		- Duty of love to suffer and bear (1 Cor. 13)
		- Point of injustice to revenge ourselves
		- We may be ignorant of the minds and actions of others

â€¢ ••Gentleness••
	• Giving good speech and showing good countenances, even to those who wrong us
	• Without desire for revenge (Rom. 12:14; Eph. 4:32)
	• Right courtesy is with an honest heart to bless when wronged

â€¢ ••Goodness••
	• Virtue of communicating good things to others for their benefit
	• Prescribed by Paul as "communicating to the necessity of the saints" (Rom. 12:13)
	• Questions:
		- What are we to communicate? (gifts of our mind, temporal goods, lives)
		- Why are we to do so? (we are members of one body, and God's pleasure is for us to be instruments of good to one another)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Goodness••
	• Goodness is a virtue that involves showing kindness and compassion to others
	• It includes actions such as giving drink to the thirsty, harboring the harborless, and visiting the sick
	• Goodness is hard to find in modern society, where people are often focused on gathering goods and honors for themselves

â€¢ ••Goodness towards others••
	• Goodness involves endeavoring to gain the soul of our neighbor to God
	• It involves four actions: admonishing the unruly, comforting the distressed, bearing with the weak, and being patient with all

â€¢ ••Faith towards God••
	• Faith towards God involves believing in the remission of sins and reconciliation with God through Christ
	• However, many people have a false, dead, or ceremonial faith that is not accompanied by a change of heart and life

â€¢ ••Characteristics of true faith••
	• True faith is joined with the exercises of invocation and repentance
	• It is mixed with contrary unbelief, and those who believe often feel a lack of faith and much unbelief
	• Many people claim to have perfect faith, but this is often a vain persuasion

â€¢ ••Faith towards men••
	• Faith towards men involves speaking the truth from the heart and being faithful and just in keeping promises
	• This is a rare virtue in modern society, where people often use lying, dissembling, and other forms of deception to get ahead

â€¢ ••Importance of faith and truth••
	• God's commandment is to speak the truth to our neighbors
	• Truth is a mark of God's child, and those who lie bear the image of the devil
	• Liars will be punished eternally in the lake of fire

â€¢ ••Consequences of lying••
	• Liars are entertained at the same table with murderers and thieves
	• Liars will never go unpunished, and their tongues will be used as instruments of the devil

â€¢ ••God's character••
	• God's ways are all truth, and He hates a lying tongue
	• God's Spirit is the Spirit of truth, and those who lie are not like God

â€¢ ••The devil's character••
	• The devil is the father of lies, and those who lie bear his image
	• Liars are instruments of the devil, and their tongues will be used to deceive others

â€¢ ••The importance of truth in relationships••
	• Speaking the truth from the heart is essential for building trust and strong relationships
	• Christians should strive to be like the Turks, who are known for their honesty and openness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Meekness and Longsuffering••
	• Meekness is a general virtue
	• Longsuffering is the highest degree of meekness
	• Both involve enduring hardships and difficulties

â€¢ ••Temperance••
	• Moderation in the use of God's gifts and creations
	• Four rules for practicing temperance:
		- Moderation in eating and drinking
		- Moderation in apparel
		- Moderation in acquiring goods
		- Moderation in spending goods

â€¢ ••Against Such There is No Law••
	• Paul describes the benefits of virtues such as meekness and temperance
	• Against people who possess these virtues, there is no law to condemn them
	• This is because they freely obey God without constraint

â€¢ ••Spiritual Men••
	• Spiritual men are a voluntary and free people
	• They serve God freely without constraint
	• They would love and serve God even if there were no reward or punishment

â€¢ ••Crucifying the Flesh••
	• Spiritual men have crucified the flesh with its affections and lusts
	• This is a reason why there is no law against them
	• They are governed by a different spirit, one that is contrary to sin

â€¢ ••The Scope of the Passage••
	• Paul proves that there is no law against spiritual men
	• He gives two reasons for this:
		- Spiritual men are Christ's
		- They have crucified the flesh with its affections and lusts

â€¢ ••What is a Christian?••
	• A Christian is one who is Christ's
	• There are five ways to be Christ's:
		- By the right of creation
		- By the right of redemption
		- By the free gift and donation of God the Father
		- By propagation
		- By donation in baptism

â€¢ ••The Nature of the Flesh••
	• The flesh is the corruption of the whole nature of man.
	• It consists of the loss of the image of God and a proneness to all wickedness.

â€¢ ••The Distinction between Nature and Corruption••
	• The nature and faculties of the soul are corrupted.
	• Corruption is not separate from nature or faculties, but is a part of them.

â€¢ ••The Two Aspects of the Flesh••
	• Affections: inordinate and carnal feelings, such as anger, love of pleasures, and immoderate sorrow.
	• Lusts: inordinate and insatiable desires for worldly things, such as riches, honors, and pleasures.

â€¢ ••Examples of Carnal Men••
	• Herod was a carnal man because of his inordinate love of his brother's wife.
	• Judas was a carnal man because of his inordinate lust of covetousness.

â€¢ ••The Office of a Christian Man••
	• The primary task of a Christian is to crucify the flesh with its affections and lusts.

â€¢ ••The Action of Christ••
	• Christ's crucifixion is threefold:
		- Upon the cross, where He bore the burden of our sins.
		- In us, where He conveys the virtue of His death into our hearts.
		- In baptism, where He seals the two former to those who believe.

â€¢ ••Our Action: Imitating Christ••
	• We must imitate Christ by bringing ourselves into the presence of God, setting ourselves at the bar of God's judgment, and surrendering to His will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Vain Glory••
	• Vain glory is a form of pride where individuals seek the approval and admiration of others, rather than God's judgment and approval.
	• It is a branch of pride that refers to the honoring and advancing of oneself.

â€¢ ••Order of Priorities••
	• The vain-glorious man prioritizes the approval of others over God's judgment and approval.
	• The correct order of priorities is: God's judgment and approval, one's own, and then the approval of others.

â€¢ ••Types of Boasting••
	• There is lawful boasting, which is confessing one's good qualities to God's glory.
	• There is also unlawful boasting, which is ascribing one's gifts and abilities to oneself, rather than to God.

â€¢ ••Excuse 1: Seeking God's Judgment••
	• The vain-glorious man may excuse his behavior by saying he is seeking God's judgment and approval.
	• However, this is not the case, as he is primarily seeking the approval of others.

â€¢ ••Excuse 2: Seeking God's Blessings••
	• The vain-glorious man may excuse his behavior by saying he is seeking God's blessings and gifts.
	• However, this is not the case, as he is primarily seeking honor and glory for himself.

â€¢ ••Degrees of Honor••
	• There are two degrees of honor: the honor that comes with one's place and calling, and the honor that is above one's place and calling.
	• The vain-glorious man seeks the latter, which is not his to seek.

â€¢ ••Why the Galatians Were Warned••
	• The Galatians were warned about vain glory because they were men of understanding and knowledge, and were adorned with many excellent gifts.
	• This made them prone to vain glory, as they were more likely to be proud of their gifts and abilities.

â€¢ ••Where Vain Glory is Found••
	• Vain glory is a common vice that spreads far and wide.
	• It can be found in individuals who prioritize the approval of others over God's judgment and approval.

â€¢ ••Remedies for Vain Glory••
	• The remedy for vain glory consists of meditation and practice.
	• Meditation involves recognizing the dangers of vain glory and the benefits of humility.

â€¢ ••Remedies in Meditation••
	• God resists proud persons and gives grace to the humble (1 Peter 5:5).
	• The devil works to puff up the mind with self-liking and conceit, but God works to abase men and exalt them in His good time.
	• There is no religion in a heart that is wholly bent on seeking the praise of others.

• ••The Consequences of Vain Glory••
	• Vain glory can lead to a range of negative consequences, including contention, envy, and pride.

	• The commentary is published in 1617 by John Legatt, printer to the University of Cambridge.
	• The dedication is to Sir Bassingbourne Gaudy, a knight.

	• Perkins was licensed to interpret St. Paul's epistles.
	• His executor and friends asked Cudworth to supplement his commentary.

	• Cudworth apologizes for his boldness in attempting to equal Perkins' work.
	• He compares himself to Hirtius, who was hesitant to continue Caesar's commentaries.

â€¢ ••The Role of the Holy Spirit••
