	• All true virtues are wrought by the operation of God's Spirit in us

â€¢ ••Giving Glory to God••
	• We must return all praise and glory to God for the gifts and graces we receive
	• We should not attribute our virtues to ourselves

â€¢ ••Who to Turn to in Need••
	• We should turn to God alone in our need, not to saints or the Virgin Mary
	• God is the fountain of grace (Jer. 2:13)

â€¢ ••The Presence of the Spirit••
	• The Spirit of God is present with His graces to inspire, cherish, and increase them
	• We must not quench the Spirit (1 Thess. 5:19)

â€¢ ••The Reason for Using Meekness••
	• We should use meekness because we are brethren in the Lord (Rom. 12:10)
	• We should treat others with moderation, lenity, and gentleness because of our spiritual bond

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The importance of considering others••
	• We should consider our brethren to avoid the contagion of evil example (Rom. 16:17)
	• We should consider others to be able to reprove and censure them (Judg. 19:30)
	• We should consider others to follow their good example (Phil. 3:17)

â€¢ ••The importance of considering ourselves••
	• We should consider ourselves to learn more mildness towards others in our reproofs
	• We should consider our own weakness to practice meekness
	• We should consider our own sins to be merciful and forgiving

â€¢ ••The danger of self-righteousness••
	• The Pharisee considered himself, but only his supposed virtues, not his sins (Luke 18:11)
	• He was reproved by Christ because he did not consider his own weakness

â€¢ ••The example of God's mercy••
	• God forgives us innumerable sins (Matt. 18:32-33)
	• We should forgive others as God forgives us (Matt. 18:32-33)

â€¢ ••The importance of meekness••
	• We should restore those who have fallen in all meekness (because we may fall as well)
	• We should deal mercifully with others because we stand in need of mercy

â€¢ ••The example of Christ's mercy••
	• Christ became like unto us in all things and was tempted in like sort (Heb. 2:17-18; 4:15)
	• Christ is a merciful and faithful high priest who can be touched with a sense of our infirmities

â€¢ ••The objection that one who knows they cannot be overcome by temptation is not to reprove in the spirit of meekness••
	• No man is sure, and therefore no man can be secure

â€¢ ••The answer to the objection••
	• Even if a man knows he cannot be totally overcome by temptation, he should still consider himself and practice meekness

â€¢ ••The importance of considering our own weakness••
	• We should consider our own weakness to practice meekness
	• We should consider our own weakness to be merciful and forgiving

â€¢ ••The example of God's consideration of our weakness••
	• God considers that we are but flesh and a wind that passeth (Ps. 78:39)
	• God corrects and reproves us, descending to our weakness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Importance of Meekness••
	• Spiritual men should remember their own struggles with temptation
	• They should use meekness and mildness when correcting others
	• This is because they were once carnal and weak, just like others

â€¢ ••Christ's Example••
	• Jesus learned by experience how hard it was to overcome temptations
	• He has a fellow-feeling for our infirmities
	• This is why spiritual men should be gentle and compassionate

â€¢ ••The Need for Self-Reflection••
	• Spiritual men should consider their own past struggles and weaknesses
	• They should look to their own example when correcting others
	• This helps them to reprove others in the spirit of meekness

â€¢ ••Paul's Reason for Meekness••
	• Paul says we should show all meekness to all men (Titus 3:2-3)
	• This is because we were once unwise and disobedient, just like others

â€¢ ••The Importance of Considering Oneself••
	• It is natural for men to spy motes in others' eyes and not see beams in their own
	• We should begin with ourselves when judging and reproving others

â€¢ ••Brotherly Correction and Christian Reproof••
	• This doctrine is important for understanding how to correct and reprove others

â€¢ ••Who Should be Reproved?••
	• All brethren, including those by nature, affinity, country, and religion
	• This includes those who are part of the same community or fellowship

â€¢ ••For What Should We Reprove?••
	• For sin and disobedience
	• For actions that are contrary to the teachings of Christ

â€¢ ••By Whom Should We Reprove?••
	• By those who are spiritual and have a fellow-feeling for others' infirmities
	• By those who have a gentle and compassionate spirit

â€¢ ••In What Manner Should We Reprove?••
	• In the spirit of meekness and mildness
	• By considering our own past struggles and weaknesses
	• By beginning with ourselves when judging and reproving others

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Brethren••
	• Includes those related by nature, affinity, or country
	• Also includes those related by religion or profession
	• Does not include those outside the visible church (e.g. Jews, Turks, pagans)

â€¢ ••Scope of Reproof••
	• Belongs to those within the visible church
	• Includes those who are members of the same particular church
	• May also include those from other churches who profess the same religion

â€¢ ••Purpose of Reproof••
	• To procure the good of one another (as members of the same body)
	• To maintain the bond of unity within the church

â€¢ ••Examples of Reproof••
	• Nathan reproved David (2 Sam. 12)
	• Azariah rebuked Uzziah (2 Chron. 26:18)
	• Paul reproved Peter to his face (Gal. 2:11)

â€¢ ••Importance of Enduring Reproof••
	• Those who cannot endure reproof are to be censored
	• They contemn the ordinance of God

â€¢ ••Examples of Those Who Should Endure Reproof••
	• King David, who patiently endured reproof by Nathan
	• King Uzziah, who was stricken with leprosy for resisting God

â€¢ ••Exemption of Clergy••
	• The popish sort exempt their clergy from reproof
	• This is contrary to Paul's teaching, who would have ministers and elders reproved as well as others

â€¢ ••Duty to Reprove Superiors••
	• We are bound to reprove all those in the church to whom we owe a duty of love
	• This includes superiors, whom we should love and reprove as well as others

â€¢ ••Reasons for Reproving Superiors••
	• They are in greater danger of falling due to their higher place
	• They should be reproved as well as others, if not more so

â€¢ ••Importance of Reproof in the Church••
	• Reproof is necessary to maintain the bond of unity within the church
	• It is a part of ecclesiastical discipline, which reaches those within the visible church.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••I. Who are to be reproved••

• ••Those who need admonitions and reproofs••
	• Because they are prone to sin and have few who will reprove them
	• Because they have many who will flatter them, but few who will dare to reprove them
• ••Exceptions to reproof••
	• Open scorners, contemnors, and persecutors of the word
	• Those who have not acknowledged their faults and the greatness of their sin

••II. Who are not to be accounted as dogs••

• ••Those who acknowledge their faults••
	• The Canaanite woman, who was a true believer
	• Those who acknowledge their sins and the merit of Christ
• ••St. Paul's practice••
	• He did not excommunicate or suspend the carnal Corinthians
	• He commanded Titus to rebuke the Cretans sharply for their lying and idleness

••III. Forgiveness and punishment••

• ••Christ's teaching on forgiveness••
	• He would have us forgive not only till seven times, but till seventy times seven times
	• He does not deny pardon to those who fall by recidivation
• ••Distinguishing between church and state••
	• Notorious offenders may be punished by the magistrate
	• They may be received into the church as sons of the church when they repent

••IV. For what faults are men to be reproved••

• ••Every known sin••
	• Because every sin brings death if not repented of
	• Because every sin is a dishonor to God and a scandal to the church
• ••Christ's command to reprove••
	• Not limited to private injuries
	• Extended to every sin because it affects the whole church and God's majesty

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Understanding the phrase "against thee"••
	• The phrase "against thee" is not limited to private wrongs against us
	• It refers to any sin committed against God
	• We should be more grieved when men sin against God than when they sin against us

â€¢ ••Why we should reprove known sins••
	• We are wronged when men sin against God
	• We should make God's quarrel our own quarrel
	• We should be offended when we see men sinning against God

â€¢ ••Scriptural basis for reproof••
	• "Thou shalt not hate thy brother in thine heart, but thou shalt plainly rebuke him" (Lev. 19:17)
	• The apostle Paul reproves all known sins, not just one kind (1 Cor. 5)

â€¢ ••Scope of reproof••
	• Reproof is not limited to one kind of sin, but extends to all known sins
	• Injuries and wrongs offered us are not to be excluded from reproof

â€¢ ••Purpose of reproof••
	• To teach the point of reproof, Jesus teaches that none should offend or scandalize their brother (Matt. 18)
	• Reproof is to be done in a way that is not accusatory, but rather a witness to the truth

â€¢ ••Role of the party offended••
	• The party offended is a witness to the truth, not an accuser
	• They are fitter to reprove the offender than any other person

â€¢ ••Reasons for the party offended to reprove••
	• They have a better understanding of the offense
	• Their reproof will take a better place when the offender sees their desire for their good

â€¢ ••Addressing the objection of revenge••
	• Reproof can be done without revenge
	• The right use of reproof should not be neglected because of the abuse thereof

â€¢ ••Avoiding heretics and schismatics••
	• General places of Scripture should be expounded according to particular limitation in other places
	• Heretics and schismatics should not be avoided, but rather reproved

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Reproof is a necessary part of Christian living
	• We should reprove known sins, not just private wrongs against us, but also sins committed against God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••I. Paul's Practice and Christ's Precept••

• Paul's practice is not contrary to Christ's precept.
	• Paul's practice is limited to excommunicating in the name and power of Christ (1 Cor. 5:4).
	• This is in accordance with Christ's institution (Matt. 18:23).

••II. Paul's Excommunication Practice••

• Paul does not excommunicate unclean persons, fornicators, or wantons without former admonition.
	• He makes his third coming unto them instead of three admonitions or witnesses against them (2 Cor. 13:1-2).
	• He threatens to come with a rod to those who live securely in the open breach of the law (1 Cor. 4:21).

••III. The Meaning of Reproof••

• The word "reproof" signifies reproof in word.
	• It can signify grave, serious, and effectual reproof by the church (2 Cor. 2:6).
	• It can also signify receiving into favor and familiarity before excommunication upon repentance.

••IV. The Incestuous Person••

• Even if the incestuous person was excommunicated, it does not necessarily mean they were excommunicated without precedent admonition.
	• The Scripture is silent on this point, and therefore it is not recorded.

••V. Admonishing Inordinate Livers••

• The apostle commands that we should have no familiarity with inordinate livers (2 Thess. 3:6).
	• However, he adds that if there are any amongst them that will not obey, they should note him by a letter (v. 14).
	• He expressly commands that they should admonish the inordinate (1 Thess. 5:14).

••VI. Who Are to Reprove?••

• It is a duty that concerns all men.
	• Our Savior Christ says, "If thy brother sin against thee, reprove him" (Luke 17).
	• The command to reprove is given to all men, not just to the church or its leaders.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••All Christians are bound to reprove their brethren••
	• This is because all Christians are brethren (Lev. 19:17)
	• They are members of the same body with Christ as the head (Eph. 4:15-16)
	• They are tied together by charity and a desire to help one another (Gal. 6:2)

â€¢ ••The duty of reproof is not limited to the perfect••
	• Even those with their own sins and weaknesses are still bound to reprove others (Matt. 7:3-5)
	• Sin does not exempt anyone from this duty (Rom. 3:23)

â€¢ ••The goal of reproof is to help and edify••
	• Reproof should be done with the goal of helping and edifying the person being reproved (2 Tim. 3:16-17)
	• It should not be done with the goal of causing scandal or harm (Prov. 9:7-8)

â€¢ ••Pastors and ministers have a special responsibility••
	• They are called to be overseers and watchmen (Ezek. 33-34)
	• They are responsible for inquiring into the lives of those under their care (Acts 20:28)

â€¢ ••The duty of reproof is not limited to pastors and ministers••
	• All Christians are bound to reprove their brethren (Lev. 19:17)
	• They should be instant in season and out of season to reprove and exhort (2 Tim. 4:2)

â€¢ ••There are five cases where reproof is not necessary••
	• If a man is ignorant of the offense (Prov. 24:15)
	• If a man is not bound to pry into the actions of others (Prov. 24:15)
	• If a man is not bound to seek out those who need reproof (Matt. 25:31-46)

â€¢ ••Reproof is a public debt••
	• It is not a debt due to any private person (Matt. 18:15)
	• It is sufficient to discharge this debt to those we meet with (Matt. 25:31-46)

â€¢ ••The failure to reprove is a serious matter••
	• It is as much as killing or destroying the person who could have been helped (Ezek. 33:6)
	• Those who say "Am I my brother's keeper?" are severely to be censured (Gen. 4:9)

â€¢ ••The duty of reproof is not optional••
	• Every man is bound to be clear and blameless, especially of open crimes (1 Tim. 3:2)
	• No man is exempted from this duty (Rom. 3:23)

â€¢ ••The goal of reproof is to bring about repentance and restoration••
	• Reproof should be done with the goal of bringing about repentance and restoration (2 Cor. 7:10)
	• It should not be done with the goal of causing harm or scandal (Prov. 9:7-8)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••When to Reprove••
	• Reprove only if the person has not already repented of their act
	• Reproof is not necessary if the person is already reclaimed
	• Punishment by the magistrate is allowed for the common good

â€¢ ••Effectiveness of Reproof••
	• Do not reprove if there is no hope of amendment
	• Reproof can make the person worse, not better
	• It is foolish to spend labor in vain on someone who will not be improved

â€¢ ••Differences between Reproof and Punishment••
	• The judge punishes for the common good, not for the person's private good
	• Reproof is intended for the person's private good, not for the common good

â€¢ ••When to Omit Reproof••
	• Omit reproof if the person will redress their fault without it
	• Omit reproof if others will admonish them of their fault
	• Omit reproof if it can be done more conveniently and profitably another time

â€¢ ••Consequences of Not Reproving••
	• Not reproofing a brother is guilty of their sin (Lev. 5:1)
	• Except in the five cases mentioned above

â€¢ ••Manner of Reproof••
	• Reprove in a way that advances God's glory and the person's good
	• Avoid defaming the person abroad in the world
	• Show love and aim for the person's good, not revenge or vain-glory

â€¢ ••Prayer and Guidance••
	• Pray for God's guidance in reproof
	• Ask God to move the person's heart to accept the reproof

â€¢ ••Avoiding Slander••
	• Do not traduce the person to others before or after reproof
	• This is a general rule, with the rest of the rules being more specific

â€¢ ••Grounding Reproof in Knowledge••
	• Reproof must be grounded in a certain knowledge of the fault committed
	• Do not go on private surmises, flying reports, or vehement suspicions

â€¢ ••Special Considerations••
	• Every reproof must be grounded in a certain knowledge of the fault committed
	• The rest of the rules are more specific and will be discussed in the following points.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Reproof should be done with caution••
	• Avoid accusing someone of a secret sin without knowledge
	• Don't reprove someone based on false reports or surmises

â€¢ ••Consider the source of information••
	• Reprove based on credible information, like St. Paul did with the Corinthians
	• If the report is not certain, reprove hypothetically and not peremptorily

â€¢ ••Consider yourself before reproving others••
	• Reflect on your own weaknesses and faults
	• Consider yourself in three respects: past, present, and future

â€¢ ••Past consideration••
	• Recognize that you were once as wicked and profane as the person you're reproving
	• Show meekness and gentleness to others, as Paul advises

â€¢ ••Present consideration••
	• Acknowledge that you're a frail and fallible person
	• Recognize that you're not able to resist temptation on your own

â€¢ ••Future consideration••
	• Remember that you may fall into the same sin as others in the future
	• Be compassionate towards others, as you would want them to be towards you

â€¢ ••Don't be a censorious critic••
	• Avoid being overly critical of others, as this can lead to blindness to your own faults

â€¢ ••The reprover should not be tainted with the same fault••
	• Avoid reproving someone for a fault you yourself possess
	• This can make your reproof seem hypocritical and unworthy

â€¢ ••Seek reproof from a righteous person••
	• Value reproof from someone who is righteous and not tainted with the same fault
	• This can be a beneficial and profitable experience for you

â€¢ ••Be cautious of your own reputation••
	• Avoid being seen as a hypocrite or someone who is unworthy to reprove others
	• This can damage your reputation and relationships with others

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Who to reprove••
	• Oneself, as deep in fault as anyone
	• Others, with love, hatred of sin, and zeal for God's glory

â€¢ ••Purpose of reproof••
	• To bring the party reproved to a true sight of their sin
	• To a lively sense and feeling of their sin
	• To a compunction of heart and a sense of God's wrath

â€¢ ••Example of God's reproof••
	• "I will reprove thee, and set thy sins in order before thee" (Ps. 50:21)
	• The prophet Nathan's parable that convicted David's conscience

â€¢ ••Paul's precept on reproof••
	• "Reprove, rebuke, exhort with all long suffering and doctrine" (2 Tim. 4:2)
	• To convict the conscience of sin

â€¢ ••Qualifying reproofs••
	• Showing the true meaning of the law and the curse of God
	• Unfolding the horrible greatness of sin to the conscience

â€¢ ••Danger of unqualified reproofs••
	• May cherish sin in the person reproved
	• May not lead to true repentance

â€¢ ••Tempering reproof with exhortation••
	• Using the law (corrosives) and the gospel (cordials)
	• Applying loving and affectionate persuasion

â€¢ ••The skillful approach to reproof••
	• Using section and cauterization (sharp instruments) and mollifying ointments (gentle persuasion)
	• Cleansing and suppling the wound with loving care

â€¢ ••Paul's commands on reproof••
	• "The servant of the Lord must be gentle toward all men" (2 Tim. 2:25)
	• "Instruct with meekness them that are contrary minded" (2 Tim. 2:25)

â€¢ ••Restoring brethren with meekness••
	• Setting a broken bone or a disjoined member with gentle care
	• Handling the person with tenderness and gentleness

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Approach to Reproof••
	• Reprove a man directly, but carry yourself in a way that makes the party see themselves reproved by God, not by you.
	• Use a preface to explain your intentions, such as wishing the person well and considering your own potential for error.

â€¢ ••Fitting the Reproof to the Person••
	• Consider how to reprove someone based on their quality and condition.
	• There are four ways to reprove someone:
		- Friendly admonition (one equal to another)
		- Reverent and submissive exhortation (younger to elder, inferior to superior)
		- Sharp reprehension (elders or superiors to inferiors, for notorious or scandalous offenses)
		- Due chastisement and correction (superiors to inferiors, with authority)

â€¢ ••Fitting the Reproof to the Offense••
	• Put a difference between sins committed through human frailty or ignorance and those committed through malice, pride, or presumption.
	• Reprove sins of malice, pride, or presumption with sharpness and severity, while winning over those committed through human frailty or ignorance with gentleness and lenity.

â€¢ ••Administering Reproof••
	• Consider the timing of the reproof, choosing a time when you can do the most good.
	• Avoid reproving someone when they are drunk, passionate, or in a state of heightened emotion.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Timing••
	• A reproof delivered in due season is like apples of gold with pictures of silver (Prov. 25:11).
	• There is a time for all things, and the timing of a reproof is crucial for its effectiveness.

â€¢ ••Considering the Person's Perspective••
	• Consider how the person being reproved might perceive your words, and try to avoid causing unnecessary offense or hurt.

â€¢ ••Using Spiritual Wisdom and Discretion••
	• Use spiritual wisdom and discretion when deciding how to reprove someone, taking into account their individual circumstances and the nature of the offense.

â€¢ ••The Role of Authority••
	• Superiors have a responsibility to reprove their inferiors, using due chastisement and correction when necessary.
	• Inferiors should not reprove their superiors, even if they do it with mildness.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Compassion••
	• Have compassion on those who are struggling with sin, and distinguish between sins committed through human frailty or ignorance and those committed through malice, pride, or presumption.

â€¢ ••The Role of Reproof in Spiritual Growth••
	• Reproof is an important tool for spiritual growth and development, helping individuals to recognize and overcome their sins and weaknesses.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••When to Reprove••
	• Reproof must be done at the right time and season.
	• Secret sins should be reproved secretly, between the person and the one reproving them.

â€¢ ••Reproof of Secret Sins••
	• Secret sins should not be divulged or shared with others.
	• Love and reconciliation should be prioritized over public rebuke.

â€¢ ••Reproof of Open Sins••
	• Open sins should be reproved openly, in front of others.
	• This is a special commandment for church leaders to reprove elders who have committed crimes.

â€¢ ••Consequences of Public Rebuke••
	• Public rebuke can harden people in their sins, rather than reforming them.
	• It can lead to a person defending their sin and becoming worse.

â€¢ ••Interpretation of 1 Timothy 5:20••
	• The verse is not a general commandment to reprove all sins openly.
	• It is a special commandment for church leaders to reprove elders who have been accused by witnesses.

â€¢ ••Order of Reproof••
	• We must try to reclaim our brother through private reproof first.
	• If that fails, we should take one or two witnesses to reprove the person.

â€¢ ••Reasons for Taking Witnesses••
	• To witness that the person is worthily reproved for their sin.
	• To convince the person of the act they have committed.
	• To witness that the person who reproves has discharged their duty.

â€¢ ••Proceeding Against Offenders••
	• We should not excommunicate offenders at the first instance.
	• We should proceed against them in an orderly and loving manner.

â€¢ ••Patience and Long-Suffering••
	• We should reprove with all long-suffering and patience.
	• We should avoid excommunicating offenders without first trying to reclaim them.

â€¢ ••Avoiding Heretics••
	• We should avoid heretics after one or two admonitions, as commanded by Paul in Titus 3:10.
	• However, this does not mean we should proceed against offenders in a harsh or public manner.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Christ's Commandment••
	• The text does not contradict Christ's commandment to restore a brother who falls into sin.
	• The commandment is given to pastors or bishops, who represent the whole church.
• ••Order of Proceeding••
	• The order of proceeding is to privately deal with the party, then publicly admonish them.
	• If the party does not recant, they are to be rejected as an heretic.
• ••Exceptions to the Order••
	• ••I. Danger to the Church or Commonwealth••
		- When the sin tends to hurt the church or commonwealth, and there is danger in delay.
		- In this case, the party is to be publicly detected and dealt with by the magistrate.
	• ••II. Greater Fault than Loss of Credit••
		- When the fault is greater than the loss of credit, even if it is published.
		- Examples include plotting treason or attempting murder.
	• ••III. Private Reproof Will Do No Good••
		- When private reproof will do no good, and the party will not take it.
		- In this case, the matter is to be reported to those who can and will redress it.

• ••Brotherly Love••
	• The apostle propounds another rule touching brotherly love.
	• This rule depends on the former doctrine, as an answer to a secret objection.
• ••Bearing One Another's Burdens••
	• Infirmities in brethren that cannot be amended by brotherly correction are to be borne and tolerated.
	• This is enforced by an argument taken from the excellency of the rule.
• ••Fulfilling the Law of Christ••
	• The rule of bearing one another's burdens is the keeping and fulfilling of the law of Christ.
	• This is a key principle of Christian living.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Law of Christ••
	• The apostle Paul commands us to "fulfill the law of Christ"
	• This involves bearing one another's burdens

â€¢ ••Types of Burdens••
	• Two types of burdens: those that must be borne alone, and those that can be shared with others
	• Four types of burdens that can be shared:
		- Poverty, sickness, nakedness, hunger, thirst, banishment, imprisonment, etc.
		- Outward and bodily wants (blindness, deafness, maimedness, lameness, envy, etc.)
		- Personal or actual sins (anger, hatred, jealousy, envy, etc.)
		- Outward frailties in actions (curiousness, niceness, slowness, self-conceitedness, frowardness, hastiness, etc.)

â€¢ ••Bearing Burdens••
	• Three ways to bear burdens:
		- Having a holy sympathy and fellow feeling (weeping with those who weep)
		- Bearing with others in their wants and infirmities
		- Helping and easing others in their necessities

â€¢ ••Bearing Personal Sins••
	• Not to be borne by:
		- Dissembling or yielding to them
		- Bolstering men up in them or partaking with them
	• Instead, to be borne by:
		- Gentle and mild admonition
		- Tolerating them and passing by them as if not perceived
		- Praying for them

â€¢ ••Importance of Bearing Burdens••
	• Necessary for leading a quiet and comfortable life in society
	• Essential for maintaining brotherly love and Christian society

â€¢ ••Consequences of Not Bearing Burdens••
	• Breaking the bond of brotherly love and Christian society
	• Walking in love is impossible if we do not bear one another's burdens

â€¢ ••Paul's Practice••
	• Paul practiced bearing burdens by:
		- Weeping with those who weep
		- Remembering those in bonds as if bound with them
		- Being weak and afflicted with those who are weak and afflicted

â€¢ ••Scriptural Examples••
	• Moses told Pharaoh not to leave a hoof behind
	• Paul would not give place to Peter and others for a moment to preserve the truth of the gospel

â€¢ ••Solomon's Wisdom••
	• It is the glory of man to pass by infirmity

â€¢ ••Ephesians 4:2••
	• We are to support one another through love

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The duty of bearing one another's burdens••

	• Especially by those who are strong in Christ

â€¢ ••The consequences of not bearing one another's burdens••

â€¢ ••The mutual duty of bearing one another's burdens••
	• Both strong and weak must bear one another's burdens

â€¢ ••The importance of bearing with one another's infirmities••

â€¢ ••Bearing with one another••

â€¢ ••The importance of considering the rule••

	• This is essential for fulfilling the law of Christ and covering a multitude of sins before God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Law of Christ••
	• Refers to the doctrine, precept, or commandment of Christ
	• Enjoins the love of our brethren
	• A new commandment given by Christ Himself

â€¢ ••Antithesis with the Law of Moses••
	• Paul uses the word "law" to contrast with the law of Moses
	• The law of Moses includes circumcision, days, times, months, and years
	• The law of Christ is a new and superior law

â€¢ ••Why is it called the Law of Christ?••
	• Because it is a new commandment given by Christ Himself
	• After a special manner, Christ commands the love of our brethren

â€¢ ••Why is it called a new commandment?••
	• Some say it is because it shows a new manner of loving our brethren
	• Others say it is because Christ loved us freely, and we should do the same

â€¢ ••Interpretation of "as I have loved you"••
	• Some say it means loving our brethren more than ourselves
	• However, this is not supported by Scripture (John 13:34-35, 1 John 2:7-8)

â€¢ ••The new commandment is simply "Love one another"••
	• Without any modification or limitation
	• Our Savior Himself says that by loving one another, all men will know we are His disciples

â€¢ ••The moral law is not an exact rule••
	• "Love thy neighbor as thyself" is not a precise measure of love
	• We are not required to love our neighbor more than ourselves in all cases

â€¢ ••The law of Christ is not a rule of supererogation••
	• We are not required to do more than the law requires
	• The law of Christ is a new and superior law that is necessary to be kept

â€¢ ••The law of Christ is not a leaden and false rule••
	• It is a true and living law that guides us in our relationships with others
	• It is a law that is necessary to be kept in order to fulfill the commandment of Christ

â€¢ ••The law of Christ is a law of love and freedom••
	• It is a law that commands us to love our brethren freely and without condition
	• It is a law that is necessary to be kept in order to fulfill the commandment of Christ.

â€¢ ••Understanding the "new commandment"••

â€¢ ••The "new commandment" in relation to us••
	• It is called "new" because it is renewed in our hearts by the Spirit of God
	• It is renewed in our minds, where we are taught to love our enemies and friends

â€¢ ••The characteristics of the "new commandment"••

â€¢ ••The significance of the "new commandment"••

â€¢ ••The Commandment to Love••

â€¢ ••The Law and the Gospel••
	• The law and the gospel agree in several things
	• They agree in their author (God), their preaching, and their general matter and end

â€¢ ••Differences between the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law and the gospel differ in five things
	• The law was known by nature before the fall, but the gospel was not

â€¢ ••The Manner of Revealing••
	• The law was revealed through nature, but the gospel is a mystery
	• The gospel requires a special revelation and work of God's Spirit

â€¢ ••The Subject of the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law preaches absolute justice to transgressors
	• The gospel shows how justice is qualified with mercy

â€¢ ••The Relationship between the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law and the gospel confirm and establish each other
	• The law is fulfilled in Christ, and we fulfill it by faith in Him

â€¢ ••The End of the Law••
	• The law is fulfilled in Christ, who has ended it for everyone who believes
	• The law is established by faith, which purifies the heart and leads to obedience

â€¢ ••The Glory of God••
	• Both the law and the gospel tend directly to the manifestation of God's glory
	• The law and the gospel are both revealed by God to show His glory

â€¢ ••The Gospel as a New Law••
	• The gospel is not a new law, but a new way of fulfilling the law
	• The gospel shows how to live according to the Spirit, rather than the flesh

â€¢ ••The Purpose of the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law and the gospel both require righteousness to come to eternal life
	• The law and the gospel both show the glory of God, but in different ways

Here are the 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The Law and the Gospel are Different••
	• The law cannot justify us, but the gospel can (Acts 13:39)
	• The law teaches what we should be and do, but the gospel shows us how to become that (2 Cor 5:21)

â€¢ ••The Law's Purpose••
	• The law is given to the unjust and lawless to show them their sins and the punishment they deserve (1 Tim 1:9-10)
	• The gospel is for the penitent, who are broken in heart and mourn for their sins (Matt 11, Isa 57, Luke 4)

â€¢ ••Promises of the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law promises eternal life on condition of works (Matt 19:17)
	• The gospel promises eternal life freely, without any condition of works (Rom 4:5, Rom 3:21-22)

â€¢ ••Effects of the Law and the Gospel••
	• The law is no instrumental cause of faith, repentance, or saving grace (2 Cor 3:7, Rom 4:15)
	• The gospel causes life and brings salvation (Titus 2:11)

â€¢ ••The Law's Impossibility••
	• No mere man can perfectly fulfill the law in this life (Rom 7)
	• The regenerate still have original corruption, which stains their best actions (Rom 7)

â€¢ ••Arguments Against Fulfilling the Law••
	• A corrupt fountain cannot send forth sweet waters (Matt 15:16-20)
	• A corrupt tree cannot bear good fruit (Matt 7:17-20)
	• St. James says that one who offends in one thing is guilty of all (James 2:10)

â€¢ ••Popish Doctors' Response••
	• They argue that original corruption is not a sin, but rather a fuel for sin
	• They claim that the first motions to evil are not sins, but rather a lack of consent of will

â€¢ ••Rebuttal to Popish Doctors••
	• Every transgression of the law is a sin (1 John 3:4)
	• The tenth commandment forbids the first motions to evil, whether with or without consent of will

â€¢ ••Paul's Teaching on Original Corruption••
	• Paul teaches that the motions preventing all consent of will are formally opposed to the law (Rom 7:23)
	• These motions are not sins, but rather a corruption that prevents us from fulfilling the law

â€¢ ••Augustine's Change of Opinion••
	• Augustine initially thought that Paul spoke of the unregenerate in Rom 7, but later retracted that opinion
	• Augustine's retraction was due to the fact that Paul's teaching on original corruption is not limited to the unregenerate.

â€¢ ••Paul's Confession••

â€¢ ••Roman Catholic Response••

â€¢ ••Critique of Roman Catholic Response••

â€¢ ••The Law••
	• There is one only rule of righteousness, not two
	• The law requires absolute obedience, not just for the life to come

â€¢ ••Adam's Obedience••

	• The law requires the same perfect obedience from us, even in the state of apostasy

â€¢ ••Consequences of the Law••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Argument 1: The Law is Impossible to Fulfill••
	• The law is impossible to fulfill for humans
	• Paul's statement "O miserable man that I am" (Rom. 7:24) suggests he is unable to fulfill the law
	• Peter calls the law a "yoke" that is too heavy to bear (Acts 15)

â€¢ ••Argument 2: The Need for a Mediator••
	• If humans could fulfill the law, there would be no need for a mediator (Christ)
	• Christ's death would be in vain if humans could fulfill the law on their own
	• The fulfilling of the law is by grace, not by human strength

â€¢ ••Argument 3: Christ as Savior••
	• Christ is not just an instrument for fulfilling the law, but a Savior
	• We are made righteous through Christ, not by our own efforts
	• We are complete in Christ, not by our own completeness

â€¢ ••Argument 4: The Universality of Sin••
	• The Bible teaches that all men are shut up under sin (Prov. 20:9, 1 John 1:8)
	• Even the most sanctified people are not immune to sin
	• Job and David both acknowledge their inability to perform good works

â€¢ ••Counterargument: Venial Sins••
	• Some argue that some sins are venial (not against the law) and do not prevent fulfillment of the law
	• However, this distinction is not supported by Scripture
	• All sins, even the smallest, deserve death and make a person subject to the curse

â€¢ ••Counterargument: Different Types of Works••
	• Some argue that the Bible is referring to different types of works and actions of the saints
	• However, this is not supported by Scripture, which often refers to the same individual work as both good and evil

â€¢ ••The Nature of Sin••
	• Sin is not just against the law, but also beside the law (i.e., idle words, jesting)
	• These types of sin are explicitly forbidden in Scripture
	• They are not just minor infractions, but rather serious offenses against God

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Sin••
	• All sins, even the smallest, deserve death and make a person subject to the curse
	• There is no distinction between mortal and venial sins in terms of their consequences

â€¢ ••The Role of Christ••
	• Christ is not just an instrument for fulfilling the law, but a Savior who makes us righteous
	• We are complete in Christ, not by our own completeness

â€¢ ••The Universality of Human Inability••
	• The Bible teaches that all men are shut up under sin and are unable to perform good works
	• This is not just a matter of individual weakness, but rather a universal human condition.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The law is impossible to fulfill in this life••
	• The author argues that no one can perfectly fulfill the law in their lifetime

â€¢ ••Why fulfill the law if we can't perfectly do it?••
	• The law can be fulfilled in three ways:
		- Personal obedience (only Christ has done this)
		- Imputed obedience (regenerate people fulfill the law in Christ)
		- Inchoate obedience (faithful people strive to obey God, but imperfectly)

â€¢ ••Fulfilling the law is not always about perfection••
	• Sometimes it means opposing transgression of the law (only Christ has done this)
	• Sometimes it means opposing hypocrisy and dissimulation (all saints strive to do this)

â€¢ ••The law has three uses••
	• To restrain the outward man through fear of punishment
	• To arouse the drowsy conscience by revealing sin, wrath, and conviction
	• To serve as a rule of good life, showing what is good and evil

â€¢ ••The law is not just for the righteous••
	• It is given to the lawless and disobedient to keep them in order
	• It is a schoolmaster that brings us to Christ

â€¢ ••The law is not just about external actions••

â€¢ ••The law is a lantern to our feet and a light to our paths••
	• It shows us what is good and evil
	• It guides us in our daily lives

â€¢ ••Fulfilling the law is not just about individual effort••
	• It is also about community and mutual support
	• It is about bearing one another's burdens and supporting each other in love.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The natural tendency to overestimate oneself••
	• People tend to think too highly of themselves and their abilities.
	• This is a form of pride, which is a fundamental human flaw.
	• It leads to a distorted view of oneself and others.

â€¢ ••The dangers of self-love••
	• When people are in love with themselves, they become blind to their own flaws.
	• They see their own weaknesses as virtues and their own strengths as extraordinary.
	• This self-love can lead to a false sense of self-importance.

â€¢ ••Examples of overestimating oneself••
	• Simon Magus, a sorcerer, thought himself to be a great man with great power.
	• The church of Laodicea thought itself to be rich and self-sufficient, despite being spiritually poor.
	• The scarlet strumpet in Revelation thought herself to be a queen, despite being morally corrupt.

â€¢ ••The corrupting influence of knowledge and gifts••
	• Even those who have received great gifts of knowledge or sanctification can become proud and overestimate themselves.
	• This is because knowledge can puff people up and make them think they are superior to others.

â€¢ ••The apostles' own struggles with pride••
	• Even the apostles contended with each other over who was the greatest.
	• This shows that even those who are close to God can struggle with pride and self-importance.

â€¢ ••Historical examples of pride and self-importance••
	• The Pharisees, who thought themselves to be righteous and superior to others.
	• The Catharists, who thought themselves to be without sin.
	• The Donatists, who thought themselves to be a church without spot or wrinkle.

â€¢ ••The causes of pride and self-importance••
	• The bitter root of pride that was present in the first parents.
	• Overestimating one's own good qualities and accomplishments.
	• Comparing oneself to others' weaknesses and infirmities.
	• False flattering and applause from others.

â€¢ ••The consequences of pride and self-importance••
	• People deceive themselves about their own abilities and worth.
	• They become blind to their own flaws and weaknesses.
	• They can become isolated and disconnected from others.

â€¢ ••The need for humility and self-awareness••
	• People need to recognize their own flaws and weaknesses.
	• They need to be aware of their tendency to overestimate themselves.
	• They need to cultivate humility and a sense of their own limitations.

â€¢ ••The importance of God's grace and intervention••
	• God's grace is necessary to resist the temptation to pride and self-importance.
	• People need to seek God's help and guidance to overcome their own flaws and weaknesses.

â€¢ ••Deception by Others••

	• This deception is occasional and not a fundamental issue

	• Men can also deceive themselves, which is a more fundamental issue

â€¢ ••Consequences of Self-Deception••
	• Men deceive themselves in their imagination, leading to vain religion
	• They may appear to others as something they are not, to gain prestige

â€¢ ••Examples of Self-Deception••

	• Men who are proud and conceited are easiest deceived, even by themselves

â€¢ ••The Nature of Self-Deception••
	• Men deceive themselves to appear better to others, rather than being genuinely good
	• This self-deception is a form of spiritual guile, which is most dangerous

â€¢ ••The Danger of Self-Deception••
	• Men delude themselves into thinking they know God and their neighbor, when they do not
	• This leads to damnable pride and presumption, rather than genuine faith

â€¢ ••The Indignity of Self-Deception••
	• Men are willing to be deceived by others, but not by themselves
	• This is an indignity, especially in matters of greatest importance, such as salvation

â€¢ ••The Ease of Self-Deception••
	• Men are easily deceived by the devil, because they are already willing to deceive themselves
	• This makes them vulnerable to the devil's seduction

â€¢ ••The Importance of Humility••
	• Men who are proud and conceited are easiest deceived, even by themselves
	• Humility is essential to avoiding self-deception and spiritual guile

â€¢ ••The Call to Humility••
	• No man, regardless of their gifts or position, is too good to bear other men's burdens
	• Men should be willing to put others before themselves, rather than seeking to appear better to others.

â€¢ ••The Problem of Self-Conceit••

â€¢ ••The Consequences of Self-Conceit••

â€¢ ••Recognizing our true nature••

â€¢ ••Using our own evils as a remedy••
	• We must consider our own sins, blemishes, and imperfections
	• This can help us nip pride in the bud and kill the serpent of self-love
	• We should focus on our own weaknesses and flaws, rather than our strengths

â€¢ ••Considering the things we possess as lent to us••
	• We have not earned or deserved the gifts and graces we possess
	• We are only stewards and bailiffs of these things, not owners
	• We must be accountable for how we use these gifts, and give an account of our stewardship

â€¢ ••The importance of humility••
	• We should be humbled by the consideration of God's strict reckoning
	• We are but worms in comparison to God's majesty
	• We should not be puffed up with pride, but rather recognize our own insignificance

â€¢ ••Comparing ourselves to God••
	• We are less than nothing and vanity itself in comparison to God
	• Every knee and tongue should bow to God, and we should not be conceited
	• We should recognize our place in the universe and our dependence on God

â€¢ ••The dangers of pride••
	• Pride can lead us to sacrifice to our own nets and burn incense to our own yarn
	• We should not boast of things that are not our own, but rather recognize our dependence on God

â€¢ ••The importance of self-examination••
	• We should regularly examine ourselves and our own hearts
	• We should consider our own weaknesses and flaws, and seek to improve
	• We should not be satisfied with our own self-love and self-liking

â€¢ ••The need for repentance••
	• We should repent in dust and ashes, recognizing our own wretched estate
	• We should seek to become flat nothing in ourselves, that we may be something in Christ
	• We should be willing to deny ourselves and abhor ourselves, in order to become humble and dependent on God

â€¢ ••The call to wisdom••
	• We should seek to be wise, but not in the world's wisdom
	• We should be willing to become fools, in order to become wise in God's eyes
	• We should recognize that true wisdom comes from God, and that we are but foolish in comparison.

â€¢ ••True Glorying••

	• Aims at the glory of God alone

		- "Our glorying is this, the testimony of our conscience, that in simplicity and godly sincerity, we have had our conversation in the world" (2 Cor. 1:12)

â€¢ ••Glorying Before God and Men••

		- "For no flesh can glory in his presence" (1 Cor. 1:29)

		- "Our glorying is this, the testimony of our conscience, that in simplicity and godly sincerity, we have had our conversation in the world" (2 Cor. 1:12)

â€¢ ••Vain Glory vs. True Glorying••

	• True glorying: grounded in the fruits of regeneration, aims at the glory of God alone

â€¢ ••Comparing Ourselves with Others••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Objection 1: Bearing one's own burden••
	• Paul says "every man shall bear his own burden" (Galatians 6:5)
	• However, the apostle also says "bear ye one another's burdens" (Galatians 6:2)
	• The answer is that we are to bear the burdens of our brothers, not just our own

â€¢ ••Objection 2: Giving account for others••
	• Every man is not to give account for himself alone, but for those committed to his care
	• Governors and superiors are not to give account for the sins of those under their care, but for their own failure to guide and correct them
	• The example is given in Ezekiel 33:8-9, where a shepherd is held accountable for not warning the wicked of their way

â€¢ ••Objection 3: Infants bearing the burden of Adam's sin••
	• Infants are said to bear the burden of Adam's sin
	• However, the answer is that this is not original sin, but rather personal or actual sins
	• Infants partake in Adam's sin and therefore bear the punishment, but this is not the same as bearing the burden of their own personal sins

â€¢ ••Objection 3 (continued)••
	• Adam's sin is considered our original sin, and therefore we are held accountable for it
	• This is not a matter of personal guilt, but rather a matter of imputation and propagation of sin through nature

â€¢ ••Objection 4: Visiting the sins of the fathers••
	• The second commandment threatens to visit the sins of the fathers upon the children to the third and fourth generation
	• However, this does not contradict the principle that the son shall not bear the iniquity of the father
	• The answer is that the threat is conditional, and only applies if the children persist in their sins and walk in their father's ways

â€¢ ••Objection 4 (continued)••
	• The example is given in Ezekiel 18:14-17, where a wicked man's son is not held accountable for his father's sins if he fears and does not follow in his father's ways
	• The same principle is applied in the second commandment, where God will not visit the sin of the fathers if the children turn from their wicked ways

â€¢ ••The principle of bearing one's own burden••
	• The principle is that every man shall bear his own burden, but also bear the burdens of his brothers
	• This is not a matter of giving account for others, but rather a matter of taking responsibility for one's own actions and the well-being of those under one's care

â€¢ ••The role of governors and superiors••
	• Governors and superiors are not to give account for the sins of those under their care, but for their own failure to guide and correct them
	• This is a matter of taking responsibility for one's own actions and the well-being of those under one's care

â€¢ ••The nature of original sin••
	• Original sin is considered our sin, and therefore we are held accountable for it
	• This is not a matter of personal guilt, but rather a matter of imputation and propagation of sin through nature

â€¢ ••The principle of conditional responsibility••
	• The principle is that responsibility is conditional, and only applies if one persists in their sins and walks in their father's ways
	• This is illustrated in Ezekiel 18:14-17, where a wicked man's son is not held accountable for his father's sins if he fears and does not follow in his father's ways.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Objection 1: Children are not punished for their parents' sins••
	• The text argues that children are punished for their parents' sins, but not vindicated by them
	• Four reasons are given:
		- Children are a part of their parents, so punishing them punishes the parents too
		- Parents are tormented by knowing their children will be punished
		- The punishment of posterity relates to the parent, as God threatens to punish children for their parents' sins
		- Parents' sins often cause their children's sins, and they deserve punishment for their own sins

â€¢ ••Objection 2: The chief Israelites were punished for their own sins••
	• The text argues that the chief Israelites were punished for their own sins, not their children's
	• They were punished for their own idolatry and fornication, and were principal ringleaders in the sin
	• A thousand of them were hanged up, and the rest of the people were slain by the sword

â€¢ ••Objection 3: David and others were punished for their own sins••
	• The text argues that David and others were punished for their own sins, not their children's
	• David was punished for his own sin of numbering the people, and the people were punished for their own sins
	• Achan was punished for his own sin, and the people fell slain before the men of Ai

â€¢ ••Objection 4: Children of the Sodomites and Egyptians were punished for their parents' sins••

	• However, the text also argues that the people were punished for their own sins, and that the children's punishment was deserved

â€¢ ••Answer to Objection 4••
	• The text argues that the children of the Sodomites and Egyptians were punished for their own sins, not their parents'
	• Their death was deserved, even though they could not imitate or approve their parents' actions

â€¢ ••Contradiction between Moses and Paul••
	• The text argues that there is no contradiction between Moses and Paul, despite a seeming discrepancy in the numbers of people punished
	• The text suggests that Paul is not contrary to Moses, and that the numbers are not inconsistent

â€¢ ••Reason for Paul's use of a smaller number••
	• The text suggests that Paul used a smaller number because it was a more round number, and not because he was contradicting Moses

â€¢ ••God's mercy and justice••
	• The text argues that God's mercy is that we are not consumed by our sins, and that we deserve punishment for our own sins

â€¢ ••Punishment of infants••
	• The text argues that even infants who perished in the deluge and in the overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah deserved punishment for their parents' sins

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• The text concludes that children are punished for their parents' sins, but not vindicated by them, and that the punishment of posterity relates to the parent.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••1. Profaneness and Infidelity••
• The author criticizes those who profane the name of God by trivializing the threats of God.
• They use phrases like "the burden of the Lord" in a flippant manner, implying that God's threats are empty.

••2. Downplaying Sin••
• Some people downplay the severity of sin, saying it's not as ugly or hell is not as hot as preachers claim.
• They also believe that they won't be alone in their sins and will share the burden with others.

••3. The Burden of Sin••
• The author emphasizes that the burden of sin is unbearable and will lead to eternal damnation.
• Sin will press down on individuals and condemn them to the "bottomless pit".

••4. No Sin is Small••
• The author warns that even small sins have weight and can condemn individuals.
• Many small sins can be as condemning as a few great sins.

••5. The Weight of Sin••
• The author compares the weight of sin to a great mountain that presses down on individuals.
• This weight can be oppressive and unbearable.

••6. Repentance and Faith••
• The author emphasizes the importance of repentance and faith in Christ to ease the burden of sin.
• Jesus promises to ease the burden of those who come to him (Matthew 11:28).

••7. Maintenance of the Ministry••
• The apostle Paul lays down a rule for the maintenance of the ministry in Galatians.
• The rule is: "Let him that is taught in the word, make him that hath taught him, partaker of all his goods."

••8. Meaning of the Rule••
• The author explains that the rule applies to anyone who is taught or instructed in the word.
• The word "taught" has a broader meaning, including instruction in the gospel.

••9. Reasons for the Rule••
• The author suggests that the ministry among the Galatians was neglected and not respected as it should be.
• The rule is intended to promote the well-being of the ministry and its leaders.

••10. Doctrine and Uses••
• The author will discuss the objections to the rule and the doctrine and uses that can be gathered from it.
• The rule is intended to promote the maintenance and respect of the ministry.

â€¢ ••The Importance of Gratitude••

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Arguments for a set stipend for ministers••
	• Cuts off flattery and suspicion of flattery
	• Does not disgrace the poorer sort
	• Eliminates dissembling and deceit
	• Makes it harder for evil-disposed people to leave their minister
	• Reduces ostentation in giving
	• Eliminates suspicion of covetousness
	• Preserves the dignity of the ministry
	• Aligns with the Levitical law

â€¢ ••Objection: Ministers do not work hard••
	• Ministers do not engage in physical labor
	• Their work is easy and painless
	• Therefore, they should not be maintained

â€¢ ••Response to the objection••
	• There is a twofold labor: physical and mental
	• Mental labor is more painful and consuming
	• The ministry is a worthy work (Eph. 4:12, 1 Tim. 3:1)
	• Scripture compares the ministry to toilsome labor (e.g. husbandman, builder, shepherd)

â€¢ ••Scripture supports the idea that ministry is hard work••
	• Paul exhorts the Thessalonians to know and love their ministers (1 Thess. 5:12-13)
	• Scripture compares the ministry to various forms of hard labor (e.g. plowing, sowing, reaping)

â€¢ ••Don't judge the painfulness of the ministry by its outward appearance••
	• A king's role and care are far greater than they seem
	• A master builder's labor is double that of his workmen
	• A ship's master guides the ship, but his labor is greater than that of the sailors

â€¢ ••The ministry is a high calling••
	• It requires great care and responsibility
	• It is a worthy work that deserves honor and respect

â€¢ ••The objection that ministers do not work hard is misguided••
	• It judges the ministry by its outward appearance, rather than its true nature
	• It fails to recognize the mental and spiritual labor involved in the ministry

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Objection 1: Ministers should support themselves••
	• Ministers seem to do nothing, but their labor is double and treble that of other men
	• They should maintain themselves by their own labors, not be chargeable to the church

â€¢ ••Answer to Objection 1: Paul's example••
	• Paul labored with his hands to make tents, but received contributions from other churches
	• Paul's example does not prove that ministers should live by their own labors

â€¢ ••Reasons for Paul's refusal of wages••
	• Lest he be a burden to the church
	• To give a precedent for others to follow
	• To manifest his end in teaching the gospel, not to seek himself but the salvation of his hearers
	• To confound false apostles who received wages for their labor

â€¢ ••Objection 2: Ministers should not receive wages••
	• Paul's example proves that ministers should not receive wages
	• Ministers should maintain themselves by their own labors

â€¢ ••Answer to Objection 2: Paul's example is impertinent••
	• Paul himself received wages from other churches
	• Paul's refusal of wages was on special consideration
	• Paul commanded that ministers should be maintained

â€¢ ••Objection 3: "Freely ye have received, freely give"••
	• This text is specially understood of the apostles' extraordinary gift of working miracles
	• It forbids seeking to enrich themselves or win reputation and glory among men

â€¢ ••Answer to Objection 3: The text is not a blanket prohibition••
	• The text forbids making merchandise of the word of God, not receiving wages
	• The text is a precept forbidding filthy lucre, not receiving support from the church

â€¢ ••Reasons for the precept••
	• The graces of God exhibited in preaching are precious and cannot be valued at any price
	• Ministers should not seek to benefit themselves, but rather the glory of God and the salvation of their hearers

â€¢ ••The precept is not a blanket prohibition••
	• The precept forbids making merchandise of the word of God, not receiving wages
	• The precept is a reminder of the importance of ministering for the glory of God and the salvation of others

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Ministers should be supported by the church, not live by their own labors
	• The precept "Freely ye have received, freely give" is not a blanket prohibition on receiving wages, but rather a reminder of the importance of ministering for the glory of God and the salvation of others.

â€¢ ••The Resurrection••
	• The body will be partaker with the soul in either misery or felicity.

â€¢ ••Rewarding Defects••

â€¢ ••Punishment and Reward••

	• Nadab and Abihu were punished with fire from heaven (Lev. 10:1-2)

â€¢ ••God punishes sinners with the same sin••
	• Adonibezek was punished with the same cruelty he inflicted on others (Judg. 1:7)
	• Agag's sword was used against him, making his mother childless (1 Sam. 15:33)
	• David's adultery and murder were punished in his own family (2 Sam. 12:10)

â€¢ ••God punishes spiritual sins with physical consequences••
	• The Israelites' spiritual adultery was punished with physical harlotry (Hos. 4:12-13)
	• The Church of Rome's spiritual fornication is punished with physical pollution (Hos. 4:12-13)

â€¢ ••God punishes sinners with the same sin, but in a worse way••
	• The rich glutton was punished with a lack of water to cool his thirst (Luke 16)
	• The heathen were punished with unclean minds and bodies (Romans 1:24-27)

â€¢ ••God punishes sinners with shame and humiliation••
	• The women of Jerusalem were punished with shame and humiliation (Isa. 3:24)
	• The house of Abimelech was punished with childlessness (Gen. 20:18)

â€¢ ••God punishes sinners with a fitting punishment••
	• The Israelites were punished with a multitude of unreasoning beasts (Wis. 11:15-16)
	• Job acknowledges that God's punishment is just and fitting (Job 31:1-4)

â€¢ ••God's punishment is a warning to others••
	• The punishment of the wicked is a warning to others to avoid sin (Prov. 23:29-31)
	• The punishment of the proud is a warning to others to avoid pride (Isa. 3:24)

â€¢ ••God's punishment is a demonstration of His justice••
	• God's punishment is a demonstration of His justice and righteousness (Ps. 19:9)
	• God's punishment is a warning to others to fear and obey Him (Prov. 1:7)

	• Argue that good works are the proper cause of eternal life
	• Claim that seeds have a hidden virtue to bring forth fruit, just like good works

	• The scope of the similitude is to show that good works are not the cause of eternal life
	• The analogy between seed and good works is not meant to imply that good works have a hidden virtue

â€¢ ••Papists' Doctrine on Good Works••
	• Teach that good works are meritorious by merit of condignity

â€¢ ••Rebuttal to Papists' Argument••

	• Eternal life is a gift of God's mercy and promise

â€¢ ••Conclusion••
	• Sowing to the Spirit leads to life everlasting, while sowing to the flesh leads to corruption and eternal death.

• ••The Last Judgment••
	• The last judgment is not to make men just, but to manifest them to the world as they truly are.
	• Men are judged by their works, not by their faith.
• ••Faith and Works••
	• Faith makes men just, but works make them known to be just.
	• A tree is known to be good by its fruit, not by its sap.
• ••The Judgment by Works••
	• The last judgment will proceed according to evidence that is on record.
	• Men will be judged by their works, which are evident and apparent to all.
• ••The Role of Works••
	• Works are not the cause of reward, but only the common measure by which God gives greater or lesser reward.
	• Works are like a king's highway to eternal life, prepared by God for us to walk in.
• ••The Meaning of "For"••
	• The word "for" does not always signify a cause, but can imply any argument or reason.
	• In the text "Come ye blessed, for I was hungry, and ye gave me meat", "for" does not imply a cause, but only a sign that the person is indeed blessed.
• ••The Nature of Good Works••
	• Good works are not meritorious causes of eternal life, but rather the means by which we come to eternal life.
	• Good works are like climbing and digging to obtain a treasure that was freely given.
• ••The Parity of Reason••
	• The same reasoning applies to both the reward of the godly and the punishment of the wicked.
	• Both are based on the principle that good works lead to eternal life, while wicked works lead to perdition.
• ••The Reward of Good Works••
	• The reward of good works is not based on merit, but rather on the principle of God's justice.
	• Good works are like a means to an end, rather than the end itself.
• ••The Punishment of Wicked Works••
	• Wicked works lead to perdition, not because they are punished, but because they are the natural consequence of a wicked life.
	• Wicked works are like a path that leads to destruction, rather than a means to salvation.
• ••The Conclusion••
	• The text argues that men will be judged by their works, not by their faith.
	• The reward of good works is not based on merit, but rather on the principle of God's justice.

â€¢ ••Objection I: Good works are the cause of eternal life••
	• The objection is that good works are not only the way to eternal life, but also the cause of it.
	• The answer is that good works are the way, but not the cause, as they are a means to an end, not the end itself.

â€¢ ••The legal and evangelical covenants••
	• The legal covenant promises eternal life to those who keep the commandments.
	• However, no one can fulfill the law, so no one can merit eternal life through good works.
	• The evangelical covenant promises eternal life to those who are faithful, but not for the merit of their works, but for the merit of Christ.

â€¢ ••The promise is made to the worker, not the work••
	• The promise is made to the person who is faithful, not to their fidelity.
	• Fidelity is a sign that the person is in Christ, and it is through Christ that the promise is made.

â€¢ ••The reward is not due to the merit of the work••
	• If anything is due to works, it is not because of the merit of the work, but because of God's merciful promise.
	• Augustine says that God made Himself a debtor by promising, not by owing anything.

â€¢ ••The covenant of works vs. the covenant of grace••
	• We are not under the covenant of works, but under the covenant of grace.
	• The covenant of grace is based on the merits of Christ, not on our own works.

â€¢ ••The pillars of the Romish church are wrong••
	• The pillars of the Romish church teach that the promise made upon condition of performing the work makes the performer merit.
	• This is not sufficient to make a meritorious work, as the work must be answerable and correspondent in worth and value to the reward.

â€¢ ••The reward is not always due to merit••
	• The reward can be due to favor, not just debt.
	• Ambrose says that there is one reward of liberality and favor, and another reward which is the stipend of virtue and recompense of our labor.

â€¢ ••The double reward••
	• There is a double reward, one of favor and another of debt.
	• The reward of favor is not always due to merit, but is a gift bestowed upon another.

â€¢ ••Eternal life is a reward, but not a proper reward••
	• Eternal life is called a reward in a general signification, when it is used absolutely and not relatively.
	• It is not a proper reward, but is called a reward because it is given at the end of a man's life, after their travail and warfare is ended.

â€¢ ••The Hebrew and Greek words for reward and end are used interchangeably••
	• The Hebrew word for reward also means heel or end.
	• The Greek words for reward and end are used indifferently one for the other.

â€¢ ••The Timing of the Reward••
	• The reward will come "in due season" (1 Cor. 15:58)
	• We must be patient and trust in God's timing

â€¢ ••The Condition for Receiving the Reward••
	• We must not faint or give up in our efforts (1 Cor. 15:58)
	• We must persevere in our good works and trust in God's promise

â€¢ ••The Promise of Reward in Scripture••
	• Jesus teaches that we should make friends with "unrighteous mammon" (Luke 16:9)
	• Paul exhorts servants to work heartily, knowing they will receive a reward from the Lord (Col. 3:23-24)

â€¢ ••The Promise of Reward in Practice••
	• Abraham was willing to leave his native country and dwell in a strange land because he looked for a city with a foundation (Heb. 11:9-10)
	• Moses chose to suffer with Christ rather than enjoy the treasures of Egypt (Heb. 11:26)

â€¢ ••The Example of Christ••
	• Christ endured the cross and despised the shame because of the joy that was set before Him (Heb. 12:2)
	• Christ's example is one of perseverance and trust in God's promise

â€¢ ••The Practice of the Saints••
	• The Colossians continued and increased in faith and love because of the hope laid up for them in heaven (Col. 1:5)
	• Paul encourages us to run in such a way that we may obtain the prize (1 Cor. 9:24)

â€¢ ••The Reason for the Promise••
	• The promise is based on God's character and faithfulness
	• God is a rewarder of those who seek Him (Heb. 11:6)

â€¢ ••The Importance of Considering the Reward••
	• We should consider the reward and let it motivate us to greater alacrity in our Christian walk
	• We should trust in God's promise and persevere in our good works.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The End of Our Actions••
	• The end of our faith and hope is eternal life (Rom. 6:22, 1 Peter 1:9)
	• We should direct all our actions towards attaining this end

â€¢ ••Motivation for Good Works••
	• Christians can do good in hope of eternal reward (1 Cor. 9:25)
	• This is a lawful motivation, as it is for a laborer or a husbandman

â€¢ ••Patience and Perseverance••
	• We should be patient and settle our hearts for the coming of the Lord (James 5:7-8)
	• God will recompense every man according to his works

â€¢ ••Fear of Punishment vs. Hope of Reward••
	• It is lawful to abstain from sin for fear of punishment (Matt. 10:28)
	• It is also lawful to do good in hope of eternal reward

â€¢ ••The Property of a Hireling••
	• A hireling looks only for his hire, not for the glory and honor of God
	• This is not the case for Christians, who should prioritize God's glory

â€¢ ••The Promise of Reward••
	• God promises to give eternal life to those who seek honor and immortality (Rom. 2:7)
	• This promise is a motivation for good works, but not the only motivation

â€¢ ••The Impudence of Critics••
	• The Rhemists and others have falsely accused us of teaching that no man should do good in respect of reward
	• This is not our doctrine, but rather a motivation for good works

â€¢ ••The Importance of Duty••
	• We should do our duty, even if there were no reward or punishment
	• The love of God should constrain us to do good works

â€¢ ••The Endless Love and Mercy of God••
	• God holds out His golden scepter to us, offering eternal life
	• He pleases to win us by gifts, incite us by rewards, and allure us by promises

â€¢ ••The Relationship Between Reward and Duty••
	• We should prioritize the glory of God and the performance of our duty
	• The hope of reward should be a secondary motivation for good works

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Definition of Goodness••
	• Goodness is a property of God that we should strive to resemble
	• It is a blessed thing to give rather than to receive (Acts 20:35)

â€¢ ••Threefold Nature of Goodness••
	• Persevering goodness: doing good to others and keeping them from sin
	• Uniting goodness: setting men at unity and reconciling those in conflict
	• Communicating goodness: sharing blessings and resources with others

â€¢ ••Persevering Goodness••
	• Doing good to others, not just ourselves
	• Laboring to keep others from sin and backsliding
	• Examples: Barnabas, who confirmed the Antioch church in their faith

â€¢ ••Uniting Goodness••
	• Setting men at unity and reconciling those in conflict
	• Making peace and amity where there is enmity and dissension
	• Example: Christ calls peacemakers the children of God (Matt. 5:9)

â€¢ ••Communicating Goodness••
	• Sharing temporal and spiritual blessings with others
	• Examples: Paul's exhortation to minister to others (1 Peter 4:10)
	• Being plentiful in works of mercy and good deeds

â€¢ ••Degrees of Communicating Goodness••
	• Temporal things: communicating to the necessities of the saints (Rom. 12:13)
	• Spiritual blessings: ministering to others with the manifold grace of God (1 Peter 4:10)
	• Works of mercy: being rich in good works and ready to distribute (1 Tim. 6:18)

â€¢ ••Abundant Goodness••
	• Being abundant in goodness, communicating blessings to others
	• Examples: Job, who was blessed by the poor; the virtuous woman who opens her hands to the poor (Prov. 31:20)

â€¢ ••Exceeding Goodness••
	• Exceeding measure in doing good
	• Examples: The poor widow who gave all she had to the treasury of the Lord's temple (Mark 12:41-44)

â€¢ ••Characteristics of Goodness••
	• Being liberal and generous in supplying the wants of others
	• Being ready to distribute and communicate (1 Tim. 6:18)

â€¢ ••Examples of Goodness••
	• Tabitha (Dorcas), who clothed the poor with her own hands (Acts 9:39)
	• Job, who was blessed by the poor (Job 31:20)
	• Obadiah, who hid a hundred of the Lord's prophets from Jezebel (1 Kings 18:13)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Giving from one's own resources••
	• We should give from our own surplus, not from someone else's.
	• We should not steal or deal unjustly with others to give to someone else.

â€¢ ••Cheerfulness and alacrity in giving••
	• We should give with a cheerful and willing heart.
	• A gift given unwillingly is not a true gift.

â€¢ ••Not disabling oneself from future good deeds••
	• We should not give so much that we are unable to continue doing good.
	• We should be mindful of our own well-being and not sacrifice it for the sake of giving.

â€¢ ••Giving according to one's ability••
	• We should give according to our means, not beyond what we can afford.
	• We should not give so much that we are pinched or unable to meet our own needs.

â€¢ ••Doing good within the compass of one's calling••
	• We should do good within the scope of our duties and responsibilities.
	• We should not try to do more than we are capable of doing.

â€¢ ••Not imitating God's example in doing good••
	• We are not bound to do all the good we can, like God does.
	• We are subject to the law and should not try to do evil to achieve good.

â€¢ ••Three reasons why we should not imitate God's example••
	• We are subject to the law and should not try to do evil to achieve good.
	• We are not able to draw good out of evil, like God can.
	• We are particular goods, not general goods, and our role is different.

â€¢ ••The nature of the general good••
	• The general good is concerned with the overall well-being of all things.
	• The general good is not concerned with doing all the good we can or preventing all evil.

â€¢ ••The nature of the particular good••
	• The particular good is concerned with doing all the good we can and preventing all evil.
	• The particular good is concerned with the well-being of individuals and communities.

â€¢ ••The importance of discretion in giving••
	• We should be discreet and wise in our giving, not reckless or extravagant.
	• We should consider the consequences of our giving and ensure that it does not harm ourselves or others.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••The duty to do good••
	• We must do good to all people, regardless of their character or actions
	• This is in line with Jesus' teaching to "love your enemies, bless them that curse you, do good to them that hate you, pray for them that hurt you and persecute you" (Matt. 5:44)
• ••The importance of doing good to all••
	• We should do good to our enemies, just as the Good Samaritan did (Luke 10:30-37)
	• We should show kindness to our enemies' property, such as their animals, and even more so to the person themselves
• ••The reasons for doing good to all••
	• ••Reason 1: Love and beneficence are in all men••
		- All men have the image of God within them, which should draw out our love and affection
		- We are all part of the same human nature, and should show kindness to one another
		- We participate in the death of Christ, and should show love to all people as a result
	• ••Reason 2: God is good and bountiful to all••
		- God causes His sun to shine and His rain to fall on both the good and the bad
		- We should follow God's example and show kindness to all people
	• ••Reason 3: We should do to others as we would have them do to us••
		- If we would be glad to receive good from a wicked person, we should do good to them in return
	• ••Reason 4: Our profession and reward require us to do good to all••
		- If we only do good to those who do good to us, we are no different from publicans
		- Our reward in this life is not enough, and we should expect a greater reward in the next life if we do good to all people.
