Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

••I. The Importance of Doing Good While We Have Time••

• We must do good while we have time, as tomorrow may be too late (Prov. 3:28)
	• Delaying can lead to discouragement, hardening of heart, or loss of riches
	• Our Savior bids us "walk in the light, while we have light" (John 12:35)

••II. Redeeming the Time••

• We must redeem the time by employing it fruitfully (Eph. 5:16)
	• Not letting time slip away due to sloth, idleness, or vain pleasures
	• Gaining lost time with double diligence and redeeming it with loss of ease and profits

••III. The Shortness, Preciousness, and Irrevocability of Time••

• Time is short, precious, and irrevocable
	• Guided by diligence due to its shortness
	• Valued highly and not lavishly spent due to its preciousness
	• Redeemed by taking opportunities due to its irrevocability

••IV. Knowing Times and Seasons••

• Paul commands us to know times and seasons to observe the shortness of time
	• Numbering our days to apply our hearts to wisdom
	• Not knowing and observing time is a sin, as seen in Matthew 16:3 and Luke 12:56

••V. The Importance of Knowing the Time of Mercy and Grace••

• Even brute beasts know their appointed times and seasons
	• But God's people often do not know the time of mercy and grace
	• Christ threatens Jerusalem due to their ignorance of the time of visitation (Luke 19:44)

••VI. The Day of Salvation••

• We must know the day of our visitation, the acceptable time, and the day of salvation
	• When God offers mercy by rising early and calling us through His word
	• If we do not listen, we will stand with the five foolish virgins and be shut out of heaven (Matt. 25:11-12)

••VII. The Two Types of Observing Time••

• There is a twofold observing of time: good and evil, lawful and unlawful
	• Unlawful and superstitious observation of time is forbidden (Gal. 4:10-11)
	• Good and lawful observation of time is encouraged

••VIII. The Jewish and Superstitious Observation of Times••

• The Jewish and superstitious observation of times is when religion is placed in the keeping of them
	• This is an opinion that binds the conscience to the strict observing of times
	• This is an unlawful and superstitious observation of time

••IX. The Importance of Observing Time••

• Observing time is not forbidden, but rather a good and lawful thing
	• We must observe the time of mercy and grace to be saved

••X. Conclusion••

• We must redeem the time, know the times and seasons, and observe the time of mercy and grace to be saved
	• This is a crucial aspect of our Christian walk and a key to eternal life.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Forbidden Observances••
	• Observing days and months in relation to good or bad success
	• Counting leap years as ominous
	• Observing planetary hours and climacteric years
	• Using the horoscope or time of birth to determine fate
• ••Paul's Concerns••
	• The Galatians observed Jewish ceremonies and heathenish superstitions
	• These practices were vain and unlawful
• ••Heathenish Superstitions••
	• Observing unlucky days and months
	• Changing the name of a month to overcome superstition (e.g. July instead of Quintilis)
• ••Examples of Superstition••
	• Alexander the Great changed the name of June to July to overcome superstition
	• Lucullus changed an unlucky day to a fortunate one
	• The same day can be lucky or unlucky depending on the individual
• ••Lawful Observing of Time••
	• ••Human••: observing time in relation to natural, civil, and ecclesiastical matters
		- Natural: observing the motion of the sun, moon, and stars
		- Civil: observing time in relation to politics and the common good
		- Ecclesiastical: observing time in the church for order and worship
	• ••Divine••: observing time in relation to God's visitation and judgment
• ••Divine Observance••
	• Preparing for death and eternity
	• Using time to apply one's heart to wisdom
	• Observing the day of God's merciful visitation
	• Hiding oneself under God's wings in times of judgment
• ••The Importance of Time••
	• Time is limited and fleeting
	• We should use time to do good and benefit others
	• After death, all possibility of doing good is cut off
• ••Civil Observance••
	• Observing time in relation to politics and the common good
	• Examples include fair times, market times, and term times
• ••Ecclesiastical Observance••
	• Observing time in the church for order and worship
	• Examples include festival days and solemnities
• ••The Purpose of Observance••
	• To prepare for death and eternity
	• To use time to apply one's heart to wisdom
	• To observe the day of God's merciful visitation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Paul's Introduction••
	• Paul commends his diligence, love, and care for the Galatians
	• He writes a large epistle, which is unusual for him

â€¢ ••The Meaning of "Large"••
	• The word "large" can be interpreted as quantity (length) or quality (importance)
	• Paul means that he wrote the longest epistle with his own hand to any church

â€¢ ••Paul's Care for the Churches••
	• Paul shows great care for the churches, both when present and absent
	• He is fearful of false teachers and wants to win them to the gospel

â€¢ ••The Importance of Paul's Letter••

	• The Galatians should be diligent in reading and observing the letter

â€¢ ••A Precedent for Pastors••

â€¢ ••Not Censuring the Minister••
	• If a minister goes beyond what is intended or expected, they should not be censured
	• The Galatians should not have found fault with Paul's large letter

â€¢ ••The Importance of Patience••
	• Hearers should be patient and not prescribe limits to their teachers

â€¢ ••Paul's Instrumental Cause••
	• Paul wrote the epistle with his own hand, which is unusual for him
	• This shows his great care and diligence for the Galatians

â€¢ ••The Opinion of the Doctors••
	• Some doctors believe that Paul did not write the entire epistle with his own hand
	• They think that only the latter part of the epistle was written by Paul

â€¢ ••The Purpose of Paul's Letter••
	• Paul's letter is a testament to his great care and diligence for the Galatians
	• It shows his willingness to go beyond what is expected to win them to the gospel

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Chrysostom's opinion is disputed••
	• Chrysostom said Paul wrote the whole epistle himself
	• However, this opinion is not widely accepted among ancient scholars
• ••Ancient scholars disagree with Chrysostom's opinion••
	• Ambrose, Chrysostom, Primasius, Theodoret, Theophylact, Oecumenius, and Anselm all disagree with Chrysostom's opinion
	• They argue that Paul wrote the epistle with his own hand
• ••Jerome's commentary is not a reliable source••
	• Jerome's commentary on 2 Thessalonians 3:17 contradicts his own opinion on the matter
	• This suggests that Jerome may not be a reliable source on this topic
• ••The text itself supports the idea that Paul wrote the epistle with his own hand••
	• Paul says "You see how large a letter I have written with mine own hand"
	• This suggests that he is emphasizing the authenticity of the epistle
• ••Paul wrote the epistle with his own hand for several reasons••
	• To prove that he was not a changeling, but the same man he was before
	• To prevent the epistle from being counterfeited and sent in his name
	• To testify his sincere love towards the Galatians
• ••The New Testament books can be categorized into three groups••
	• Some were neither written by an apostle nor subscribed (e.g. Gospel of Mark and Luke)
	• Some were subscribed but not written (e.g. Epistle to the Romans)
	• Some were both written and subscribed (e.g. Epistle to the Galatians and Epistle to Philemon)
• ••Paul subscribed every epistle with his own hand••
	• He says "The salutation of me Paul with mine own hand, which is a sign in every epistle"
	• This is a way of authenticating his epistles and preventing counterfeiting
• ••Paul warns against false teachers and forged letters••
	• He says "They would not be troubled by spirit, nor by word, nor by letter, as though it came from him"
	• He emphasizes the importance of authenticating epistles to prevent deception
• ••The sign of Paul's subscription is a key to authenticity••
	• If an epistle has Paul's subscription, it is likely to be authentic
	• If an epistle lacks Paul's subscription, it may be a forgery
• ••We should be cautious in accepting opinions attributed to the fathers••
	• Some scholars may attribute opinions to the fathers that are not actually supported by their writings
	• We should be careful to verify the accuracy of such attributions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Interpretation of Paul's Epistles••
	• The phrase "Which is a sign in every epistle" refers to a specific phrase in Paul's epistles.
	• The phrase is not just a salutation, but a sign that distinguishes Paul's epistles from counterfeit ones.
• ••Possible Meanings of the Phrase••
	• Some think it refers to the phrase "The salutation of me Paul with mine own hand".
	• However, this phrase is only found in a few of Paul's epistles, not all of them.
• ••Alternative Interpretation••
	• The phrase "The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ" is a possible candidate for the sign in every epistle.
	• This phrase is found in most of Paul's epistles, and is a common way for him to conclude his letters.
• ••Support from Other Commentators••
	• Ambrose, Primasius, and Jerome's commentaries all suggest that the phrase "The grace of our Lord" is the sign that distinguishes Paul's epistles.
	• Chrysostom and Theodoret also support this interpretation.
• ••Haimo's Interpretation••
	• Haimo suggests that the phrase "So I write" refers to the phrase "The grace of our Lord" that follows.
	• However, this interpretation is not universally accepted.
• ••The Sign of Paul's Epistles••
	• The sign of Paul's epistles is not just his name written in the epistle, but also the farewell phrase "The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ be with you all".
	• This phrase is not found in every epistle, but is a common way for Paul to conclude his letters.
• ••Cajetan's Mistake••
	• Cajetan thinks that the entire salutation is a note that distinguishes Paul's epistles.
	• However, this is an incorrect interpretation, as the salutation is not always present in Paul's epistles.
• ••Counterfeit Epistles••
	• The Epistle to the Colossians is not the only one that lacks the full salutation.
	• The Epistles to Timothy and Titus also lack the full salutation.
• ••Paul's Meaning••
	• Paul's meaning is not to tie himself precisely to a certain phrase or syllable.
	• Rather, he is commending his readers to the grace of Christ, sometimes mentioning Christ and sometimes not.
• ••Conclusion••
	• The second part of the conclusion is a recapitulation of the main points.
	• The apostle repeats the key points to drive home the main message.

	• They advance the Pope above all that is called God or worshiped.

	• They compel others to fast, while they feast themselves.

â€¢ ••Inscriptions or titles••
	• These are not part of Scripture written by the apostles, but were added by others
	• The fathers doubted whether Paul was the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews, despite its title bearing his name

â€¢ ••Baronius' opinion••
	• He believes 1 Timothy was written from Macedonia, based on 1 Timothy 1:3
	• He also thinks 2 Timothy was written from Rome, but this is unlikely because Paul was an evangelist, not a bishop or pastor

â€¢ ••Theodoret's opinion••
	• He agrees with Baronius that 1 Timothy was written from Macedonia
	• He also thinks 2 Timothy was written from Rome, but this is unlikely because Paul was an evangelist, not a bishop or pastor

â€¢ ••Claudius Espencaeus' opinion••
	• He does not provide a clear opinion on the location of the epistles
	• He simply passes over the issue in silence

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••Authorship of Hebrews••
	• Some attribute the Epistle to the Hebrews to Barnabas, Luke, or Clement
	• Others, like Oecumenius, simply title it "The Epistle to the Hebrews"

â€¢ ••Titles of other epistles••
	• Some epistles (James, Peter, John, Jude) have titles that are not original
	• These titles are "canonical" or "catholic", but were not given by the apostles

â€¢ ••Meaning of "canonical"••
	• The title "canonical" implies a more perfect and absolute rule of doctrine and manners
	• However, some divines have rejected these epistles as not part of canonical Scripture

â€¢ ••Rejection of certain epistles••
	• The epistles of James, 2 Peter, 2 John, 3 John, and Jude were doubted in ancient times
	• The Syrian church does not receive them as part of their canon
	• Some Protestant churches, like the Lutherans, also reject them

â€¢ ••Reason for "canonical" title••
	• No reason can be given why these seven epistles should be called "canonical"
	• They were not given this title by the Greek church, but by the Latin church

â€¢ ••Greek church's view••
	• The Greek church did not give these epistles the title "canonical"
	• They were only given this title by the Latin church

â€¢ ••Excuse for "canonical" title••
	• The title "canonical" may be excused as a way to distinguish these epistles from others
	• It is not meant to imply a higher level of holiness or authority

â€¢ ••Jewish division of Scripture••
	• The Jews divided their Scripture into four parts: Law, Former Prophets, Later Prophets, and Kethubim (Scriptures)
	• These divisions were not meant to imply a higher level of holiness or authority

â€¢ ••Greek term for Scripture••
	• The Greek term "giagrÂ£fa" means "Holy Writ"
	• It does not imply a higher level of holiness or authority

â€¢ ••Distinction between epistles••
	• The title "canonical" may be given to these epistles as a way to distinguish them from others
	• It should not be understood as implying a higher level of holiness or authority.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

â€¢ ••The term "catholic" is not suitable for the seven epistles of James, Peter, and John••
	• The epistles were not written to the entire Catholic Church, but rather to specific groups of people (e.g. the 12 tribes of Israel, the Jews in various regions)
	• The term "catholic" implies a universal scope, but the epistles were not written to the entire world

â€¢ ••The epistles were not written to the Gentiles••
	• James' epistle was written to the 12 tribes of Israel (James 1:1)
	• Peter's epistle was written to the Jews in various regions (1 Peter 1:1)

â€¢ ••The term "catholic" is not unique to the seven epistles••
	• Other epistles, such as those to Timothy, Titus, and Philemon, could also be called "catholic" in the same sense

â€¢ ••The epistle to the Hebrews could also be called "catholic"••
	• It was written to all the Jews, and only to the Jews

â€¢ ••The seven epistles do not contain unique or universal doctrine••
	• All of Paul's epistles contain universal doctrine (Rom. 15:4)

â€¢ ••The word "catholic" is not ancient••
	• Pacianus, an ancient father, says that the term was not used in the apostles' days

â€¢ ••Pacianus' testimony is ignored by some scholars••
	• Baronius dissembles Pacianus' testimony, claiming that the term "catholic" is as ancient as the apostles

â€¢ ••The Syriac translation contains errors••
	• The translation makes only three epistles of James, Peter, and John, omitting others
	• The translation also contains a second error, affirming that James was present at the transfiguration of Christ

â€¢ ••The Syriac church does not receive the disputed epistles into their canon••
	• The church does not recognize the Second of Peter, the Second and Third of John, and the epistle of Jude as part of Holy Scripture

â€¢ ••The errors in the title of the Syriac translation are significant••
	• The title contains two foul errors, which undermine the credibility of the translation.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Authorship of the Epistle••
	• The author of the epistle is James, the son of Alphaeus.
	• This James is different from James, the son of Zebedee, who was a brother of John.
• ••Death of James, Son of Zebedee••
	• James, the son of Zebedee, was killed by King Herod.
	• This event occurred before the destruction of Jerusalem and the dispersion of the twelve tribes.
• ••Two Jameses in the New Testament••
	• There are only two Jameses mentioned in the New Testament.
	• One is James, the son of Zebedee, also known as James the Greater.
	• The other is James, the son of Alphaeus, also known as James the Lesser.
• ••Relationship of James, Son of Alphaeus••
	• James, the son of Alphaeus, is the brother of Jude.
	• He is also the brother of our Lord, Jesus Christ.
• ••Scriptural Evidence••
	• The author cites various passages from the New Testament to support his argument.
	• These passages include Matthew 17:1-2 and Acts 12:1-2.
• ••Pacianus and the Catholic Church••
	• The author mentions Pacianus, a bishop of Barcelona, and his views on the Catholic Church.
	• Pacianus is cited as an authority on the subject of the Catholic Church.
• ••Scripture Index••
	• The author provides a scripture index, listing various passages from the Old Testament.
	• The index includes references to Genesis, Exodus, and other books of the Old Testament.
• ••Editorial Notes••
	• The author includes editorial notes and references to other works.
	• These notes provide additional context and information for the reader.
• ••Gratitude to Jesus••
	• The author expresses gratitude to Jesus, using the phrase "Gratias Tibi Domine Jesu".
	• This phrase is a Latin expression of thanks to Jesus.
• ••Historical Context••
	• The author provides historical context for the epistle, including the destruction of Jerusalem and the dispersion of the twelve tribes.
	• This context helps to understand the significance of the epistle and its author.

	• The book is divided into 13 chapters, with a clear structure and purpose.
	• The chapters are grouped into three main sections: thanksgiving and introduction (chapters 1-2), the ministry of reconciliation (chapters 3-5), and the collection for the Jerusalem church (chapters 6-9).
• ••Key Themes••
	• The book emphasizes the importance of the ministry of reconciliation, which is the central theme of the letter.
	• Other key themes include the power of the Spirit, the sufficiency of Christ, and the importance of giving and generosity.
• ••References to Other Books••
	• The book contains references to other books of the Bible, including the Old Testament and other New Testament letters.
	• These references demonstrate the author's knowledge of the broader biblical context and his use of scripture to support his arguments.
• ••Paul's Ministry••
	• The book provides insight into Paul's ministry and his relationships with the Corinthian church.
	• Paul's ministry is characterized by his emphasis on the power of the Spirit, his reliance on God's strength, and his commitment to the gospel.
• ••The Ministry of Reconciliation••
	• The ministry of reconciliation is the central theme of the book, emphasizing the importance of God's work of reconciliation through Christ.
	• This ministry is characterized by its power, sufficiency, and glory.
• ••The Collection for Jerusalem••
	• The book contains a section on the collection for the Jerusalem church, which was a major concern for Paul and the Corinthian church.
	• The collection was a way of demonstrating the unity of the church and supporting the poor in Jerusalem.
• ••Paul's Relationship with the Corinthians••
	• The book provides insight into Paul's relationship with the Corinthian church, which was complex and multifaceted.
	• Paul's relationship with the Corinthians was characterized by his love, concern, and commitment to their spiritual well-being.
• ••Theological Themes••
	• The book contains a range of theological themes, including the nature of God, the person of Christ, and the work of the Spirit.
	• These themes are developed throughout the book, emphasizing the importance of the gospel and the ministry of reconciliation.
• ••Conclusion••
	• The book concludes with a final section on the importance of the ministry of reconciliation and the power of the Spirit.
	• The conclusion emphasizes the significance of the gospel and the importance of living out the Christian life in accordance with the principles of the ministry of reconciliation.

	• The Nature of Humanity: discussed in relation to its significance in the life of believers (e.g. as a source of guidance and instruction)
• ••Miscellaneous••
	• Various other topics are discussed in the text, including the role of the Holy Spirit, the nature of faith, and the significance of various biblical figures and events.

â€¢ ••Christian Liberty••
	• Christians have the freedom to live according to God's will (318)
	• This liberty should be used to serve others, not to indulge in sin (349)

â€¢ ••Faith and Repentance••
	• Faith is the foundation of a Christian's life (140-142)
	• Repentance is a necessary part of the Christian life (129)

â€¢ ••God's Law and Human Laws••
	• God's law is superior to human laws (81)
	• Christians should obey God's law, not human laws that contradict it (81)

â€¢ ••Love and Charity••
	• Love for God, neighbor, and brethren is a fundamental aspect of Christianity (131, 138, 429-430)
	• Love should be demonstrated through actions, not just words (349)

â€¢ ••Mary and the Church••
	• Mary is revered as the mother of God (64)
	• The church is the body of Christ, and its members should demonstrate love and charity towards one another (289)

â€¢ ••Ministry and Ministry of Death••
	• The ministry of death refers to the old covenant, which was fulfilled by Christ (304)
	• The ministry of the Spirit refers to the new covenant, which is based on faith in Christ (152, 194, 196)

â€¢ ••Morality and Ethics••
	• The moral law continues to be relevant for Christians (206)
	• Christians should strive to live a life of morality and ethics, guided by the principles of the Bible (174-175, 202)

â€¢ ••Spiritual Growth and Development••
	• Christians should strive for spiritual growth and development (393-394)
	• This involves living a life of faith, hope, and love, and demonstrating the fruits of the Spirit (384, 386)

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• ••Criticisms of the Catholic Church••
	• Claims it cannot err (304)
	• Denies certainty of salvation (260)
	• Departed from the grace of God (29-30)
	• Depraved the gospel (87)
• ••Doctrinal Differences••
	• Denies double justification (124, 126, 162, 193, 303, 332)
	• Rejects ex opere operato (225)
	• Believes in justification by faith alone (111, 116, 117, 121, 167, 325)
• ••Traditions and Practices••
	• Criticizes the monastic system (293)
	• Denounces the worship of images (267, 532)
	• Rejects the use of indulgences (188-89)
• ••Scripture and Interpretation••
	• Believes in the authority of Scripture (307)
	• Interprets Scripture literally (301-2)
	• Sees Scripture as the voice of Christ (308)
• ••Soteriology and Sanctification••
	• Believes in justification by faith alone (140)
	• Sees sanctification as a result of faith (73, 334, 348)
	• Denounces the idea of a second justification (116, 126, 162, 177, 334)
• ••Morality and Ethics••
	• Criticizes the Catholic Church's teachings on marriage (366)
	• Denounces the idea of merits of man's works (143, 273, 290, 325)
	• Believes in the importance of self-examination (196, 233, 261)
• ••Theology and Doctrine••
	• Denounces the idea of a "servant of all" (349)
	• Believes in the importance of service to God (269)
	• Sees the Bible as sufficient for salvation (33)
• ••History and Church History••
	• Mentions the writings of schoolmen (36, 468)
	• References the rule of Paul (557, 559-60)
	• Criticizes the Catholic Church's use of titles (531)
• ••Practices and Rituals••
	• Denounces the use of sacraments as a means of salvation (94)
	• Criticizes the Catholic Church's teachings on the invocation of saints (263, 370)
	• Believes in the importance of prayer (267)
• ••Theology and Doctrine (continued)••
	• Denounces the idea of a "second death" (168, 180, 181)
	• Believes in the importance of the second grace (22, 57, 364-65, 565)
	• Sees the Bible as the Word of God (39, 152, 259, 261)

	• Scripture is the ultimate authority for believers (260)

	• True religion is characterized by love, joy, and peace (19, 143, 153, 165-166)

	• Usury and vagabondage are sinful behaviors (350)
• ••Suffering and Trials••
	• Suffering can be a mark of adoption (156)

	• Weakness can be a source of strength for believers (282)
• ••Spiritual Growth••

• ••Miscellaneous••
	• Time is in God's hand (245)
	• The Trinity is a fundamental doctrine of the Christian faith (14, 26n25, 246, 259)
	• The use of holy means, such as the Word and sacraments, is essential for spiritual growth (214-215)
